Hereditary extriangulated categories: Silting objects, mutation, negative extensions
Mikhail Gorsky, Hiroyuki Nakaoka, Yann Palu

Abstract
. In this article, we initiate the study of hereditary extriangulated categories. Many important categories arising in representation theory in connection with various theories of mutation are hereditary extriangulated. Special cases include homotopy categories of 2-term complexes with projective components, which are related to silting mutation, cluster categories (with relevant relative extriangulated structures) where cluster tilting mutation take place, and more general triangulated categories with relative structures connected with relative tilting theory. We prove that there is a theory of irreducible mutation for maximal rigid objects and subcategories in hereditary extriangulated categories of dominant dimension 1, which we shall call 0-Auslander. We show that many notions of maximality for rigid subcategories become equivalent in 0-Auslander extriangulated categories. Applied to the examples above, this recovers 2-term silting mutation in triangulated categories, cluster tilting mutation, and relative rigid mutation. By constructing suitable extriangulated categories, we also recover τ \tau -tilting mutation for gentle algebras and flips for their non-kissing facets. Combined with results by Adachi–Tsukamoto and Pauksztello–Zvonareva, our mutation also provides mutation for intermediate co- t t -structures, extending the known construction of mutation of bounded intermediate co- t t -structures. In spirit of our earlier work, we study negative extensions in hereditary extriangulated categories. We give sufficient conditions for the existence of universal balanced negative extensions. We explicitly compute certain, a priori non-universal, versions of negative extensions in 0-Auslander categories constructed from triangulated categories with rigid subcategories. We discuss examples where these two constructions give the same δ \delta -functors and where they disagree. In both constructions, the δ \delta -functors are concentrated in degrees [ − 2 , 1 ] . [-2,1].



1.
Introduction

Flips of triangulations are the combinatorial shadows of
mutations
in cluster algebras.
In order to categorify cluster algebras and the key notion of mutation, Aslak Buan, Bethany Marsh, Markus Reineke, Idun Reiten and Gordana Todorov introduced cluster tilting theory in
[
BMR+06
]
.
They proved that in a cluster category some specific objects categorifying clusters (the so-called
cluster tilting objects
) have a well behaved theory of mutation.
Mutation of cluster tilting objects was quickly adapted or generalized to various settings: to cluster tilting in some abelian or exact categories defined from preprojective algebras
[
GLS08
]
, to
\tau
-tilting in module categories of finite-dimensional algebras
[
AIR14
]
, to 2-term silting in homotopy categories of complexes with projective components over a finite-dimensional algebra
[
AI12
,
Kim20
]
, to relative cluster tilting theory in triangulated categories
[
YZ19
,
YZZ19
]
…

The flip of triangulations can also be generalized to a flip for dissections (or partial triangulations), that turned out to be the flip of so-called
non-kissing facets

[
McC17
,
BDM+20
,
PPP21
]
in a different guise
[
PPP19
]
.

Our aim in this paper is to advertise the ubiquity of hereditary extriangulated categories in this context. We develop a theory of mutation for silting objects in certain hereditary extriangulated categories that encompasses all mutations mentioned above, while permitting elementary proofs.

The setting we work in is that of
0-Auslander extriangulated categories
, whose prototypical examples are given by homotopy categories of two-term complexes of projectives.

Definition 1.1
.

An extriangulated category
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
is 0-Auslander if

•

it has enough projectives;

•

it is hereditary:
\forall X\in\mathscr{C}
,
\exists

P_{1}\rightarrowtail P_{0}\twoheadrightarrow X\dashrightarrow
where
P_{0},P_{1}
are projective;

•

it has dominant dimension at least 1: for any projective object
P
, there is a conflation
P\rightarrowtail Q\twoheadrightarrow I
where
I
is injective and
Q
is projective-injective.

It is reduced if moreover its only projective-injective object is 0 (up to isomorphism).

This definition turns out to be self-dual: one can ask for the existence of enough injectives, injective dimension to be at most 1, and codominant dimension to be at least 1. Such categories can be thought of as extriangulated analogues of Auslander-regular rings of global dimension at most 1.
1
1

1

We thank Osamu Iyama for this comment. Our choice of the name “0-Auslander” instead of “1-Auslander” is motivated by the notation in higher dimensional Auslander-Reiten theory. In particular, in
[
IJ17
]
, certain additive categories
{\mathcal{A}}
are called
d-Auslander dualizing R-varieties
, for a positive integer
d
, if they satisfy
\operatorname{gl.dim}{\mathcal{A}}\leq d+1\leq\operatorname{dom.dim}{\mathcal{A}}
.

In 0-Auslander categories, several candidate definitions of tilting objects turn out to be equivalent.

Proposition 1.2

(particular case of
Theorem

4.3
)
.

Let
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
be a Krull–Schmidt, Hom-finite, reduced 0-Auslander extriangulated category with
P=P_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus P_{n}
a basic projective generator, and let
R=R_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus R_{k}
be a basic rigid object (i.e.
\mathbb{E}(R,R)=0
).
Then
k\leq n
and the following are equivalent:

(a)

R
is maximal rigid: If
R\oplus X
is rigid, then
X\in\operatorname{add}R
;

(b)

R
is silting: The smallest thick (that is, strictly full, closed under direct summands, and satisfying the 2-out-of-3 property for
\mathfrak{s}
-triangles) subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
containing
R
is
\mathscr{C}
itself.

(c)

R
is tilting: There is an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
P\rightarrowtail R^{0}\twoheadrightarrow R^{1}\dashrightarrow
with
R^{0},R^{1}\in\operatorname{add}R
;

(d)

R
is cotilting: There is an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
R^{1}\rightarrowtail R^{0}\twoheadrightarrow I\dashrightarrow
where
R^{0},R^{1}\in\operatorname{add}R
and
I
is an injective cogenerator;

(e)

R
is
\mathbb{E}
-tilting: If
\mathbb{E}(R,X)=0=\mathbb{E}(X,R)
then
X\in\operatorname{add}R
;

(f)

R
is complete: We have
k=n
.

Some examples of reduced 0-Auslander extriangulated categories are given by:

•

Cluster categories endowed with a fixed cluster tilting object, and the maximal relative extriangulated structure making it projective. In this case, the silting objects are precisely the cluster tilting objects.

•

The homotopy category of complexes with projective components and concentrated in degrees -1 and 0 over a finite-dimensional algebra. In this case, the silting objects are the 2-term silting objects.

•

Certain well-chosen subcategories of triangulated categories with a fixed rigid object and endowed with a suitable extriangulated structure. In this case, the silting objects are the relative tilting objects.

•

Certain exact categories constructed from gentle algebras. In this case, the silting objects categorify accordion dissections, or non-kissing facets.

•

The category of finite-dimensional representations of a Dynkin quiver of type
A
, endowed with a certain exact structure introduced by Thomas Brüstle, Eric Hanson, Sunny Roy and Ralf Schiffler. In this case, the silting objects are the maximal almost rigid modules of
[
BGMS23
]
.

Our next main result shows that there is a well-behaved theory of irreducible mutation for silting subcategories (only stated here for silting objects for simplicity)
in 0-Auslander extriangulated categories, thus generalizing and unifying the mutation theories or flips in all four examples above.

Theorem 1.3

(particular case of
Theorem

4.26
)
.

Let
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
be a Hom-finite, Krull–Schmidt, reduced 0-Auslander extriangulated category with a projective generator, and let
R
be a basic silting object.
Write
R=R^{\prime}\oplus X
with
X
indecomposable.
Then, there is (up to isomorphism) a unique indecomposable object
Y
, non-isomorphic to
X
, such that
R^{\prime}\oplus Y
is silting.
Moreover, there is precisely one exchange
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle

X\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}E\overset{p}{\twoheadrightarrow}Y\dashrightarrow\hskip 7.0pt\text{ or }\hskip 10.0ptY\overset{i^{\prime}}{\rightarrowtail}E^{\prime}\overset{p^{\prime}}{\twoheadrightarrow}X\dashrightarrow,

where
E,E^{\prime}
belong to
\operatorname{add}R^{\prime}
,
i,i^{\prime}
are left
R^{\prime}
-approximations and
p,p^{\prime}
are right
R^{\prime}
-approximations.

We note that
Theorem

1.3
also applies to non-reduced 0-Auslander extriangulated categories, but there, projective-injective summands cannot be mutated (and thus behave like coefficients in cluster algebras). Note also that the last sentence of
Theorem

1.3
means that in 0-Auslander categories the distinction between left and right mutations is very transparent.

In order to prove
Propositions

1.2
and
1.3
, we are led to studying Bongartz completions, indices and coindices (this permits us to define categorical
c
-vectors as in
[
JY20
]
) and some form of Iyama–Yoshino reduction
[
IY08
]
.

We show that extended cohearts of co-
t
-structures
[
Pau08
]
(also known as weight structures
[
Bon10
]
) on triangulated categories, as considered by
[
PZ20
]
, are reduced 0-Auslander extriangulated categories.
By combining results of Adachi–Tsukamoto
[
AT22
]
relating tilting subcategories with complete cotorsion pairs with one of the main theorems of Pauksztello–Zvonareva
[
PZ20
]
, we then deduce from
Proposition

1.2
a theory of irreducible mutations of intermediate co-
t
-structures. This recovers the previously known construction of irreducible mutations of intermediate bounded co-
t
-structures
[
AI12
,
KY14
,
BY13
]
and commutes with restrictions to and extensions from the thick subcategory generated by the coheart of the original co-
t
-structures.

The remainder of the paper
concerns negative extensions in hereditary extriangulated categories. Long exact sequences of homomorphisms and positive extensions in extriangulated categories a priori do not continue naturally in the negative direction. In
[
GNP21
]
we suggested a definition of negative extensions in extriangulated categories (see also
[
AET21
]
): we defined covariant and contravariant versions of negative extensions in extriangulated categories satisfying certain natural conditions and proved their respective functoriality and universality. The universality means that these bifunctors are covariant and contravariant left derived functors of the functor
\mathrm{Hom}
.

In this paper, we study bivariant, or
balanced
, versions of negative extensions in hereditary extriangulated categories. In
[
GNP21
]
we gave sufficient conditions on covariant and contravariant negative extensions to agree. In this case, they give a bivariant
\delta
-functor satisfying natural universal properties in both arguments. These conditions are satisfied for exact categories and for the homotopy category of 2-term complexes with projective components over a finite-dimensional algebra. The requirement on the covariant and contravariant versions to agree is nonetheless very restricitve: the cluster category of type
A_{2}
with the maximal relative extriangulated structure making some cluster tilting object projective is a basic example of a 0-Auslander category where they disagree.

We give a fairly natural construction of a bivariant connected sequence of bifunctors
(\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp},\delta^{\sharp})
, with
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{i}=\mathbb{E}^{i}
for
i\geq 0
and
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-i}=0
for
i\geq 3
, for essentially small hereditary extriangulated categories having enough projectives and enough injectives.

Theorem 1.4

(see
Theorem

7.24
for more details)
.

The bivariant connected sequence of functors
(\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp},\delta^{\sharp})
is a bivariant
\delta
-functor if and only if a certain 4-term complex is exact at the middle terms. In that case,
(\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp},\delta^{\sharp})
satisfies the relevant universal property. Its components
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-n}
can thus be seen as universal balanced negative extensions.

For a reduced 0-Auslander category arising from a rigid subcategory of a triangulated category, we define another bivariant
\delta-
functor
(\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp},\delta^{\sharp})
(see
Definition

7.33
), a priori depending on the ambient triangulated category. We expect that whenever such a category admits universal balanced negative extensions, these are given by
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{\bullet}
, and so
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{\bullet}
is the correct replacement of
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}
in this setting. These two constructions agree for cluster categories with relative structures corresponding to acyclic or self-injective cluster tilting objects, but differ in general. The simplest example when they differ comes from a non-acyclic cluster seed of type
A_{4}
.

Further directions.

(i)

In work in progress of M.G. and Y.P. with Xin Fang, Pierre-Guy Plamondon, and Matthew Pressland, certain further properties and examples of 0-Auslander categories are studied. In particular, Higgs categories coming from ice quivers with potential, as recently defined by Yilin Wu
[
Wu21
]
, are shown to be 0-Auslander when endowed with suitable relative extriangulated structures.

(ii)

The definition of
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-1}(-,-)
is quite nontrivial: it is given by the quotient of certain morphism space in the ambient triangulated category by an ideal of morphisms factoring through morphisms between objects in certain subcategories (see
Definition

7.31
). The fact that it makes sense in this context to consider quotients by morphisms factoring through certain
morphisms
rather than through certain
objects
seems fairly unexpected. A conjectural explanation, of sorts, will be given in the same work in progress as in (i). We also wonder if this definition can be deduced or explained naturally for extriangulated categories admitting enhancements, either via exact
\infty
-categories and their stable hulls
[
Bar15
,
NP20
,
Kle22
]
, or via differential graded categories as in the ongoing Ph.D. project of Xiaofa Chen.

(iii)

It is natural to ask which of the results of the present paper hold, up to suitable modifications, for extriangulated categories of higher global and (co)dominant dimension, which could be called d-Auslander extriangulated.
Similarly, one can also consider the following generalisation.
Extriangulated categories are a special case of
n
-exangulated categories
introduced by Herschend, Liu, and H.N.
[
HLN21
]
: they correspond to
n
being equal to
1
. Projective and injective objects in
n
-exangulated categories are defined for general
n
, and one can adapt
Definition

1.1
to this generality. Some implications in
Proposition

1.2
and parts of
Theorem

1.3
extend to the framework of extriangulated categories of higher global and (co)dominant dimension, and some generalise to
n
-exangulated analogues of 0-Auslander extriangulated categories. Both of these generalisations will be treated in work in progress
joint with Nicholas Williams.

Organisation of the paper.

The article is organised as follows:
Reminders on extriangulated categories are gathered in
Appendix

A
.
In
Section

2
, we discuss equivalent definitions of hereditary extriangulated categories and behaviour of those under natural operations. In
Section

3
, we introduce 0-Auslander extriangulated categories and prove some of their basic properties. We list many examples of such categories in
Section

3.3
.

Section

4
, forming the heart of the paper, is devoted to studying tilting subcategories in 0-Auslander extriangulated categories.
We prove that various possible notions of maximality for rigid subcategories are in fact equivalent in
Section

4.1
.
For a better understanding of complete rigid objects (
Section

4.4
), we prove a version of Bongartz completion in
Section

4.2
and define indices, coindices and categorical
c
-vectors in
Section

4.3
.

Sections

2.2.3
and
4.5
introduce reduction for hereditary extriangulated categories, which is a crucial tool in
Section

4.6
for proving
Theorem

1.3
.
In
Section

5
, we apply our results on mutation of tilting subcategories in 0-Auslander extriangulated categories to extended cohearts of co-
t
-structures in triangulated categories. We explain why this recovers mutation for intermediate co-
t
-structures.
In
Section

6
, we construct, starting from any finite-dimensional gentle algebra, a 0-Auslander extriangulated category of walks.
This allows us to prove that flips of non-kissing facets in the sense of McConville
[
McC17
]
are instances of mutations of tilting objects in 0-Auslander extriangulated categories.

Section

7
is concerned with negative extensions in hereditary extriangulated categories. We give explicit computations in a large class of examples (
Section

7.4
), construct a candidate bivariant connected sequence of functors for any hereditary extriangulated category, and find a necessary and sufficient condition so that it is acyclic and universal in
Section

7.3
.

2.
Hereditary extriangulated categories

In this section, we discuss several equivalent definitions and some properties of extriangulated categories of (positive) global dimension 1. Basics on extriangulated categories are recalled in
Appendix

A
. We assume all extriangulated categories to be essentially small and linear over a commutative ring
R
with
1
.
Recall from
Appendix

A
the notion of cone and that of cocone (or fiber).

2.1.
Equivalent definitions of hereditary extriangulated categories

In
[
GNP21
, Section 3]
, we defined higher positive extensions
\mathbb{E}^{i},i\geq 1
via a version of Yoneda’s theory. Let
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
be an extriangulated category,
C,A\in\mathscr{C}
. The underlying set of the
R
-module
\mathbb{E}^{2}(C,A)
admits the following explicit description first sketched in
[
NP19
, Remark 5.10]
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{2}(C,A)

\displaystyle=\Big(\coprod_{M\in\mathscr{C}}\Big(\mathbb{E}(M,A)\times\mathbb{E}(C,M)\Big)\Big)/\sim,

where the equivalence relation
\sim
is generated by

(\kappa,f_{\ast}(\lambda))\sim(f^{\ast}(\kappa),\lambda),

for arbitrary
f\in\mathscr{C}(M,N),\kappa\in\mathbb{E}(N,A),\lambda\in\mathbb{E}(C,M)
. An element of
\mathbb{E}^{2}(C,A)
is
0
if and only if it is equivalent to a pair
(0,\lambda),
if and only if it is equivalent to a pair
(\kappa,0).

For any
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
A\overset{x}{\longrightarrow}B\overset{y}{\longrightarrow}C\overset{\delta}{\dashrightarrow}
, we have a pair of long exact sequences
[
GNP21
, Theorem 3.5]
:

(2.1)

\mathscr{C}(-,A)\to\mathscr{C}(-,B)\to\mathscr{C}(-,C)\to\mathbb{E}(-,A)\to\mathbb{E}(-,B)\to\mathbb{E}(-,C)\to\mathbb{E}^{2}(-,A)\to\cdots

in
\mathrm{Mod}\mathscr{C}
, and

(2.2)

\mathscr{C}(C,-)\to\mathscr{C}(B,-)\to\mathscr{C}(A,-)\to\mathbb{E}(C,-)\to\mathbb{E}(B,-)\to\mathbb{E}(A,-)\to\mathbb{E}^{2}(C,-)\to\cdots

in
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{Mod}
.

If
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
has enough projectives and enough injectives, another construction of the same higher extension groups had been introduced earlier in
[
LN19
]
,
[
HLN22
]
by using (co)resolutions.

Proposition 2.1
.

(1)

The following conditions on an extriangulated category
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
are equivalent.

(i)

The functor
\mathbb{E}(X,-)
is right exact for any
X\in\mathscr{C};

(ii)

The functor
\mathbb{E}(-,Y)
is right exact for any
Y\in\mathscr{C};

(iii)

The bifunctor
\mathbb{E}^{2}(-,-)
is zero;

(iv)

Let
\delta\in\mathbb{E}(Z,Y)
and
\delta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{E}(X,Z)
be realized respectively by

Y\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}U\overset{f^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}Z\ \text{ and }\ Z\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}T\overset{e}{\longrightarrow}X.

Then there exist an object
V\in\mathscr{C}
, a commutative diagram in
\mathscr{C}

(2.3)

\displaystyle\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 58.22858pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern-58.22858pt\raise 17.07156pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 17.07156pt\raise-65.441pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.8978pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\delta^{\prime}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 17.07156pt\raise-79.6673pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\raise-1.70752pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}}}}

with
\delta^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{E}(T,Y)
realized by
Y\overset{h}{\longrightarrow}V\overset{h^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}T
and

\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{E}(X,U)
realized by
U\overset{g}{\longrightarrow}V\overset{g^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}X
,
which satisfy the following compatibilities.

(i)

f^{\prime}_{\ast}\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}=\delta^{\prime}
,

(ii)

d^{\ast}\delta^{\prime\prime}=\delta
,

(iii)

f_{\ast}\delta^{\prime\prime}=e^{\ast}\delta^{\prime\prime\prime}
.

(v)

Let
f
be a composition
f=i\circ d,
where
i:Z\to T
is an inflation with cone
X,

d:U\to Z
is a deflation with cocone
Y
. Then
f
admits a decomposition as
f=d^{\prime}\circ i^{\prime},
where
d^{\prime}:V\to T
is a deflation with cone
X,

i^{\prime}:U\to V
is an inflation with cocone
Y
, and, moreover,
U\overset{\tiny{\begin{bmatrix}-d\\
i^{\prime}\end{bmatrix}}}{\rightarrowtail}Z\oplus V\overset{\tiny{\begin{bmatrix}i&d^{\prime}\end{bmatrix}}}{\twoheadrightarrow}T
is a conflation.

(2)

If
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
has enough projectives, then these conditions are satisfied if and only if any object admits a projective resolution of length 1: For each
X\in\mathscr{C}
, there is a conflation
P_{1}\rightarrowtail P_{0}\twoheadrightarrow X
, with
P_{0},P_{1}
projective.

(3)

Dually, if
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
has enough injectives, then these conditions are satisfied if and only if any object admits a injective coresolution of length 1: For each
X\in\mathscr{C}
, there is a conflation
X\rightarrowtail I^{0}\twoheadrightarrow I^{1}
, with
I^{0},I^{1}
injective.

Proof.

The implications (iii)
\Rightarrow
(i), (iii)
\Rightarrow
(ii) follow from the long exact sequences (
2.1
) and (
2.2
).

The implications (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii) and (i)
\Rightarrow
(iv) follow from the axiom (ET4) as follows. Let
A\overset{x}{\longrightarrow}B\overset{y}{\longrightarrow}C\overset{\delta}{\dashrightarrow}
be an arbitrary
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle. Consider an arbitrary
X\in\mathscr{C}
and an arbitrary
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle of the form
X\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}Y\overset{g}{\longrightarrow}A\overset{\theta}{\dashrightarrow}
.
For (ii), it is enough to show that for each such data, there exists
\nu\in\mathbb{E}(B,X)
such that
x^{\ast}\nu=\theta
.
By the assumption (i), there exists
\tau\in\mathbb{E}(C,Y)
such that
g_{\ast}\tau=\delta
. Consider any realization of
\tau
, it has the form
Y\overset{t}{\longrightarrow}Z\overset{u}{\longrightarrow}C\overset{\tau}{\dashrightarrow}
. By (ET4), there exists a diagram of the form

\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{Y}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{Z}
\textstyle{B^{\prime}}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{C}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{t}
\scriptstyle{x^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{u}
\scriptstyle{y^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\scriptstyle{\mu}
\scriptstyle{\tau}
\scriptstyle{g_{\ast}\tau}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

such that
(x^{\prime})^{\ast}\mu=\theta
. Since
g_{\ast}\tau=\delta,
there exists
b\in\mathscr{C}(B,B^{\prime})
making the diagram

\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{B^{\prime}}
\textstyle{C}
\scriptstyle{x}
\scriptstyle{y}
\scriptstyle{b}
\scriptstyle{x^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{y^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

commute. Then
b^{\ast}\mu\in\mathbb{E}(B,X)
satisfies
x^{\ast}(b^{\ast}\mu)=(x^{\prime})^{\ast}(\mu)=\theta,
hence we can take
\nu=b^{\ast}\mu.

Moreover, the isomorphism
b
allows to replace
B^{\prime}
by
B
in the first diagram above, thus giving (iv).

The implications (ii)
\Rightarrow
(i) and (ii)
\Rightarrow
(iv) are proved dually by using (ET4)
op
.

(iv)
\Rightarrow
(iii): Each element in
\mathbb{E}^{2}(X,Y)
can be represented by a pair
(\delta,\delta^{\prime})
which can in turn be realized by a pair of
\mathfrak{s}
-triangles

Y\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}U\overset{f^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}Z\ \text{ and }\ Z\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}T\overset{e}{\longrightarrow}X.

By the assumption, they can be completed to a diagram of the form (
2.3
). Then the class
(\delta,\delta^{\prime})=(\delta,f^{\prime}_{\ast}\delta^{\prime\prime\prime})
is equivalent to
(f^{\prime\ast}\delta,\delta^{\prime})
. But
f^{\prime\ast}\delta=0
by
[
NP19
, Lemma 3.2]
, hence
(\delta,\delta^{\prime})\sim(0,\delta^{\prime})=0.

(iv)
\Leftrightarrow
(v) follows from
[
LN19
, Proposition 1.20]
by using the diagram (
2.3
).

Parts (2) and (3) are immediate consequences of
[
GNP21
, Corollary 3.21]
and constructions in
[
HLN22
, Section 3.1]
and
[
LN19
, Section 5.1]
.
∎

Remark 2.2
.

By
[
GNP21
, Proposition 3.17]
, conditions in
Proposition

2.1
.(1) are also equivalent to the following: for all
X,Y\in\mathscr{C},
every
\alpha\in\mathbb{E}^{2}(X,Y)
admits a trivialization. In fact, condition (iv) is essentially a more explicit rephrasing of this condition.

Definition 2.3
.

An extriangulated category is called
hereditary
if it satisfies the equivalent conditions of
Proposition

2.1
.

2.2.
Operations on hereditary extriangulated categories

2.2.1.
Extension-closed subcategories

Lemma 2.4
.

An extension-closed subcategory of a hereditary extriangulated category, considered with the induced extriangulated structure, is hereditary.

Proof.

It is straightforward to check that if the condition (iv) in
Proposition

2.1
.(1) holds for an extriangulated category, it holds for each of its extension-closed subcategories.
∎

2.2.2.
Relative theories

Given a relative structure
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{F},\mathfrak{s}_{\mathbb{F}})
of an extriangulated structure
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
, one may ask whether the latter being hereditary implies the former being hereditary, or vice versa. The following easy examples explain why neither implication is true already for exact structures. Nontrivial examples of hereditary relative structures of non-hereditary extriangulated structures will appear throughout the paper.

Example 2.5
.

The larger structure is hereditary, the smaller is not.

Consider the category
\operatorname{mod}kA_{3}.
It is hereditary when considered with its abelian exact structure. The relative exact structure whose class of conflations consists of all short exact sequences on which
\mathrm{Hom}(\begin{smallmatrix}3\\
2\end{smallmatrix},-)
becomes exact is no longer hereditary. This is clear from the Auslander-Reiten quiver of the category. The Auslander-Reiten triangles ending at
2
and at
3
give conflations, and the class in
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ext}}^{2}(3,1)
defined by their Yoneda product does not vanish (here we denote by
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ext}}^{i}(-,-)
(higher) extension groups in this structure).

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\begin{smallmatrix}3\\
2\\
1\end{smallmatrix}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\begin{smallmatrix}2\\
1\end{smallmatrix}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\begin{smallmatrix}3\\
2\end{smallmatrix}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 3}

Example 2.6
.

The smaller structure is hereditary, the larger is not.

Every additive category admits a split exact structure, which is by definition hereditary. Thus, any abelian category of global dimension at least 2 provides such an example: its maximal exact structure is the abelian one, its minimal exact structure is the split one.

2.2.3.
Reductions of hereditary extriangulated categories

Definition 2.7
.

Let
\mathcal{R}\subseteq\mathscr{C}
be any rigid full additive subcategory, closed under isomorphisms and under taking direct summands. Put
\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathcal{R}}=\frac{{}^{\perp_{1}}\mathcal{R}\cap\mathcal{R}^{\perp_{1}}}{[\mathcal{R}]}
.
Since all objects in
\mathcal{R}
are projective-injective in
{}^{\perp_{1}}\mathcal{R}\cap\mathcal{R}^{\perp_{1}}
, the bifunctor
\mathbb{E}
, restricted to
{}^{\perp_{1}}\mathcal{R}\cap\mathcal{R}^{\perp_{1}}
, descends to the quotient and we have an induced extriangulated category
(\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathcal{R}},\overline{\mathbb{E}},\overline{\mathfrak{s}})
(see
[
NP19
, Proposition 3.30]
).

Remark 2.8
.

The case when
\mathcal{R}
is the full subcategory whose objects are the projective-injectives in
\mathscr{C}
was considered (for non-necessarily hereditary extriangulated categories) in
[
NP19
, Proposition 3.30]
and will often be used in our proofs.

The following is an immediate consequence of the definitions and of
Lemma

2.4
.

Proposition 2.9
.

The following holds.

(1)

If
\mathscr{C}
is hereditary, then so is
\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathcal{R}}
.

(2)

For any
X,X^{\prime}\in{}^{\perp_{1}}\mathcal{R}\cap\mathcal{R}^{\perp_{1}}
, the following are equivalent.

(i)

\mathbb{E}(\operatorname{add}(\mathcal{R}\oplus X),\operatorname{add}(\mathcal{R}\oplus X^{\prime}))=0
.

(ii)

\mathbb{E}(X,X^{\prime})=0
.

(iii)

\overline{\mathbb{E}}(X,X^{\prime})=0
.

2.2.4.
Localization of hereditary categories

In this subsection, we assume all categories to be small. Let us recall some definitions and results from
[
NOS22
]
.

A full additive subcategory
\mathcal{N}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
is

thick

if it is closed under isomorphisms and direct summands and satisfies the 2-out-of-3 property for conflations. In particular,
\mathcal{N}
is closed under extensions in
\mathcal{C}
, so it carries an induced extriangulated structure.

We associate with a thick
\mathcal{N}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
two sets of morphisms:

\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{N}}=\{f\in\mathcal{M}\;|\;f\mbox{ is an inflation with Cone($f$)}\in\mathcal{N}\};

\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{N}}=\{f\in\mathcal{M}\;|\;f\mbox{ is a deflation with CoCone($f$)}\in\mathcal{N}\}.

\mathscr{S}_{\mathcal{N}}
is defined to be the set of all finite compositions of morphisms in
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{N}}
and
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{N}}
. The following lemma shows that in the hereditary case, this set has an easier description.

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
\mathcal{N}
be a thick subcategory of a hereditary extriangulated category. Then we have

\mathscr{S}_{\mathcal{N}}=\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{N}}\circ\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{N}}.

Proof.

It is enough to show that
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{N}}\circ\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{N}}\circ\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{N}}
. This follows from (v) in
Proposition

2.1
, applied to
i\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{N}},d\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{N}}.

∎

Let
\mathcal{N}\subseteq\mathscr{C}
be a thick subcategory of an extriangulated category such that
\mathscr{S}_{\mathcal{N}}
satisfies the conditions of
[
NOS22
, Theorem 3.5]
(this is the case for example when
\mathcal{N}
is biresolving, or when
\mathscr{C}
is exact or triangulated and
\mathcal{N}
is percolating, see
[
NOS22
, Section 4]
). Then the main result of
[
NOS22
]
states that the localization
\mathscr{C}/\mathcal{N}\overset{\cong}{\to}\mathscr{C}[\mathscr{S}_{\mathcal{N}}^{-1}]
carries a canonical induced extriangulated structure
(\mathscr{C}/\mathcal{N},\widetilde{\mathbb{E}},\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}})
. Its morphisms, defining bifunctor
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}
, and
\mathfrak{s}
-triangles are all described via a suitable calculus of fractions. In particular, each
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle realizing an element in
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}(C,A)
can be represented by a diagram of the form
A\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}X\overset{f}{\rightarrowtail}B\overset{f^{\prime}}{\twoheadrightarrow}Y\overset{c}{\longrightarrow}C
, with
X\overset{f}{\rightarrowtail}B\overset{f^{\prime}}{\twoheadrightarrow}Y
being a conflation in
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
and with
a,c\in\mathscr{S}_{\mathcal{N}}
.

Theorem 2.11
.

Let
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
be a hereditary extriangulated category and let
\mathcal{N}\subseteq\mathscr{C}
be a thick subcategory such that
\mathscr{S}_{\mathcal{N}}
satisfies the conditions of
[
NOS22
, Theorem 3.5]
. Then the induced extriangulated structure
(\mathscr{C}/\mathcal{N},\widetilde{\mathbb{E}},\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}})
is also hereditary.

Proof.

An element in
\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}^{2}(M,N)
can be represented by a pair of
\mathfrak{s}
-triangles in
(\mathscr{C}/\mathcal{N},\widetilde{\mathbb{E}},\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}})
which together form a diagram of the form

N\overset{a}{\longrightarrow}B\overset{b}{\rightarrowtail}C\overset{c}{\twoheadrightarrow}D\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}E\overset{e}{\longrightarrow}F\overset{f}{\rightarrowtail}G\overset{g}{\twoheadrightarrow}H\overset{h}{\longrightarrow}M,

where

B\overset{b}{\rightarrowtail}C\overset{c}{\twoheadrightarrow}D
and
F\overset{f}{\rightarrowtail}G\overset{g}{\twoheadrightarrow}H
are conflations in
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
,
a,d,e,h\in\mathscr{S}_{\mathcal{N}}
. By using
Lemma

2.10
, the composition
D\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}E\overset{e}{\longrightarrow}F
can be rewritten as
D\overset{d^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}E^{\prime}\overset{e^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}F
, with
d^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{N}},e^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{N}}
.

By applying
Proposition

2.1
.(v) three times in
\mathscr{C}
, we construct the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{E^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\wr}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where we abbreviate by
X\overset{\sim}{\to}Y
morphisms belonging to
\mathscr{S}_{\mathcal{N}}
,
thus showing that the category
(\mathscr{C}/\mathcal{N},\widetilde{\mathbb{E}},\widetilde{\mathfrak{s}})
is hereditary.
∎

3.
0-Auslander extriangulated categories

After a short discussion on the notion of dominant dimension (
Section

3.1
), we introduce the notion of a 0-Auslander extriangulated category (
Section

3.2
) and illustrate it with many examples arising from representation theory (
Section

3.3
), the most basic being the homotopy category
K^{[-1,0]}(\operatorname{proj}\Lambda)
of 2-term complexes with projective components over a finite-dimensional algebra and cluster categories with the largest relative extriangulated structure making some cluster tilting object projective.
We fix an extriangulated category
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
.

3.1.
(Co)dominant dimensions

The main result of this subsection is
Proposition

3.6
. We first prove some useful lemmata.

Lemma 3.1

(
[
LN19
]
)
.

Let
Y\rightarrowtail P\twoheadrightarrow X\dashrightarrow
be an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle with
P
projective in
\mathscr{C}
.
Then
\mathbb{E}^{2}(X,-)\cong\mathbb{E}(Y,-)
.

Proof.

This follows from the long exact sequence associated with the
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle by noticing that
\mathbb{E}(P,-)=0
and
\mathbb{E}^{2}(P,-)=0
.
∎

The following two immediate consequences will often be used in the article.

Corollary 3.2
.

Assume that there is an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
P_{1}\rightarrowtail P_{0}\twoheadrightarrow X\dashrightarrow
with
P_{0},P_{1}
projective in
\mathscr{C}
.
Then for any object
Y\in\mathscr{C}
, we have
\mathbb{E}^{2}(X,Y)=0
.

Corollary 3.3
.

Let
P\rightarrowtail I\twoheadrightarrow\Sigma P\dashrightarrow
be an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle with
I
injective.
If
\mathbb{E}^{2}(-,P)=0
, then
\Sigma P
is injective.

Lemma 3.4
.

Assume that, for each projective object
P
, we have
\mathbb{E}^{2}(-,P)=0
and there exists an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
P\rightarrowtail I\twoheadrightarrow\Sigma P\dashrightarrow
with
I
injective. If an object
X\in\mathscr{C}
is the cone of an inflation between projective objects, then it is the fiber of a deflation between injective objects.

Proof.

Let
P_{1}\rightarrowtail P_{0}\twoheadrightarrow X
be a conflation with
P_{0},P_{1}
projectives.
Fix two conflations
P_{1}\rightarrowtail I\twoheadrightarrow\Sigma P_{1}
and
P_{0}\rightarrowtail J\twoheadrightarrow\Sigma P_{0}
with
I,J
injectives.
By successively forming the diagrams:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\oplus I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma P_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma P_{1}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X\oplus I}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma P_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces J}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma P_{1}\oplus J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma P_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma P_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma P_{0}}}

we constuct a conflation

X\oplus I\rightarrowtail\Sigma P_{1}\oplus J\twoheadrightarrow\Sigma P_{0}
,
where
\Sigma P_{0},\Sigma P_{1}
are injective by
Corollary

3.3
.
Because
I
is injective, it splits from the conflation and
X
is the fiber of a deflation between injectives.
∎

Definition 3.5
.

Let
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
be an extriangulated category with enough projectives. We define its
dominant dimension

\operatorname{dom.dim}(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
to be the largest integer
n
such that for any projective object
P
, there exist
n

\mathfrak{s}
-triangles

P\rightarrowtail I_{0}\twoheadrightarrow M_{1}\dashrightarrow;

M_{1}\rightarrowtail I_{1}\twoheadrightarrow M_{2}\dashrightarrow;

\cdots

M_{n-1}\rightarrowtail I_{n-1}\twoheadrightarrow M_{n}\dashrightarrow,

with
I_{k}
being projective-injective for all
0\leq k\leq n-1
. If such an
n
does not exist, we let
\operatorname{dom.dim}(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})=\infty.

Dually, let
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
be an extriangulated category with enough injectives. We define its
codominant dimension

\operatorname{codom.dim}(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
to be the largest integer
n
such that for any injective object
I
, there exist
n

\mathfrak{s}
-triangles

N_{1}\rightarrowtail P_{0}\twoheadrightarrow I\dashrightarrow;

N_{2}\rightarrowtail P_{1}\twoheadrightarrow N_{1}\dashrightarrow;

\cdots

N_{n}\rightarrowtail P_{n-1}\twoheadrightarrow N_{n-1}\dashrightarrow,

with
P_{k}
being projective-injective for all
0\leq k\leq n-1
. If such an
n
does not exist, we let
\operatorname{codom.dim}(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
equal
\infty.

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
be an extriangulated category. The following conditions are equivalent.

(i)

(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
has enough projectives and
\mathrm{pd}(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E})\leq 1\leq\mathrm{dom.dim}(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E}).

(ii)

(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
has enough injectives and
\mathrm{id}(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E})\leq 1\leq\mathrm{codom.dim}(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E}).

Explicitly, the conditions in (i) mean that every object of
\mathscr{C}
is the cone of an inflation between projectives, and that each projective object
P
admits an inflation
P\rightarrowtail Q,
with
Q
being projective-injective (using heredity, it then follows from the long exact sequence that the cone is injective). Dually, the conditions in (ii) mean that every object of
\mathscr{C}
is the cocone of a deflation between injectives, and that each injective object
I
admits an inflation
Q\twoheadrightarrow I,
with
Q
being projective-injective.

Proof.

We only prove the implication (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii), since the proof of the converse result is dual. Assume that (i) holds. The facts that
\mathscr{C}
has enough injectives and
\mathrm{id}(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E})\leq 1
follow from
Lemma

3.4
. It remains to prove that
1\leq\mathrm{codom.dim}(\mathscr{C}).
Let
I
be an injective object in
\mathscr{C}
. By the assumption, we have a conflation
P_{1}\rightarrowtail P_{0}\twoheadrightarrow I,
with
P_{0},P_{1}
projective in
\mathscr{C}
. We also have a conflation
P_{0}\rightarrowtail Q\rightarrowtail\Sigma P_{0},
with
Q
projective-injective. Since
\mathrm{pd}(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E})\leq 1
, we know by
Proposition

2.1
that the category is hereditary and, in particular,
\mathbb{E}^{2}(-,P)=0
. Thus, by
Corollary

3.3
,
\Sigma P_{0}
is injective. By (ET4), we can complete these two conflations to a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,I}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\,R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma P_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma P_{0}}}

Since
I
is injective, the rightmost vertical conflation splits. Thus, we get a deflation
Q\twoheadrightarrow I\oplus\Sigma P_{0}.
By composing it with the projection onto the first factor, which is also a deflation, we obtain a deflation
Q\twoheadrightarrow I
thanks to
\mathrm{(ET4)}^{\mathrm{op}}
. Since
I
was an arbitrary injective object, this proves that
1\leq\mathrm{codom.dim}(\mathscr{C}).

∎

3.2.
0-Auslander extriangulated categories

Definition 3.7
.

An
extriangulated category is a
0-Auslander extriangulated category
if it satisfies the equivalent conditions of
Proposition

3.6
.
Explicitly, it has enough projectives, enough injectives, global dimension at most one, dominant dimension at least one, and codominant dimension at least one.

A 0-Auslander extriangulated category is
reduced
if (up to isomorphism) its only projective-injective object is 0.

Remark 3.8
.

If
\mathscr{C}
is a 0-Auslander extriangulated category, then it is either split, i.e.
\mathbb{E}(-,-)=0
(hence we have
\operatorname{gl.dim}\mathscr{C}=0
and
\operatorname{dom.dim}\mathscr{C}=\operatorname{codom.dim}\mathscr{C}=\infty
), or it has
\operatorname{gl.dim}\mathscr{C}=1=\operatorname{dom.dim}\mathscr{C}=\operatorname{codom.dim}\mathscr{C}
.

Remark 3.9
.

Unravelling the definition, reduced 0-Auslander extriangulated categories are precisely the categories arising in
[
PPPP19
, Section 4]
:

(1)

For every object
X\in\mathscr{C}
, there is a conflation
P_{1}\rightarrowtail P_{0}\twoheadrightarrow X
with
P_{0},P_{1}
projective in
\mathscr{C}
.

(2)

For any projective
P\in\mathscr{C}
, the morphism
P\rightarrowtail 0
is an inflation.

Remark 3.10
.

The ideal quotient of an arbitrary 0-Auslander category by the morphisms factoring through projective-injective objects is a reduced 0-Auslander extriangulated category. This will be used many times in the proofs.

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a 0-Auslander extriangulated category with full subcategory of projectives
\mathcal{P}
, and full subcategory of injectives
\mathcal{I}
.
Write
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
for the image of
\mathcal{P}
in the quotient of
\mathscr{C}
by the ideal generated by the projective-injective objects.
Then the functor sending
X\in\mathscr{C}
to
\mathscr{C}/_{\mathcal{P}\cap\mathcal{I}}(-,X)|_{\overline{\mathcal{P}}}
induces an equivalence of categories

\mathscr{C}/\mathcal{I}\cong\operatorname{mod}\overline{\mathcal{P}}.

Proof.

This was proved for reduced 0-Auslander extriangulated categories in
[
PPPP19
, Section 4]
.
The non-reduced case immediately follows by reduction.
∎

By similarly applying
[
PPPP19
, Remark 4.32 and Corollary 4.35]
, we obtain:

Lemma 3.12
.

There are quasi-inverse equivalences of additive categories
\Sigma:\overline{\mathcal{P}}\overset{\simeq}{\leftrightarrows}\underline{\mathcal{I}}:\Omega
given on objects by exchanging
P
and
I
in a fixed conflation
P\rightarrowtail Q\twoheadrightarrow I
, with
Q
projective-injective.

3.3.
Examples of 0-Auslander extriangulated categories

3.3.1.
The minimal example

The most elementary example of a reduced 0-Auslander category over a field
k
has two non-zero indecomposable objects (up to isomorpism)
P
and
I
with endomorphism rings isomorphic to
k
, no non-zero morphisms between them, and a one-dimensional space
\mathbb{E}(I,P)\cong k
realized by
\mathfrak{s}
-triangles of the form

P\rightarrowtail 0\twoheadrightarrow I\overset{\delta}{\dashrightarrow}.

While this small example may already look rather peculiar, it admits several interpretations in terms of well-known categories. Namely, it is equivalent, as an extriangulated category, to each of the following categories:

•

The homotopy category
K^{[-1,0]}(\operatorname{proj}kA_{1})
of complexes with projective components and concentrated in degrees -1 and 0 over the path algebra of the quiver
A_{1}
, with the extriangulated structure induced by the embedding into the bounded homotopy category;

•

The cluster category of type
A_{1}
, taken with the maximal relative structure making one of the two cluster tilting objects projective;

•

The quotient of the module category of the path algebra of the quiver
A_{2}
by the ideal of morphisms factoring through projective-injective objects, with the extriangulated structure induced from the abelian structure on the module category;

•

The stable category of the module category of the preprojective algebra of type
A_{2}
, taken with the maximal relative structure making one of the two simple objects projective.

3.3.2.
Module categories

Let
\Lambda
be a basic connected finite-dimensional algebra over an algebraically closed field
k
. The category of finite-dimensional
\Lambda
-modules, considered with the standard, i.e. abelian, extriangulated structure, has dominant dimension (at most) one if and only if
\Lambda
has dominant dimension (at most) one in the classical sense
[
Tac64
]
. By
Remark

3.8
,
\operatorname{mod}\Lambda
is 0-Auslander if and only if
\Lambda
is semisimple or
\operatorname{gl.dim}\Lambda=1=\operatorname{dom.dim}\Lambda.
The latter happens if and only if
\Lambda
is Morita equivalent to the
m\times m
upper triangular matrix algebra over
k
, for
m\in\mathbb{N}
; see
[
Iya11
, Proposition 1.17]
for a detailed proof of a slightly more general result. Equivalently,
\operatorname{gl.dim}\operatorname{mod}\Lambda=1=\operatorname{dom.dim}\operatorname{mod}\Lambda
if and only if
\Lambda
is (Morita equivalent to) the path algebra of a linearly oriented quiver of Dynkin type
A
.

This might suggest that there are very few examples of 0-Auslander extriangulated categories. However, for any
\Lambda
, there is an associated reduced 0-Auslander extriangulated category, of which
\operatorname{mod}\Lambda
is a quotient: its homotopy category of 2-term complexes with projective components.

3.3.3.
Homotopy category of 2-term complexes

Let
K^{\text{b}}(\operatorname{proj}\Lambda)
be the homotopy category of bounded complexes of projective modules over an Artin algebra
\Lambda
.
Consider the full subcategory
\mathscr{C}=K^{[-1,0]}(\operatorname{proj}\Lambda)
of
K^{\text{b}}(\operatorname{proj}\Lambda)
whose objects are those complexes concentrated in (cohomological) degrees -1 and 0.
Objects in
\mathscr{C}
are called two-term complexes and have been studied in
[
DF15
]
, and in
[
AIR14
,
Kim20
]
in connection with
\tau
-tilting theory and silting theory.
Then
\mathscr{C}
is a reduced 0-Auslander extriangulated category, with projective objects being the stalk complexes concentrated in degree 0 and injective objects those concentrated in degree -1. In this setting,
Proposition

3.11
means that
\operatorname{mod}\Lambda
is the costable category of
\mathscr{C}
.

Theorem

4.26
thus applies and recovers 2-term silting mutation
[
Kim20
]
.

3.3.4.
Category of 2-term complexes

There is an “unreduced” version of the previous example:
Let
\mathcal{A}
be an additive category and let
C^{[-1,0]}(\mathcal{A})
be the full subcategory of the category of complexes over
\mathcal{A}
, whose objects are concentrated in degrees -1 and 0.
Endow
C^{[-1,0]}(\mathcal{A})
with the exact structure given by component-wise split sequences.
By
[
BSSZ05
, Proposition 3.6, Corollary 3.9]
,
C^{[-1,0]}(\mathcal{A})
is a 0-Auslander exact category with projective objects having no injective summands the complexes concentrated in degree 0, injective objects having no projective summands the complexes concentrated in degree -1, and projective-injective indecomposable objects the complexes of the form
P\xrightarrow{1}P
, for
P\in\mathcal{A}
indecomposable.

Applying
Theorem

4.26
to this setting, with
\mathcal{A}=\operatorname{proj}A
, yields a version of 2-term silting mutation “with coefficients”.

3.3.5.
Maximal almost rigid modules

Fix an algebraically closed field
k
, and let
Q
be an orientation of a Dynkin diagram of type
A_{n+2}
.
Reading
[
Rea06
]
considered a polygon
P(Q)
whose geometric realization depends on the orientation of
Q
and defined a partial order on the set of triangulations of
P(Q)
. He proved that the resulting poset is a lattice which he called a
Cambrian lattice
of
Q
. This construction generalizes Tamari lattices which are shown to correspond to
Q
being linearly oriented. Barnard–Gunawan–Meehan–Schiffler
[
BGMS23
]
found a representation-theoretic realization of such Cambrian lattices for quivers of type
A
by defining maximal almost rigid modules and finding a natural partial order on them.

Definition 3.13

(
[
BGMS23
]
)
.

Let
M
be a basic representation of
Q
over
k
, and let
M=M_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus M_{r}
be a decomposition of
M
into (pairwise non-isomorphic) indecomposables. The representation
M
is called
almost rigid
if, for any
i,j=1,\ldots,r
and any non-split short exact sequence
0\to M_{i}\to E\to M_{j}\to 0
, the middle term
E
is indecomposable, and it is called
maximal almost rigid
if moreover
M\oplus N
almost rigid implies
N\in\operatorname{add}M
.

Let
\mathscr{E}
be the category
\operatorname{mod}kQ
, endowed with the collection of all short exact sequences whose middle term is not indecomposable.
The following result is due to Thomas Brüstle, Eric Hanson, Sunny Roy and Ralf Schiffler
[
BS23
]
:

Theorem 3.14
.

The category
\mathscr{E}
is a 0-Auslander exact category whose silting objects precisely coincide with the maximal almost rigid representations.

Applying
4.26
to the category
\mathscr{E}
thus recovers the mutation of maximal almost rigid representations see
[
BGMS23
, Theorem 6.8]
.

3.3.6.
Cluster categories

Let
K
be a field and let
\mathscr{C}
be a
K
-linear triangulated category with shift functor
\Sigma
.
Assume that
\mathscr{C}

•

is Hom-finite: for any
X,Y\in\mathscr{C}
,
\mathscr{C}(X,Y)
is a finite-dimensional
K
-vector space;

•

is 2-Calabi–Yau:
\Sigma^{2}
is a Serre functor, or in other words there are binatural isomorphisms
\mathscr{C}(X,\Sigma Y)\cong D\mathscr{C}(Y,\Sigma X)
, where
D=\mathrm{Hom}_{K}(-,K)
;

•

has a cluster-tilting object
T
:

–

T is rigid:
\mathscr{C}(T,\Sigma T)=0
and

–

\mathscr{C}(T,\Sigma X)=0
(or equivalently
\mathscr{C}(X,\Sigma T)=0
) implies
X\in\operatorname{add}T
.

Examples of such categories are given by cluster categories of acyclic quivers
[
BMR+06
]
(see also
[
CCS06
]
) and of algebras of global dimension 2 or Jacobi-finite quivers with potentials
[
Ami09
]
.

Replace the triangulated structure of
\mathscr{C}
by the maximal relative
[
HLN21
]
extriangulated structure making
T
projective (see
Definition

A.11
): endow
\mathscr{C}
with those triangles

X\to Y\to Z\xrightarrow{\eta}\Sigma X
for which
\eta
factors through
\operatorname{add}\Sigma T
.
With this extriangulated structure,
\mathscr{C}
is a reduced 0-Auslander extriangulated category, with projective objects
\operatorname{add}T
and injective objects
\operatorname{add}\Sigma T
.
This extriangulated structure was introduced in
[
PPPP19
]
where it was used for studying polytopal realizations of
g
-vector fans associated with cluster algebras of finite type.

Applied to this setting,
Theorems

4.26
and
4.28
recover the mutation theory of cluster-tilting objects (with a natural orientation): Even though our results only see one of the two exchange triangles associated with mutations (the one for which
g
-vectors, or indices, are additive), the 2-Calabi–Yau property gives the other exchange triangle.

Remark 3.15
.

We note that integral cluster categories (or acyclic cluster categories over a principal ideal domain) of
[
KS12
]
are also 0-Asulander.
Theorem

4.26
applies to them whenever all rigid objects admit a unique decomposition into indecomposables with local endomorphism rings. The unique decomposition property holds for rigid objects in such categories over arbitrary principal ideal domains, as explained in
[
KS12
, Section 5]
based on results of Crawley-Boevey
[
CB96
]
. The locality amounts to the locality of the ground ring. Thus,
Theorem

4.26
applies to acyclic cluster categories over local principal ideal domains - in other words, over fields (as discussed above) or discrete valuation rings.

3.3.7.
Rigid subcategories of triangulated categories

Let
\mathscr{D}
be a triangulated category with some rigid full subcategory
\mathcal{R}
.
Endow
\mathscr{D}
with the maximal relative extriangulated structure making all objects in
\mathcal{R}
projective.
For this relative structure, the full subcategory
\mathcal{R}\ast\Sigma\mathcal{R}
is extension-closed, hence extriangulated.
One easily checks that
\mathcal{R}\ast\Sigma\mathcal{R}
is reduced 0-Auslander.

Theorem

4.26
thus applies to

•

Extended cohearts
[
IJY14
,
PZ20
]
of co-
t
-structures
[
Pau08
,
Bon10
]
, inducing mutation for intermediate co-
t
-structures (this example is detailed in
Section

5
);

•

Triangulated categories with a fixed cluster tilting object (or subcategory). Our mutation theory thus recovers relative tilting theory
[
YZ19
, Theorem 4.10]
(and generalizes it to the setting of
[
YZZ19
]
), without making use of
\tau
-tilting theory;

•

Settings generalizing the previous one by only assuming rigidity instead of cluster tilting, see for example
[
FGL19
,
ZZ20
]
. This implies in particular a nice mutation theory for
d
-rigid objects that are finitely presented with respect to some fixed
d
-rigid subcategory;

3.3.8.
2-term subcategories in perfect derived categories

Let
A
be a non-positive differential graded algebra. Its perfect derived category
\mbox{per}A
has a bounded co-
t
-structure with coheart
\operatorname{add}_{\mbox{per}A}A
. The category
\operatorname{add}_{\mbox{per}A}A\ast\operatorname{add}_{\mbox{per}A}A[1]
gives thus a special case of the previous example, as it is the extended coheart of this co-
t
-structure. Since for a finite-dimensional algebra
\Lambda
considered as a dg algebra with
0
differential, its perfect derived category is
K^{\text{b}}(\operatorname{proj}\Lambda)
, this also generalizes the example from
Section

3.3.3
.
Such categories appeared in a version of the Brenner-Butler theorem for 2-term subcategories in
K^{\text{b}}(\operatorname{proj}\Lambda)
in recent work
[
BZ21
]
.

The fundamental domain of Amiot’s equivalence
[
Ami09
]
is a special case of such categories which plays an important role in the context of additive categorifications of cluster algebras.

3.3.9.
Rigid subcategories of exact categories

Let
\mathscr{E}
be an exact category with enough injectives, and let
\mathcal{R}
be a rigid full subcategory of
\mathscr{E}
, containing all injectives.
Write
\mathscr{C}=\operatorname{Coker}(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{R})
for the full subcategory of
\mathscr{E}
whose objects are those
X
for which there exists a conflation
R_{1}\rightarrowtail R_{0}\twoheadrightarrow X
with
R_{0},R_{1}\in\mathcal{R}
.

Endow
\mathscr{E}
with the maximal relative exact structure, denoted
\mathscr{E}_{\mathcal{R}}
, making all objects in
\mathcal{R}
projective.
Because
\mathcal{R}
is rigid,
\mathscr{C}
is extension-closed in
\mathscr{E}_{\mathcal{R}}
hence exact, for this relative structure.
Moreover, all (non-relative) conflations
R_{1}\rightarrowtail R_{0}\twoheadrightarrow X
of
\mathscr{E}
expressing
X
as an object of
\operatorname{Coker}(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{R})
are conflations in
\mathscr{C}
(for the relative exact structure).

In particular, a conflation
R\rightarrowtail Q\twoheadrightarrow\Sigma R
in
\mathscr{E}
with
R\in\mathcal{R}
and
Q
injective in
\mathscr{E}
gives a conflation in
\mathscr{C}
.
Moreover,
Q
is projective-injective in
\mathscr{C}
(because it is injective in
\mathscr{E}
, hence in
\mathscr{C}
, and also belongs to
\mathcal{R}
, hence is projective in
\mathscr{C}
).
The conflations
R_{1}\rightarrowtail R_{0}\twoheadrightarrow X
witness the fact that
\mathscr{C}
has enough projectives and has vanishing
\mathrm{Ext}^{2}
.
It follows that
\Sigma R
is injective in
\mathscr{C}
.
Hence,
\mathscr{C}
is a 0-Auslander extriangulated category, which is generally non-reduced.

This class of examples contains the categories of 2-term complexes of
Section

3.3.4
, but also the Frobenius exact cluster categories appearing in
[
GLS08
,
FK10
,
JKS16
,
Pre20
,
Pre21
]
thus showing relative versions (in the sense of
Section

3.3.11
) of cluster-tilting mutation in that setting.

3.3.10.
Rigid subcategories of extriangulated categories

One easily generalizes
Section

3.3.9
to extriangulated categories, up to replacing cokernels of inflations by cones over inflations.
This gives a common generalization of
Sections

3.3.9
and
3.3.7
.

As a consequence we recover relative tilting theory also for extriangulated categories
[
LZ22
]
, as detailed in the following section.

3.3.11.
Relative tilting theory

In this section, we give a (possibly new) point-of-view on relative tilting theory
[
YZ19
]
, thus including it into our approach to mutation via 0-Auslander extriangulated categories.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be an extriangulated category with enough projectives and enough injectives.
Assume that
\mathscr{C}
has a
left-cluster tilting
subcategory
\mathcal{R}
:
\mathcal{R}
is a rigid, additive full subcategory such that, for every object
X\in\mathscr{C}
, there is an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
X\rightarrowtail R_{X}^{0}\twoheadrightarrow R_{X}^{1}\dashrightarrow
with
R_{X}^{0},R_{X}^{1}\in\mathcal{R}
.

Notation 3.16
.

For each object
X\in\mathscr{C}
, we fix two
\mathfrak{s}
-triangles

X\overset{i_{X}}{\rightarrowtail}I_{X}\overset{p_{X}}{\twoheadrightarrow}\Sigma X\overset{\eta_{X}}{\dashrightarrow}\text{ and }\Omega X\overset{\iota_{X}}{\rightarrowtail}P_{X}\overset{\pi_{X}}{\twoheadrightarrow}X\overset{\varepsilon_{X}}{\dashrightarrow},

where
P_{X}
is projective and
I_{X}
is injective.
If
\mathcal{X}
is any full additive subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
, we write
\Omega\mathcal{X}
(resp.
\Sigma\mathcal{X}
) for the full subcategory whose objects are all those
Y
that appear in an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle of the form
Y\rightarrowtail P\twoheadrightarrow X\dashrightarrow
with
P
projective and
X
in
\mathcal{X}
(resp. of the form
X\rightarrowtail I\twoheadrightarrow Y\dashrightarrow
with
I
injective and
Y
in
\mathcal{X}
).
We note that subcategories of the form
\Omega\mathcal{X}
contain all projectives.
Dually subcategories
\Sigma\mathcal{X}
contain all injectives.

By
[
HLN21
, Proposition 3.19]
, there is a relative extriangulated structure on
\mathscr{C}
given by:

\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{R}}(Z,X)=\{\delta\in\mathbb{E}(Z,X)\;|\;X\xrightarrow{\forall f}R\text{ with }R\in\mathcal{R},f_{\ast}\delta=0\}.

This is the biggest closed additive sub-bifunctor of
\mathbb{E}
for which all objects in
\mathcal{R}
are injective.

As already mentioned in
Section

3.3.7
, there is a dual version, denoted by
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{R}}
that makes all objects in
\mathcal{R}
projectives.

Definition 3.17
.

An object
X\in\mathscr{C}
(or a full additive subcategory
\mathcal{X}
) is
\mathcal{R}
-rigid
, or alternatively
relative rigid
, if it is rigid in
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{R}})
.

Although this is not the definition that appears in the literature, the lemma below shows that it is equivalent to it.

Lemma 3.18
.

For any
X\in\mathscr{C}
, the following are equivalent:

(a)

X
is
\mathcal{R}
-rigid,

(b)

Any morphism in
[\mathcal{R}](X,\Sigma X)
factors through
p_{X}
.

The lemma follows from the more detailed:

Proposition 3.19
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a left-cluster tilting subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
.
Then we have:

(1)

\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{R}}=\mathbb{E}_{\Omega\mathcal{R}}
;

(2)

for any
X,Y\in\mathscr{C}
,
\mathbb{E}_{\Omega\mathcal{R}}(X,Y)
is the quotient of
[\mathcal{R}](X,\Sigma Y)
by the image of
(p_{Y})_{\ast}:\mathscr{C}(X,I_{Y})\to\mathscr{C}(X,\Sigma Y)
;

(3)

(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{R}})
has enough injectives and is hereditary;

(4)

If moreover,
\mathcal{R}
contains the projective objects of
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E})
, then the extriangulated category
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E}_{\Omega\mathcal{R}})=(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{R}})
is 0-Auslander with subcategory of injectives
\mathcal{R}
and subcategory of projectives
\Omega\mathcal{R}
.

Proof.

We consider the exact sequence

(\ast)\hskip 15.0pt\mathscr{C}(X,I_{Y})\to\mathscr{C}(X,\Sigma Y)\xrightarrow{(\eta_{Y})_{\sharp}}\mathbb{E}(X,Y)\to 0

associated with the
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
Y\overset{i_{Y}}{\rightarrowtail}I_{Y}\overset{p_{Y}}{\twoheadrightarrow}\Sigma Y\overset{\eta_{Y}}{\dashrightarrow}
,
and we prove the statement in six easy steps:

(a)

For any
u\in[\mathcal{R}](X,\Sigma Y)
, we have
u^{\ast}\eta_{Y}\in\mathbb{E}_{\Omega\mathcal{R}}(X,Y)
.

(b)

For any
u\in\mathscr{C}(X,\Sigma Y)
such that
u^{\ast}\eta_{Y}\in\mathbb{E}_{\Omega\mathcal{R}}(X,Y)
, we have
u\in[\mathcal{R}](X,\Sigma Y)
.

(c)

\mathbb{E}_{\Omega\mathcal{R}}\subseteq\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{R}}
.

(d)

\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{R}}\subseteq\mathbb{E}_{\Omega\mathcal{R}}
.

(e)

(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{R}})
has injective dimension at most one.

(f)

If
\mathcal{R}
contains all projectives, then
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{R}})
has codominant dimension at least one.

We now begin the proof.

(a) Let
u\in[\mathcal{R}](X,\Sigma Y)
, let
R\in\mathcal{R}
, let
\Omega R\overset{\iota_{R}}{\rightarrowtail}P_{R}\twoheadrightarrow R\dashrightarrow
and let
P\oplus\Omega R\xrightarrow{[\pi\,f]}X
be any morphism with
P
projective.
We want to show that
[\pi\,f]^{\ast}u^{\ast}\eta_{Y}=0
, or equivalently (by the exact sequence (
\ast
) and since
P
is projective) that
uf
factors through
p_{Y}
.
Because
\mathcal{R}
is rigid, any morphism from
\Omega R
to some
R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}
factors through
\iota_{R}
.
Since
u
factors through
\mathcal{R}
by assumption, this implies that the composition
uf
factors through
\iota_{R}:\Omega R\to P_{R}
.
The latter object being projective,
uf
factors through
p_{Y}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P_{R}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Sigma Y}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}

(b) Let
u\in\mathscr{C}(X,\Sigma Y)
be such that
u^{\ast}\eta_{Y}\in\mathbb{E}_{\Omega\mathcal{R}}(X,Y)
.
There are
\mathfrak{s}
-triangles
X\rightarrowtail R^{0}_{X}\twoheadrightarrow R_{X}^{1}\dashrightarrow
and
\Omega R_{X}^{0}\rightarrowtail P\twoheadrightarrow R_{X}^{0}\dashrightarrow
with

and where

is projective.
By (ET4
{}^{\text{op}}
) and
[
LN19
, Proposition 1.20]
, there is a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega R_{X}^{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega R_{X}^{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Omega R_{X}^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R_{X}^{0}}}

\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(wPO)}

where the square indicated by

is a weak push-out.
By assumption the composition
up
factors through
p_{Y}
.
Since
I_{Y}
is injective and

is an inflation, we obtain a commutative diagram as below and applying the property of a weak push-out gives a factorisation of

through

.
Hence

belongs to

.

(c) Let
\delta\in\mathbb{E}_{\Omega\mathcal{R}}(X,Y)
.
Combining (b) with the exact sequence
(\ast)
, we obtain the existence of a morphism
u\in[\mathcal{R}](X,\Sigma Y)
such that
\delta=u^{\ast}\eta_{Y}
.
Fix a factorisation
u=u_{2}u_{1}
where the domain of
u_{2}
belongs to
\mathcal{R}
.
We then have, for any morphism
Y\xrightarrow{g}R
with
R\in\mathcal{R}
:

g_{\ast}\delta=g_{\ast}u^{\ast}\eta_{Y}=g_{\ast}u_{1}^{\ast}u_{2}^{\ast}\eta_{Y}=u_{1}^{\ast}(u_{2}^{\ast}g_{\ast}\eta_{Y})=0

where the extension inside the brackets vanishes since
\mathcal{R}
is rigid.
Hence,
\delta\in\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{R}}(X,Y)
.

, let

and let

be any morphism.
There is some

, together with the injectivity of

, to obtain the solid diagram below.
Since

, the inflation

Equivalently,

The projection onto

factors through

because

(the latter equality is proven as in (a), using that

and that

is rigid).
Hence (2) precomposed with

factors as the sum of two compositions, one using (3) and the other using (4), which implies that

factors as (3) followed by

.
Because the top-right square is a weak pull-back, we obtain a factorisation of

via (5) which shows that

belongs to
\mathbb{E}_{\Omega\mathcal{R}}(X,Y)
.

(e) For any

, there is an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle

with

.
Because
\mathcal{R}
is rigid,
\varepsilon
belongs to
\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{R}}(R_{X}^{1},X)
.
By definition of
\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{R}}
, objects in
\mathcal{R}
are
\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{R}}
-injective so that this
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle witnesses the fact that

has injective dimension at most one.

, fix an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle

, with

\mathbb{E}
-projective, and assume that all
P_{R}
belong to
\mathcal{R}
.
The same remark as in (e) shows that
\varepsilon_{R}
belongs to
\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{R}}(R,\Omega R)
.
Moreover,
R
is
\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{R}}
-injective and
P_{R}
, which is
\mathbb{E}
-projective and in
\mathcal{R}
, is
\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{R}}
-projective-injective.
Since
\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{R}}=\mathbb{E}_{\Omega\mathcal{R}}
, all
\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{R}}
-projectives are, up to isomorphism, of the form
P\oplus\Omega R
for some
R\in\mathcal{R}
and some
\mathbb{E}
-projective
P
.
Therefore
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{R}})
has codominant dimension at least one.

This implies statement (4) of the proposition by
Proposition

3.6
.
∎

As a consequence,
Theorem

1.3
gives a proof of the existence of mutation for relative-tilting objects, and of exchange
\mathfrak{s}
-triangles, that does not refer to
\tau
-tilting theory.

3.3.12.
Gentle algebras

Let
(Q,I)
be a gentle bound quiver.
There is an associated
blossoming
gentle bound quiver
(Q^{\text{❀}},I^{\text{❀}})
first introduced in
[
Asa12
]
and re-introduced independently in
[
BDM+20
,
PPP21
]
.
All vertices of the blossoming bound quiver
(Q^{\text{❀}},I^{\text{❀}})
that are not leaves (sinks or sources) are 4-valent, and deleting its leaves recovers the bound quiver
(Q,I)
.
Alternatively, one can view
(Q^{\text{❀}},I^{\text{❀}})
as obtained from the same surface with dissection
[
ABCJP10
,
BCS21
,
OPS18
,
PPP19
]
as
(Q,I)
, but with more vertices corresponding to boundary arcs.

Using the blossoming bound quiver, the
\tau
-tilting theory of gentle algebras was related in
[
BDM+20
,
PPP21
]
to the combinatorics of non-kissing walks
[
McC17
]
: walks on
(Q,I)
can be defined as maximal strings for
(Q^{\text{❀}},I^{\text{❀}})
.

In
[
INP18
]
, a strategy was proposed on a specific example for constructing an exact category whose objects are walks and where extensions categorify kissings of walks.
We carry out this strategy in full generality in
Section

6
.
The category is obtained as an extension-closed full subcategory of
\operatorname{mod}(Q^{\text{❀}},I^{\text{❀}})
, and turns out to be (non-reduced) 0-Auslander.
Thus,
Theorem

4.26
gives a new proof that there are flips for non-kissing facets
[
McC17
,
BDM+20
,
PPP21
]
and also recovers the mutation theory of support
\tau
-tilting modules
[
AIR14
]
over gentle algebras.

4.
Mutation of silting subcategories

In this section, we compare several possible definitions of
maximality
for rigid objects in hereditary extriangulated categories.
Under rather mild hypotheses, they are all shown to be equivalent.
We also make use of versions of Bongartz completion and of indices (
g
-vectors) and coindices in order to establish a mutation theory for silting subcategories in 0-Auslander extriangulated categories.
Our main point is that this setting allows for quite elementary proofs, while being general enough to recover various known mutation theories used in representation theory.

4.1.
Comparison of definitions

Assume that
\mathscr{C}
is a 0-Auslander extriangulated category, and let
\mathcal{P}
be the full subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
formed by the projective objects. The full subcategory of injective objects is denoted
\mathcal{I}=\Sigma\mathcal{P}
(see
Lemma

3.12
).

Definition 4.1
.

An object
R\in\mathscr{C}
is
rigid
if it satisfies
\mathbb{E}(R,R)=0
. A full additive subcategory
\mathcal{R}
of
\mathscr{C}
is rigid, if all its objects are rigid or, equivalently, if for any
R,R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}
, we have
\mathbb{E}(R,R^{\prime})=0
.
Recall from
Section

2.2.4
that a full subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
is
thick
if it is closed under taking summands, extensions, cones of inflations and fibers of deflations. For any subcategory
\mathcal{X}
of
\mathscr{C}
, we let
\operatorname{thick}\mathcal{X}
be the smallest thick subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
containing
\mathcal{X}
.

Notation 4.2
.

We write
\operatorname{pr}\mathcal{R}
for
\operatorname{Cone}(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{R})
: the full subcategory whose objects are those
X
for which there is an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
R_{1}\rightarrowtail R_{0}\twoheadrightarrow X\dashrightarrow
with
R_{0},R_{1}\in\mathcal{R}
. Dually, we write
\operatorname{copr}\mathcal{R}
for
\operatorname{Fib}(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{R})
, the full subcategory whose objects
Y
admit an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
Y\rightarrowtail R_{0}\twoheadrightarrow R_{1}\dashrightarrow
with
R_{0},R_{1}\in\mathcal{R}
.

Theorem 4.3
.

For a given rigid, strictly full, additive subcategory
\mathcal{R}
of
\mathscr{C}
that is stable under taking summands, consider the following conditions:

(0)

\mathcal{R}
is
silting
:
\operatorname{thick}\mathcal{R}=\mathscr{C}
.

(i)

\mathcal{R}
is
maximal rigid
: For any
X\in\mathscr{C}
such that
\mathcal{R}\oplus\operatorname{add}X
is rigid, we have
X\in\mathcal{R}
.

(ii)

\mathcal{R}
is
\mathbb{E}
-tilting
: For any
X\in\mathscr{C}
such that
\mathbb{E}(X,\mathcal{R})=0
and
\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{R},X)=0
, we have
X\in\mathcal{R}
.

(iii)

\mathcal{R}
is
tilting
: For any
P\in\mathcal{P}
, there is a conflation
P\rightarrowtail R^{0}\twoheadrightarrow R^{1}
with
R^{0},R^{1}\in\mathcal{R}
.

(iv)

\mathcal{R}
is
cotilting
: For any
I\in\mathcal{I}
, there is a conflation
R_{1}\rightarrowtail R_{0}\twoheadrightarrow I
with
R_{0},R_{1}\in\mathcal{R}
.

(v)

\mathcal{R}
is
torsion-projective
:
\mathcal{R}^{\perp_{\mathbb{E}}}=\operatorname{Cone}(\mathcal{R}^{\perp_{\mathbb{E}}},\mathcal{R})
.

(
v
’)

\mathcal{R}
is
cotorsion-injective
:
{}^{\perp_{\mathbb{E}}}\mathcal{R}=\operatorname{Fib}(\mathcal{R},\,\!^{\perp_{\mathbb{E}}}\mathcal{R})
.

(vi)

\mathcal{R}
is
strongly torsion-projective
:
\mathcal{R}^{\perp_{\mathbb{E}}}=\operatorname{pr}\mathcal{R}
.

(
vi
’)

\mathcal{R}
is
strongly cotorsion-injective
:
{}^{\perp_{\mathbb{E}}}\mathcal{R}=\operatorname{copr}\mathcal{R}
.

(vii)

If
\mathscr{C}
is Krull–Schmidt and if we have
\mathcal{P}=\operatorname{add}P
and
\mathcal{R}=\operatorname{add}R
, we say that
R
is
complete rigid
if
R
and
P
have the same number of isomorphism classes of indecomposable summands.

Then
(0)

\Leftrightarrow
(
vi
)
\Leftrightarrow
(
vi
’)
\Leftrightarrow
(
v
)
\Leftrightarrow
(
v
’)
\Leftrightarrow
(
iv
)
\Leftrightarrow
(
iii
)
\Rightarrow
(
ii
)
\Rightarrow
(
i
).
If moreover any
P\in\mathcal{P}
has a left
\mathcal{R}
-approximation, or equivalently if any
I\in\mathcal{I}
has a right
\mathcal{R}
-approximation, then (
i
)
\Rightarrow
(
iii
) and, in case
\mathcal{P}=\operatorname{add}P
and
\mathscr{C}
is Krull–Schmidt, (

)
\Leftrightarrow
(

).

Remark 4.4
.

(a)

By definition, we have the implication (

)
\Rightarrow

(0)
. The implication

(b)

The proof that (

.

.

(d)

We note that definitions (

) to (

contains all projective-injective objects, allowing to consider the reduced case only in the proofs.

(e)

We also note that being
\mathbb{E}
-tilting is a weaker condition than being cluster-tilting, but both definitions are equivalent when
\mathscr{C}
is 2-Calabi–Yau.

Proof.

We only prove the statement for reduced 0-Auslander extriangulated categories, since the general case follows by reduction. The equivalence (0)
\Leftrightarrow

)

We prove the following implications:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(vi^{\prime})}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(vii)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(i)}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(v)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{in }4.4}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{if }\mathcal{R}\text{-app}}

Let

\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

.

(

)
\Rightarrow
(

) because

is rigid.

(

)

(

) Assume that
\mathcal{R}^{\perp_{\mathbb{E}}}
is included in
\operatorname{Cone}(\mathcal{R}^{\perp_{\mathbb{E}}},\mathcal{R})
.
Since
\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{R},\Sigma P)\cong\mathbb{E}^{2}(\mathcal{R},P)=0
, there is a conflation

(hence a conflation
P\rightarrowtail X\twoheadrightarrow R_{0}
), with
R_{0}\in\mathcal{R}
and
X\in\mathcal{R}^{\perp_{\mathbb{E}}}
. By assumption, there is also a conflation
Y\rightarrowtail R_{1}\twoheadrightarrow X
, with
R_{1}\in\mathcal{R}
and
Y\in\mathcal{R}^{\perp_{\mathbb{E}}}
.
By composing the deflations
R_{1}\twoheadrightarrow X\twoheadrightarrow R_{0}
, we obtain a commutative diagram of conflations

\textstyle{Y}
\textstyle{Z}
\textstyle{P}
\textstyle{Y}
\textstyle{R_{1}}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{R_{0}}
\textstyle{R_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

where the first row splits because
P
is projective in
\mathscr{C}
.
This gives a conflation
Y\oplus P\rightarrowtail R_{1}\twoheadrightarrow R_{0}
, that we push-out along the canonical projection
Y\oplus P\to Y
:

\textstyle{Y\oplus P}
\textstyle{R_{1}}
\textstyle{R_{0}}
\textstyle{Y}

\textstyle{R_{0}}
\textstyle{\Sigma P}
\textstyle{\Sigma P}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

, and we obtain the commutative diagram of conflations:

\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{R_{1}}
\textstyle{R_{1}\oplus R_{0}}
\textstyle{R_{0}}

\textstyle{\Sigma P}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

where we have used that

is rigid.
Moreover, the middle column splits because the conflation
