0=\mathbb{E}(R_{1},X)\to\mathbb{E}(Y,X)\to\mathbb{E}^{2}(X,X)=0,

and
X\in\mathcal{R}^{\perp_{\mathbb{E}}}
.
We conclude that
X\in\mathcal{R}
since
\mathcal{R}
is stable under taking sums and summands, and hence, that
\Sigma P\in\operatorname{pr}\mathcal{R}
, thus showing that
\mathcal{R}
is cotilting.

(
iv
)
\Rightarrow
(
vi
) Assume that
\mathcal{R}
is cotilting.
Since
\mathscr{C}
is hereditary, for any deflation
X\twoheadrightarrow Y
with
X\in\mathcal{R}^{\perp_{\mathbb{E}}}
, we have
Y\in\mathcal{R}^{\perp_{\mathbb{E}}}
.
This implies the inclusion of
\operatorname{pr}\mathcal{R}
in
\mathcal{R}^{\perp_{\mathbb{E}}}
.
Let
X\in\mathcal{R}^{\perp_{\mathbb{E}}}
.
Fix conflations
P_{0}\rightarrowtail X\twoheadrightarrow\Sigma P_{1}
,
R_{1}\rightarrowtail R_{0}\twoheadrightarrow\Sigma P_{1}
and
S_{1}\rightarrowtail S_{0}\twoheadrightarrow\Sigma P_{0}
, where
P_{0},P_{1}\in\mathcal{P}
and
R_{0},R_{1},S_{0},S_{1}\in\mathcal{R}
.
There is a commutative diagram of conflations

\textstyle{R_{1}}
\textstyle{R_{1}}
\textstyle{P_{0}}
\textstyle{Y}
\textstyle{R_{0}}
\textstyle{P_{0}}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{\Sigma P_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

where
R_{1}
and
X
, hence
Y
, belong to
\mathcal{R}^{\perp_{\mathbb{E}}}
.
Therefore, the conflation in the middle row of

\textstyle{S_{1}}
\textstyle{S_{1}}
\textstyle{Y}
\textstyle{S_{1}\oplus R_{0}}
\textstyle{S_{0}}
\textstyle{Y}
\textstyle{R_{0}}
\textstyle{\Sigma P_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

splits, which implies
Y\in\mathcal{R}
.
We thus have
X\in\operatorname{pr}\mathcal{R}
.

(
iii
)
\Rightarrow
(
ii
) Assume
\mathcal{R}
tilting, and let
X\in\mathscr{C}
be such that
\mathbb{E}(X,\mathcal{R})=0=\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{R},X)
.
By assumption, there are conflations
P_{1}\rightarrowtail P_{0}\twoheadrightarrow X
and
P_{1}\rightarrowtail R^{0}\twoheadrightarrow R^{1}
with
P_{0},P_{1}\in\mathcal{P}
and
R^{0},R^{1}\in\mathcal{R}
.
There is an induced commutative diagram made of conflations:

\textstyle{P_{1}}
\textstyle{P_{0}}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{R^{0}}
\textstyle{R^{0}\oplus X}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{R^{1}}
\textstyle{R^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

where the conflation in the second row splits because
\mathbb{E}(X,\mathcal{R})=0
.
Consider a conflation
P_{0}\rightarrowtail S^{0}\twoheadrightarrow S^{1}
with
S^{0},S^{1}\in\mathcal{R}
.
Together with
P_{0}\rightarrowtail R^{0}\oplus X\twoheadrightarrow R^{1}
, it induces a commutative diagram

\textstyle{P_{0}}
\textstyle{R^{0}\oplus X}
\textstyle{R^{1}}
\textstyle{S^{0}}
\textstyle{S^{0}\oplus R^{1}}
\textstyle{R^{1}}
\textstyle{S^{1}}
\textstyle{S^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

where the conflation in the second row splits because
\mathcal{R}
is rigid.
Since
\mathbb{E}(\mathcal{R},X)=0
and
\mathcal{R}
is rigid, the conflation
R^{0}\oplus X\rightarrowtail S^{0}\oplus R^{1}\twoheadrightarrow S^{1}
splits and we have
X\in\mathcal{R}
.

(
ii
)
\Rightarrow
(
i
)
immediately follows from the definitions.

Assume that
P\in\mathcal{P}
has a left
\mathcal{R}
-approximation. We show the implication (
i
)
\Rightarrow
(
iii
) and that
\Sigma P
has a right
\mathcal{R}
-approximation.
Let
P\to R^{0}
be an
\mathcal{R}
-approximation.
Because
P\rightarrowtail 0
is an inflation, any morphism with domain
P
is also an inflation.
Let thus
P\rightarrowtail R^{0}\twoheadrightarrow X
be a conflation.
For any
R\in\mathcal{R}
, it induces an exact sequence

\mathbb{E}(R,R^{0})\to\mathbb{E}(R,X)\to\mathbb{E}^{2}(R,P).

Since
R
is rigid, the first term vanishes, and by heredity the last term also vanishes.
We thus have
\mathbb{E}(R,X)=0
.
Consider the exact sequence:

\mathscr{C}(R^{0},R)\to\mathscr{C}(P,R)\to\mathbb{E}(X,R)\to\mathbb{E}(R^{0},R).

By rigidity of
\mathcal{R}
, the last term vanishes.
Moreover, by definition of an approximation, the first morphism of the exact sequence is a surjection.
This shows that we also have
\mathbb{E}(X,R)=0
.
Moreover, the exactness of the sequence

\mathbb{E}(X,R^{0})\to\mathbb{E}(X,X)\to\mathbb{E}^{2}(X,P)

implies that
X
is rigid, and hence that
X\in\mathcal{R}
.
Thus,
\mathcal{R}
is tilting.
Moreover, by pushing-out any conflation of the form
P\rightarrowtail R^{0}\twoheadrightarrow R^{1}
along the conflation
P\rightarrowtail 0\twoheadrightarrow\Sigma P
, we obtain a conflation
R^{0}\rightarrowtail R^{1}\twoheadrightarrow\Sigma P
.
Since
\mathcal{R}
is rigid, the deflation
R^{1}\twoheadrightarrow\Sigma P
is a right
\mathcal{R}
-approximation.
This shows the claim since every injective object is of the form
\Sigma P
for some projective
P
.

The proof that the existence, for any injective
I
, of a right
\mathcal{R}
-approximation of
I
implies that
(
i
)
\Rightarrow
(
iii
) and that any projective object has a left
\mathcal{R}
-approximation is dual and left to the reader.

The implications (
vi
’)
\Rightarrow
(
v
’)
\Rightarrow
(
iii
)
\Rightarrow
(
vi
’) follow from the implications (
vi
)
\Rightarrow
(
v
)
\Rightarrow
(
iv
)
\Rightarrow
(
vi
) by duality.
∎

Remark 4.5
.

Assuming the extriangulated category
\mathscr{C}
to be 0-Auslander is unnecessary for most of the implications proven above.
Recall that
\mathscr{C}
has projective dimension at most one if any object
X
appears in an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
P_{1}\rightarrowtail P_{0}\twoheadrightarrow X\dashrightarrow
with
P_{0},P_{1}
projective, and injective dimension at most one if any object
X
appears in an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
X\rightarrowtail I^{0}\twoheadrightarrow I^{1}\dashrightarrow
with
I^{0},I^{1}
injective.
We also consider the following assumptions on
\mathscr{C}
:

(H)

The extriangulated category
\mathscr{C}
is hereditary.

(P)

For any projective object
P\in\mathscr{C}
, there is an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
P\rightarrowtail Q\twoheadrightarrow I\dashrightarrow
with
I
injective and
Q
projective-injective.

(I)

For any injective object
I\in\mathscr{C}
, there is an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
P\rightarrowtail Q\twoheadrightarrow I\dashrightarrow
with
P
projective and
Q
projective-injective.

We note that, by
Proposition

3.6
,
\mathscr{C}
is 0-Auslander if and only if it satisfies (H)+(P) and has enough projectives if and only if it satisfies (H)+(I) and has enough injectives.
For future reference, we give here an explicit list of those assumptions that are used in the proof:

•

No specific assumptions on
\mathscr{C}
: (ii)
\Rightarrow
(i), (vi)
\Rightarrow
(v) and (vi’)
\Rightarrow
(v’).

•

\mathrm{pd}\mathscr{C}\leq 1
: (iii)
\Rightarrow
(ii).

•

\mathrm{id}\mathscr{C}\leq 1
: (iv)
\Rightarrow
(ii).
2
2

2

More precisely, the proof we presented went (iv)
\Rightarrow
(iii)
\Rightarrow
(ii), but the arguments dual to those in our proof of the implication (iii)
\Rightarrow
(ii) give a direct proof of the implication (iv)
\Rightarrow
(ii) and require only
\mathrm{id}\mathscr{C}\leq 1
.

•

(H)+(P): (iv)
\Rightarrow
(iii), (v’)
\Rightarrow
(iii); and (i)
\Rightarrow
(iii) under the assumption that all projectives have a left
\mathcal{R}
-approximation.

•

(H)+(I): (iii)
\Rightarrow
(iv), (v)
\Rightarrow
(iv); and (i)
\Rightarrow
(iii) under the assumption that all injectives have a right
\mathcal{R}
-approximation.

•

\mathscr{C}
is 0-Auslander: (iv)
\Rightarrow
(vi), (iii)
\Rightarrow
(vi’) and (iii)
\Leftrightarrow
(vii).

4.2.
Bongartz completions

We again assume that
\mathscr{C}
is a 0-Auslander extriangulated category (we note however that conditions (H)+(I) are sufficient) with full subcategory of projectives
\mathcal{P}
and full subcategory of injectives
\mathcal{I}
.

The following proposition is an extriangulated version of (co-)Bongartz completion, and is inspired from
[
Jas15
, Definition-Proposition 4.21]
.

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
U\in\mathscr{C}
be rigid, and let
I\in\mathscr{C}
be injective. Assume that
U^{\prime}\xrightarrow{f}I
is a right
\operatorname{add}(U,\mathcal{P}\cap\mathcal{I})
-approximation. Then, up to adding a projective-injective summand to
U^{\prime}
, we may assume that
f
is a deflation and, in the conflation
P_{U}\rightarrowtail U^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow I
, we have that
R_{U}=P_{U}\oplus U
is rigid.
If moreover
\mathcal{I}=\operatorname{add}I
, then
\operatorname{add}(R_{U},\mathcal{P}\cap\mathcal{I})
is cotilting.

Proof.

The statement follows from the case when
\mathscr{C}
is reduced 0-Auslander, which we assume now on.
First note that, since
0\twoheadrightarrow I
is a deflation, any morphism with codomain
I
is also a deflation.
Rigidity of
R_{U}
follows from the long exact sequences associated with the conflation
P_{U}\rightarrowtail U^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow I
.
More precisely, in the exact sequence

\mathscr{C}(U,U^{\prime})\to\mathscr{C}(U,I)\to\mathbb{E}(U,P_{U})\to\mathbb{E}(U,U^{\prime}),

the first morphism is surjective by construction, and because
U
is rigid, the last term is zero.
This shows that
\mathbb{E}(U,P_{U})=0
.
Next, consider the exact sequence

\mathbb{E}(U^{\prime},U)\to\mathbb{E}(P_{U},U)\to\mathbb{E}^{2}(I,U).

The first term is zero since
U
is rigid.
Moreover, we have
\mathbb{E}^{2}(I,U)=0
by heredity.
We thus have
\mathbb{E}(P_{U},U)=0
.
To see that
P_{U}
is rigid, notice that both end terms of the exact sequence

\mathbb{E}(U^{\prime},P_{U})\to\mathbb{E}(P_{U},P_{U})\to\mathbb{E}^{2}(I,P_{U})

vanish.
We have proved that
R_{U}
is rigid.
If
\mathcal{I}=\operatorname{add}I
then
\operatorname{add}(R_{U},\mathcal{P}\cap\mathcal{I})
is cotilting thanks to the conflation
P_{U}\rightarrowtail U^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow I
.
∎

Remark 4.7
.

Since
\mathscr{C}
is hereditary, we also have
P_{U}^{\perp_{\mathbb{E}}}=(P_{U}\oplus U)^{\perp_{\mathbb{E}}}
.

Corollary 4.8
.

Let
\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathscr{C}
be a rigid full subcategory stable under taking summands and containing all projective-injectives.
Assume that any injective object
I\in\mathscr{C}
has a right
\mathcal{U}
-approximation
f^{I}
(which can be assumed a deflation) and write
P^{I}_{\mathcal{U}}
for the fiber (or cocone) of
f^{I}
.
Then
\operatorname{add}(\mathcal{U}\bigoplus_{I\in\mathrm{Inj}(\mathscr{C})}P^{I}_{\mathcal{U}})
is a cotilting subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
.

Proof.

This follows immediately from
Proposition

4.6
.
∎

Lemma 4.9
.

The object
P^{I}_{\mathcal{U}}
in the corollary above is projective in
\mathcal{U}^{\perp_{\mathbb{E}}}
, hence also in the reduction of
\mathscr{C}
by
\mathcal{U}
(as defined in
Definition

2.7
).

Proof.

Applying (ET4
{}^{\text{op}}
) to the conflations
P_{\mathcal{U}}^{I}\rightarrowtail U^{I}\overset{f^{I}}{\twoheadrightarrow}I
and
P\rightarrowtail Q\twoheadrightarrow I
, and using projectivity of
Q
gives a conflation

P\rightarrowtail P^{I}_{\mathcal{U}}\oplus Q\twoheadrightarrow U^{I}
.
We thus have an exact sequence
\mathbb{E}(U^{I},-)\to\mathbb{E}(P^{I}_{\mathcal{U}}\oplus Q,-)\to\mathbb{E}(P,-)
from which the claim follows.
∎

4.3.
Indices and coindices

Assume that
\mathscr{C}
is a 0-Auslander extriangulated category, with full subcategory of projective objects
\mathcal{P}
.
Fix a tilting subcategory
\mathcal{R}
of
\mathscr{C}
.

In this section, we remark that it is possible to define the coindex of
P\in\mathcal{P}
with respect to any tilting subcategory
\mathcal{R}
of
\mathscr{C}
.
This allows to notice that some results of
[
JY20
]
hold in our context.

Proposition

4.10
will turn out to be useful when considering complete rigid objects in
Section

4.4
. We also refer to the recent
[
JS22
]
, where similar ideas, partly inspired from
[
PPPP19
]
, are considered in relation to modified Caldero–Chapoton maps
[
HJ15
,
HJ16
,
JS21
]
.

Recall that, if
X\in\mathscr{C}
, then its index is the (well-defined) element

\operatorname{ind}_{\mathcal{P}}X=[P_{0}]-[P_{1}]\in K_{0}^{\text{sp}}(\mathcal{P}),

where
P_{1}\rightarrowtail P_{0}\twoheadrightarrow X
is a conflation with
P_{0},P_{1}\in\mathcal{P}
.
Note that our assumptions imply
K_{0}(\mathscr{C})\cong K_{0}^{\text{sp}}(\mathcal{P})
.

Since
\mathcal{R}
is tilting, any
P\in\mathcal{P}
admits a conflation
P\rightarrowtail R^{0}\twoheadrightarrow R^{1}
with
R^{0},R^{1}\in\mathcal{R}
. Define

\operatorname{coind}_{\mathcal{R}}P=[R^{0}]-[R^{1}]\in K_{0}^{\text{sp}}(\mathcal{R}).

One easily checks that
\operatorname{coind}_{\mathcal{R}}P
is well-defined (see
[
PPPP19
, Lemma 4.36]
).

Our next proposition is inspired from
[
JY20
, Theorem 1.2]
and
[
DIJ19
, Corollary 6.20]
(for 2-Calabi–Yau triangulated categories).

Proposition 4.10
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a 0-Auslander extriangulated category, with full subcategory of projective objects
\mathcal{P}
.
Assume that
\mathcal{R}
is tilting.
Then the morphisms

\operatorname{coind}_{\mathcal{R}}:K_{0}^{\text{sp}}(\mathcal{P})\rightleftarrows K_{0}^{\text{sp}}(\mathcal{R}):\operatorname{ind}_{\mathcal{P}}

are inverse isomorphisms.

Proof.

For any
P\in\mathcal{P}
, fix a conflation
P\rightarrowtail R^{0}\twoheadrightarrow R^{1}
with
R^{0},R^{1}\in\mathcal{R}
. By
[
PPPP19
, Lemma 4.38]
we have
\operatorname{ind}_{\mathcal{P}}[R^{0}]=\operatorname{ind}_{\mathcal{P}}[P]+\operatorname{ind}_{\mathcal{P}}[R^{1}]
. Hence

\displaystyle[P]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{ind}_{\mathcal{P}}[P]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{ind}_{\mathcal{P}}[R^{0}]-\operatorname{ind}_{\mathcal{P}}[R^{1}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{ind}_{\mathcal{P}}\left([R^{0}]-[R^{1}]\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{ind}_{\mathcal{P}}\operatorname{coind}_{\mathcal{R}}[P].

Now, for any
R\in\mathcal{R}
, there is a conflation
P_{1}\rightarrowtail P_{0}\twoheadrightarrow R
with
P_{0},P_{1}\in\mathcal{P}
.
Consider a conflation
P_{0}\rightarrowtail U^{0}\twoheadrightarrow U^{1}
with
U^{0},U^{1}\in\mathcal{R}
.
Then there is a commutative diagram of conflations:

\textstyle{P_{1}}
\textstyle{P_{0}}
\textstyle{R}
\textstyle{P_{1}}
\textstyle{U^{0}}
\textstyle{R\oplus U^{1}}
\textstyle{U^{1}}
\textstyle{U^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

where the third column splits because
\mathcal{R}
is rigid.
This implies the following equalities:

\displaystyle\operatorname{coind}_{\mathcal{R}}[P_{0}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[U^{0}]-[U^{1}]

\displaystyle\operatorname{coind}_{\mathcal{R}}[P_{1}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[U^{0}]-[U^{1}]-[R]\hskip 28.45274pt

\displaystyle\text{hence }\hskip 42.67912pt[R]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{coind}_{\mathcal{R}}[P_{0}]-\operatorname{coind}_{\mathcal{R}}[P_{1}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{coind}_{\mathcal{R}}\operatorname{ind}_{\mathcal{P}}[R].

∎

Definition 4.11
.

Assume that
\mathscr{C}
is Krull–Schmidt and let
R\in\mathcal{R}
be indecomposable. Following
[
JY20
]
, the (categorical or homological) c-vector of
(R,\mathcal{R})
with respect to
\mathcal{P}
is the element

c_{\mathcal{P}}(R,\mathcal{R})=[R]^{\ast}\circ\operatorname{coind}_{\mathcal{R}}\text{ of }K_{0}^{\text{sp}}(\mathcal{P})^{\ast},

where
(-)^{\ast}=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(-,\mathbb{Z})
, and
[R]^{\ast}\in K_{0}^{\text{sp}}(\mathcal{R})^{\ast}
is defined by
\langle[R]^{\ast},[S]\rangle=\delta_{[R],[S]}
, for any
S\in\mathcal{P}
indecomposable.

Corollary 4.12
.

For any two indecomposable objects
R,S\in\mathcal{R}
, we have

\langle c_{\mathcal{P}}(R,\mathcal{R}),\operatorname{ind}_{\mathcal{P}}S\rangle=\delta_{[R],[S]}.

4.4.
Complete rigid objects

Assume that
\mathscr{C}
is a 0-Auslander extriangulated category, with full subcategory of injective objects
\mathcal{I}
.
Assume that all rigid subcategories (
\mathcal{I},\mathcal{R},\operatorname{add}R,\ldots
) of
\mathscr{C}
apprearing below are Krull–Schmidt. Also assume, in this section, that
\mathcal{I}=\operatorname{add}I
, where
I=I_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus I_{n}
is basic and each
I_{j}
indecomposable.
An immediate consequence of
Proposition

4.10
is:

Corollary 4.13
.

Asumme that
R=R_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus R_{m}
is basic tilting. Then
m=n
.

In fact,
Proposition

4.6
shows that some converse holds.

Proposition 4.14
.

Let
R=R_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus R_{m}
be basic rigid in
\mathscr{C}
. Assume that
I
has a right
\operatorname{add}(R)
-approximation. Then
m\leq n
, with equality if and only if
R
is tilting.

Proof.

Let
X_{R}
be the Bongartz completion of
R
, given by
Proposition

4.6
.
Then
R\oplus X_{R}
is tilting, and
Corollary

4.13
shows that
m\leq n
.
Assume that
m=n
. Then
R
and
R\oplus X_{R}
have the same number of isomorphism classes of indecomposable summands. Thus
X_{R}
belongs to
\operatorname{add}R
, which implies that
R
is tilting.
∎

Corollary 4.15
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a tilting subcategory such that
I
has a right
\mathcal{R}
-approximation.
Then there is a tilting object
R
such that
\mathcal{R}=\operatorname{add}R
.

Proof.

Let
R_{0}\to I
be a right
\mathcal{R}
-approximation, and let
R
be a maximal basic summand of
R_{0}
.
Up to adding some indecomposables in
\mathcal{R}
to
R
, we may assume that
R
is a basic rigid object with
n
summands.
By
Proposition

4.14
,
R
is tilting.

Theorem

4.3
shows that
R
is maximal rigid, hence that
\mathcal{R}=\operatorname{add}R
.
∎

4.5.
\mathbb{E}
-tilting objects and reductions of hereditary extriangulated categories

Our proof of
Theorem

4.26
makes use of the reduction procedure for hereditary extriangulated categories, as introduced in
Section

2.2.3
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be an extriangulated category (which is not assumed 0-Auslander).
Let
\mathcal{R}
be a rigid subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
, and let
\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathcal{R}}=\frac{{}^{\perp_{1}}\mathcal{R}\cap\mathcal{R}^{\perp_{1}}}{[\mathcal{R}]}
be the associated reduction.

Lemma 4.16
.

For any
X\in{}^{\perp_{1}}\mathcal{R}\cap\mathcal{R}^{\perp_{1}}
, the following are equivalent.

(i)

\operatorname{add}(\mathcal{R},X)\subseteq\mathscr{C}
is
\mathbb{E}
-tilting.

(ii)

\operatorname{add}X\subseteq\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathcal{R}}
is
\overline{\mathbb{E}}
-tilting.

Proof.

This is an immediate consequence of the fact that for any
X,X^{\prime}\in{}^{\perp_{1}}\mathcal{R}\cap\mathcal{R}^{\perp_{1}}
, we have
\overline{\mathbb{E}}(X,X^{\prime})=\mathbb{E}(X,X^{\prime})
by definition.
∎

Proposition 4.17
.

If there is an
\mathbb{E}
-tilting
projective
object
X\in\mathscr{C}
, then
\mathrm{Proj}_{(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E})}=\operatorname{add}X
holds. Dually for injectives.

Proof.

Since any
P\in\mathrm{Proj}_{(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E})}
satisfies
\mathbb{E}(P,X)=\mathbb{E}(X,P)=0
, it follows
P\in\operatorname{add}X
since
\operatorname{add}X
is
\mathbb{E}
-tilting by assumption.
∎

Remark 4.18
.

Suppose there is an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
X\to 0\to Y\overset{\theta}{\dashrightarrow}
. The following holds.

(1)

X
is uniquely determined up to isomorphism by
Y
, and vice-versa.

(2)

X=0
if and only if
Y=0
.

(3)

If
X\neq 0
, we have
\theta\neq 0
. In particular, we have
\mathbb{E}(Y,X)\neq 0
.

(4)

There are natural isomorphisms

\theta^{\sharp}\colon\mathscr{C}(X,-)\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}(Y,-)\quad\text{and}\quad\theta_{\sharp}\colon\mathscr{C}(-,Y)\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}(-,X).

In particular, we have
\mathrm{End}_{\mathscr{C}}(X)\cong\mathbb{E}(Y,X)\cong\mathrm{End}_{\mathscr{C}}(Y)
.

(5)

X
is indecomposable if and only if
Y
is indecomposable.

Proposition 4.19
.

Assume
\mathscr{C}
is hereditary. If there is a conflation
X\rightarrowtail 0\twoheadrightarrow Y
, then the following holds.

(1)

X
is projective, and
Y
is injective. In particular, both
X
and
Y
are rigid.

(2)

If moreover
X
is
\mathbb{E}
-tilting, then
\mathrm{Proj}_{(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E})}=\operatorname{add}X
. Dually for
Y
.

Proof.

(1) is immediate from the exactness of
0\to\mathbb{E}(X,-)\to 0
and
0\to\mathbb{E}(-,Y)\to 0
. (2) follows from
Proposition

4.17
.
∎

Corollary 4.20
.

Assume
\mathscr{C}
is hereditary. If
X
admits an inflation
X\rightarrowtail 0
and a deflation
0\twoheadrightarrow X
simultaneously, then
X=0
. In particular, if there is a conflation
X\rightarrowtail 0\twoheadrightarrow Y
, then
X\cong Y
holds if and only if
X=0
.

Proof.

By
Proposition

4.19
, the existence of
X\rightarrowtail 0
implies that
X
is projective. Thus a conflation
W\rightarrowtail 0\twoheadrightarrow X
should split, which forces
X=0
.
∎

Corollary 4.21
.

Assume
\mathscr{C}
is hereditary. Suppose that there exist both of

•

X\rightarrowtail 0\twoheadrightarrow Y
in which
X
is
\mathbb{E}
-tilting,

•

X^{\prime}\rightarrowtail 0\twoheadrightarrow Y^{\prime}
in which
Y^{\prime}
is
\mathbb{E}
-tilting.

Then we have
\operatorname{add}X=\mathrm{Proj}_{(\mathscr{C},E)}\ni X^{\prime}
and
\operatorname{add}Y^{\prime}=\mathrm{Inj}_{(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E})}\ni Y
.

Thus, if moreover
X
is indecomposable, it should be the unique indecomposable projective in
\mathscr{C}
. Similarly for
Y^{\prime}
.

We also have an equivalence of categories
\Sigma\colon\mathrm{Proj}_{(\mathscr{C},E)}\overset{\simeq}{\longleftrightarrow}\mathrm{Inj}_{(\mathscr{C},E)}\colon\Omega
.

Remark 4.22
.

Corollary

4.21
does not exclude possible existence of rigid objects which are non-projective and non-injective, and this is the main obstacle in proving existence of mutation for silting subcategories.

4.6.
Irreducible mutations

Recall that a morphism
X\xrightarrow{f}R
is left minimal if any endomorphism
g
satisfying
gf=f
is an isomorphism.
It is radical if for any
R\xrightarrow{g}X
,
1-gf
is an isomorphism.
Note that it follows from
Proposition

A.8
that, in an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
X\overset{f}{\rightarrowtail}Y\overset{g}{\twoheadrightarrow}Z\dashrightarrow
, the morphism
f
is left minimal if and only if the morphism
g
is radical.

Lemma 4.23
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be an extriangulated category, let
\mathcal{R}
be a Krull–Schmidt, rigid subcategory, let
P\in\mathscr{C}
be projective, and let

P\rightarrowtail R^{0}\twoheadrightarrow R^{1}\dashrightarrow
be an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle with
R^{0},R^{1}\in\mathcal{R}
and where the inflation is left minimal. Then
\operatorname{add}R^{0}\cap\operatorname{add}R^{1}=0
.

Proof.

The proof of
[
AI12
, Lemma 2.25]
carries over with only minor modifications.
Fix an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
P\overset{f}{\rightarrowtail}R^{0}\overset{g}{\twoheadrightarrow}R^{1}\dashrightarrow
as in the statement.
Let
\alpha:R^{0}\to R^{1}
be any morphism. We want to prove that
\alpha
is radical.

Because
P
is projective, there is a (plain) commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R^{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R^{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R^{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R^{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

By rigidity of
\mathcal{R}
, the morphism
f
is a left
\mathcal{R}
-approximation, hence the existence of a morphism
u
making the leftmost upper triangle commute.
Since
g
is a weak cokernel of
f
, there is a morphism
v
such that
\alpha=vg+gu
.
By assumption,
f
is left minimal and thus
g
is radical.
This shows that
\alpha
is radical, and hence that any morphism from
R^{0}
to
R^{1}
is radical.
If
R^{0}
and
R^{1}
had some common (up to isomorphism) summand
R
, then the composition
R^{0}\twoheadrightarrow R\rightarrowtail R^{1}
would not be radical.
∎

Remark 4.24
.

If
\mathcal{R}
is Krull–Schmidt, any
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle of the form
X\rightarrowtail R^{0}\twoheadrightarrow R^{1}\dashrightarrow
, with
R^{0},R^{1}\in\mathcal{R}
, is isomorphic to the direct sum of an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle as in the statement of
Lemma

4.23
and of an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle of the form
0\rightarrowtail R\overset{1}{\twoheadrightarrow}R\dashrightarrow
.

Definition 4.25
.

A rigid subcategory
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
is called
almost complete
if it contains all projective-injectives and if there exists an indecomposable
R\in\mathscr{C}
such that
\operatorname{add}(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\cup\{R\})
is tilting.
Such an indecomposable
R
is then called a
complement
for
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
.

Theorem 4.26
.

Assume that
\mathscr{C}
is a 0-Auslander extriangulated category.
Let
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
be an almost complete rigid subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
(containing all projective-injectives).
We make the following assumptions:

•

Any projective object in
\mathscr{C}
has a left
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
-approximation and any injective object has a right
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
-approximation.

•

Any projective object in the reduction
\mathscr{C}_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}
of
\mathscr{C}
by
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
has a left
\operatorname{add}(X)
-approximation if
X
is any complement for
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
.

•

The category
\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}
is Krull–Schmidt.

Then there exist, up to isomorphism, precisely two complements for
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
.

Remark 4.27
.

The second assumption is satisfied for example if
\mathscr{C}/[\mathcal{P}\cap\mathcal{I}]
is Hom-finite, and the first one is if moreover
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
only has finitely many indecomposables up to isomorphism.
We also note that the second assumption is equivalent to the dual assumption on injective objects.

Proof.

Replacing
\mathscr{C}
by
\mathscr{C}/[\mathcal{P}\cap\mathcal{I}]
, we may assume that
\mathscr{C}
is reduced 0-Auslander.
Let
(\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}},\overline{\mathbb{E}})
be the reduction of
\mathscr{C}
at
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
.
Let
I
be an indecomposable injective in
\mathscr{C}
that does not belong to
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
(such an injective exists otherwise
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
would be cotilting).
Fix an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
P\rightarrowtail 0\twoheadrightarrow I\dashrightarrow
, where
P
is thus projective.
By assumption,
P
has a left
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
-approximation and
I
has a right
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
-approximation.
We can thus apply
Corollary

4.8
,
Lemma

4.9
and their duals, to obtain
\mathfrak{s}
-triangles
Q\rightarrowtail R\twoheadrightarrow I\overset{\varepsilon}{\dashrightarrow}
and
P\rightarrowtail R^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow J\dashrightarrow
in
\mathscr{C}
where
R,R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
,
\operatorname{add}(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\cup\{Q\})
and
\operatorname{add}(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\cup\{J\})
are rigid and where
Q
is projective in
\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}
and
J
is injective in
\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}
.
Pulling-back
\varepsilon
along the deflation
0\twoheadrightarrow I
gives an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
P\rightarrowtail Q\twoheadrightarrow R\dashrightarrow
and axiom (ET4) gives a diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Q}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R^{\prime}\oplus R}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{J}}

in
\mathscr{C}
(where we have used rigidity of
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
), hence an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
Q\rightarrowtail 0\twoheadrightarrow J\dashrightarrow
in
\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}
.
Moreover,
I\notin\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
implies
J\neq 0
and thus
Q\neq 0
by
Remark

4.18
(2).

We first show that
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
has at most two complements.
Let
X
be a complement for
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
.
In particular,
X
is rigid in
\mathscr{C}
, hence also in
\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}
.

By the beginning of the proof,
Q\to 0
is an inflation in
\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}
.
Thus any left
\operatorname{add}(X)
-approximation
Q\xrightarrow{i}X_{0}
in
\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}
is an inflation and there is an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle

Q\overset{i}{\rightarrowtail}X_{0}\twoheadrightarrow Y\dashrightarrow
in
\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}
, where
i
can be assumed left minimal since
\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}
is Krull–Schmidt.
It induces an exact sequence
\overline{\mathbb{E}}(X,X_{0})\to\overline{\mathbb{E}}(X,Y)\to\overline{\mathbb{E}}^{2}(X,Q)
, whose first term vanishes since
X
is rigid and whose third term also vanishes since
\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}
is hereditary by
Proposition

2.9
.
There is also an exact sequence
\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}(X_{0},X)\to\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}(Q,X)\to\overline{\mathbb{E}}(Y,X)\to\overline{\mathbb{E}}(X_{0},X)
where the first map is surjective since
i
is a left approximation and where the fourth term is zero since
X
is rigid.
This shows that
\overline{\mathbb{E}}(X,Y)=0=\overline{\mathbb{E}}(Y,X)
.
Because
\operatorname{add}(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\cup\{X\})
is
\mathbb{E}
-tilting in
\mathscr{C}
,
X
is
\overline{\mathbb{E}}
-tilting by
Lemma

4.16
, hence
Y
belongs to
\operatorname{add}X
.
By
Lemma

4.23
we have
X_{0}=0
or
Y=0
, but not both (because
Q
is non-zero).
If
Y=0
, then
X_{0}
is isomorphic to
Q
.
Since
X
is indecomposable, we have
X\in\operatorname{add}X_{0}
and thus
X
is projective.
By
Proposition

4.17
,
X
is the unique indecomposable projective object in
\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}
.
If
X_{0}=0
, then
Proposition

4.19
shows that
Y
, hence
X
, is injective.
The dual of
Proposition

4.17
implies that
X
is the unique indecomposable injective object in
\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}
.
Thus
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
has at most two complements.

We next show that
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
has at least two complements.
Note that
Corollary

4.20
shows that
Q
and
J
cannot be isomorphic (otherwise both would be zero and
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
would already be tilting).
The previous part of the proof shows that
J
is of the form
I_{0}^{\oplus r}
where
I_{0}
is the unique indecomposable injective object in
\overline{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathcal{R}^{\prime}}
.
The proof applies to any indecomposable injective
I\in\mathscr{C}
that does not belong to
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
.
Hence
Corollary

4.8
implies that
\operatorname{add}(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\cup\{Q\})
is cotilting so that
Q
is a complement for
\mathcal{R}
.
Similarly,
J
is also a complement.
Therefore,
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
has at least two complements.
∎

Under the same assumptions on
\mathscr{C}
as in
Theorem

4.26
we obtain the following.

Corollary 4.28
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
be a silting subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
,
R\in\mathcal{R}
be any non projective-injective indecomposable object, and
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}=\operatorname{add}\{S\in\mathcal{R},S\text{ \emph{indecomposable and} }S\ncong R\}
.
Then there exists an indecomposable object
R^{\ast}
, unique up to isomorphism, such that
R^{\ast}
is not isomorphic to
R
and

\operatorname{add}\left(\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\cup\{R^{\ast}\}\right)

is silting.
Moreover, there is an exchange
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
R\rightarrowtail R^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow R^{\ast}\dashrightarrow
with
R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}

or an exchange
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
R^{\ast}\rightarrowtail R^{\prime\prime}\twoheadrightarrow R\dashrightarrow
with
R^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
, but not both at the same time.

Applying
Corollary

4.28
to the specific examples listed in
Section

3.3
, we recover:

•

Cluster-tilting theory
[
BMR+06
,
IY08
]
.

•

2-term silting mutation
[
Kim20
]
.

•

Relative tilting theory
[
YZ19
]
.

•

Mutation of intermediate co-
t
-structures
[
AI12
,
BY13
,
KY14
]
(see
Section

5
).

•

Via bijections in
[
PZ20
]
and in
[
LZ20
]
, we recover mutations of functorially finite torsion classes and support
\tau
-tilting subcategories in
\tau
-tilting theory (see
Remark

5.15
).

•

Combinatorics of walks and non-kissing
[
McC17
,
BDM+20
,
PPP21
]
(see
Section

6
).

•

Mutation of maximal almost rigid modules in type A
[
BGMS23
]
.

5.
Tilting subcategories, cotorsion pairs and intermediate co-
t
-structures

In this section, we apply
Theorem

4.26
to extended cohearts of co-
t
-structures.
As a consequence, there is a well-behaved notion of mutation for intermediate co-
t
-structures extending the previously known one from the bounded case.

5.1.
Cotorsion pairs in 0-Auslander extriangulated categories

Let
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
be a 0-Auslander extriangulated category. Recall the following

Definition 5.1
.

A complete cotorsion pair in an extriangulated category
\mathscr{C}
is a pair
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
of (strictly) full subcategories closed under direct summands and satisfying:

(a)

\mathcal{X}\perp_{\mathbb{E}}\mathcal{Y}
: for any
X\in\mathcal{X}
and any
Y\in\mathcal{Y}
, we have
\mathbb{E}(X,Y)=0
.

(b)

\mathscr{C}=\operatorname{Cone}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{X})
: for any
C\in\mathscr{C}
, there is an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
Y\rightarrowtail X\twoheadrightarrow C\dashrightarrow
with
X\in\mathcal{X}
and
Y\in\mathcal{Y}
.

(c)

\mathscr{C}=\operatorname{Fib}(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{X})
: for any
C\in\mathscr{C}
, there is an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
C\rightarrowtail Y\twoheadrightarrow X\dashrightarrow
with
X\in\mathcal{X}
and
Y\in\mathcal{Y}
.

We note that it follows from the definition that, if
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
is a complete cotorsion pair, then
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{X}^{\perp_{\mathbb{E}}}
and
\mathcal{X}=\,^{\perp_{\mathbb{E}}}\mathcal{Y}
.
It follows that:

•

\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
uniquely determine each other,

•

both
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
are extension-closed,

•

\mathrm{Proj}_{(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E})}\subseteq\mathcal{X}
and
\mathrm{Inj}_{(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E})}\subseteq\mathcal{Y}
,

•

if
\mathscr{C}
is hereditary, in any
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle

Y\rightarrowtail X\twoheadrightarrow C\dashrightarrow

with
X\in\mathcal{X}
and
Y\in\mathcal{Y}
, we have
Y\in\mathcal{X}\cap\mathcal{Y}
; and
in any
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle

C\rightarrowtail Y\twoheadrightarrow X\dashrightarrow

with
X\in\mathcal{X}
and
Y\in\mathcal{Y}
, we have
X\in\mathcal{X}\cap\mathcal{Y}
.

In order to apply
Theorem

4.26
to intermediate co-
t
-structures, we will need the following result (which is a specific case of
[
AT22
, Theorem 5.7]
):

Theorem 5.2

(Adachi–Tsukamoto)
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a 0-Auslander extriangulated category.
Write
\operatorname{cCP}(\mathscr{C})
for the class of all complete cotorsion pairs in
\mathscr{C}
, and
\operatorname{Tilt}(\mathscr{C})
for the class of all tilting subcategories of
\mathscr{C}
.
There are mutually inverse bijections

\displaystyle\varphi_{1}:\operatorname{cCP}(\mathscr{C})

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\operatorname{Tilt}(\mathscr{C}):\psi_{1},

where

\varphi_{1}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})=\mathcal{X}\cap\mathcal{Y}\text{ and}

\psi_{1}(\mathcal{R})=(^{\perp_{\mathbb{E}}}\mathcal{R},\mathcal{R}^{\perp_{\mathbb{E}}})=(\operatorname{copr}\mathcal{R},\operatorname{pr}\mathcal{R}).

Proof.

This is a specific case of
[
AT22
, Theorem 5.7]
.
For sake of completeness, we include a proof, which becomes quite easy in our specific setting.

\bullet
The map
\varphi_{1}
is well-defined: Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
be a complete cotorsion pair in
\mathscr{C}
, and let
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{X}\cap\mathcal{Y}
.
We claim that
\mathcal{R}
is a tilting subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
.
By (a) in the definition of
\operatorname{cCP}(\mathscr{C})
,
\mathcal{R}
is rigid.
For any projective object
P\in\mathscr{C}
, consider an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
P\rightarrowtail Y\twoheadrightarrow X\dashrightarrow
with
X\in\mathcal{X}
and
Y\in\mathcal{Y}
.
In the exact sequence

\mathbb{E}(X,-)|_{\mathcal{Y}}\to\mathbb{E}(Y,-)|_{\mathcal{Y}}\to\mathbb{E}(P,-)|_{\mathcal{Y}},

the left-most term vanishes since
\mathcal{X}\perp_{\mathbb{E}}\mathcal{Y}
and the right-most term vanishes since
P
is projective.
Thus
\mathbb{E}(Y,-)|_{\mathcal{Y}}=0
showing that
Y\in\mathcal{X}
, hence
Y\in\mathcal{R}
.
Moreover, in the exact sequence

\mathbb{E}(-,Y)|_{\mathcal{X}}\to\mathbb{E}(-,X)|_{\mathcal{X}}\to\mathbb{E}^{2}(-,P)|_{\mathcal{X}}

the left-most term vanishes since
\mathcal{X}\perp_{\mathbb{E}}\mathcal{Y}
and the right-most term vanishes since
\mathscr{C}
has global dimension at most one.
Hence
\mathbb{E}(-,X)|_{\mathcal{X}}=0
and
X\in\mathcal{R}
.
This shows that
\mathcal{R}
is a tilting subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
.

\bullet
The map
\psi_{1}
is well-defined: Let
\mathcal{R}
be a tilting subcategory of
\mathscr{C}
and let
\mathcal{X}=\operatorname{copr}\mathcal{R}
,
\mathcal{Y}=\operatorname{pr}\mathcal{R}
.
By
Theorem

4.3
,
\mathcal{R}
is both strongly torsion projective and strongly cotorsion injective.
We thus have
{}^{\perp_{\mathbb{E}}}\mathcal{R}=\operatorname{copr}\mathcal{R}
and
\mathcal{R}^{\perp_{\mathbb{E}}}=\operatorname{pr}\mathcal{R}
.
We now check that
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
satisfies (a) (b) and (c) in
Definition

5.1
.

(a) For any
X\in{{}^{\perp_{\mathbb{E}}}\mathcal{R}}
, and any
Y\in\operatorname{pr}\mathcal{R}
, there is an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
R_{1}\to R_{0}\to Y\dashrightarrow
, with
R_{0},R_{1}\in\mathcal{R}
.
In the exact sequence

\mathbb{E}(X,R_{0})\to\mathbb{E}(X,Y)\to\mathbb{E}^{2}(X,R_{1}),

both end-terms vanish, hence
\mathcal{X}\perp_{\mathbb{E}}\mathcal{Y}
.

(b) We prove that
\mathscr{C}=\operatorname{Cone}(\mathcal{R},\operatorname{copr}\mathcal{R})
: For any
C\in\mathscr{C}
, there is an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
P_{1}\rightarrowtail P_{0}\twoheadrightarrow C\dashrightarrow
with
P_{0},P_{1}
projective in
\mathscr{C}
.
Since
\mathcal{R}
is tilting, there are
\mathfrak{s}
-triangles
P_{1}\rightarrowtail R_{0}\twoheadrightarrow R_{1}\dashrightarrow
and
P_{0}\rightarrowtail R^{\prime}_{0}\twoheadrightarrow R^{\prime}_{1}\dashrightarrow
with
R_{0},R_{1},R^{\prime}_{0},R^{\prime}_{1}\in\mathcal{R}
.
Applying
Lemma

A.10
twice, we obtain the following diagrams of morphisms of
\mathfrak{s}
-triangles

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R^{\prime}_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R^{\prime}_{0}\oplus R_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R^{\prime}_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{R^{\prime}_{1}}}

showing that
C
belongs to
\operatorname{Cone}(\mathcal{R},\operatorname{copr}\mathcal{R})
.

(c) We have
\mathscr{C}=\operatorname{Fib}(\operatorname{pr}\mathcal{R},\mathcal{R})
: This is dual to (b) and left to the reader.

\bullet
We have
\varphi_{1}\circ\psi_{1}=1
: By
Theorem

4.3
, any tilting subcategory
\mathcal{R}
is
\mathbb{E}
-tilting, hence
\mathcal{R}^{\perp_{\mathbb{E}}}\cap\,\!^{\perp_{\mathbb{E}}}\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}
.

\bullet
We have
\psi_{1}\circ\varphi_{1}=1
: Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
be a complete cotorsion pair.
We have to show that
{}^{\perp_{\mathbb{E}}}(\mathcal{X}\cap\mathcal{Y})=\,\!^{\perp_{\mathbb{E}}}\mathcal{Y}
and that
(\mathcal{X}\cap\mathcal{Y})^{\perp_{\mathbb{E}}}=\mathcal{X}^{\perp_{\mathbb{E}}}
.
We only prove the first equality, the second one being dual.
Let
U\in\,\!^{\perp_{\mathbb{E}}}(\mathcal{X}\cap\mathcal{Y})
and let
Y\in\mathcal{Y}
.
There is an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
Y^{\prime}\rightarrowtail X^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow Y\dashrightarrow
with
X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
and
Y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}
.
Because
\mathcal{Y}
is extension-closed in
\mathscr{C}
, we have
X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}\cap\mathcal{Y}
, which implies that, in the exact sequence

\mathbb{E}(U,X^{\prime})\to\mathbb{E}(U,Y)\to\mathbb{E}^{2}(U,Y^{\prime})

both end terms vanish.
Thus
\mathbb{E}(U,Y)=0
.
∎

5.2.
Extended cohearts and mutation of intermediate co-
t
-structures

In this section, we let
\mathscr{D}
be a triangulated category. It is not assumed to be essentially small or
R
-linear except when stated otherwise.
We first recall the necessary definitions and one of the main results in
[
PZ20
]
.

Definition 5.3

(
[
Pau08
,
Bon10
]
)
.

A
co-
t
-structure
, or a
weight structure
, on
\mathscr{D}
is a pair
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
of full subcategories, closed under summands, such that:

(i)

\mathcal{A}\perp\mathcal{B}
: for any
A\in\mathcal{A}
and any
B\in\mathcal{B}
, we have
\mathscr{D}(A,B)=0
,

(ii)

\mathscr{D}=\mathcal{A}\ast\mathcal{B}
: for any
D\in\mathscr{D}
there is a triangle
A\to D\to B\to\Sigma A
, with
A\in\mathcal{A}
and
B\in\mathcal{B}
.

(iii)

\mathcal{A}\subseteq\Sigma\mathcal{A}
(or equivalently,
\Sigma\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{B}
).

A co-t-structure
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
is
bounded
if

\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\Sigma^{i}\mathcal{A}=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\Sigma^{i}\mathcal{B}=\mathscr{D}.

If
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
is a co-
t
-structure, its
coheart
is the full subcategory
\mathcal{S}=(\Sigma\mathcal{A})\cap\mathcal{B}
, and its
extended coheart
is the full subcategory
\mathscr{C}=\mathcal{S}\ast\Sigma\mathcal{S}=\Sigma^{2}\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{B}
.

As for cotorsion pairs, the subcategories
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
forming a co-
t
-structure determine each other and are closed under extensions (see the comments after
Definition

5.1
), hence
\mathscr{C}
is extension-closed in
\mathscr{D}
and inherits an extriangulated structure.

Write
\operatorname{Co-}t(\mathscr{D})
for the collection of all co-
t
-structures on
\mathscr{D}
.
If
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
is a fixed co-
t
-structure on
\mathscr{D}
, write

\operatorname{Co-}t_{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})}(\mathscr{D})
for the collection of
intermediate
co-
t
-structures:

\operatorname{Co-}t_{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})}(\mathscr{D})=\{(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime})\in\operatorname{Co-}t(\mathscr{D})\;|\;\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\subseteq\Sigma\mathcal{A}\}.

We note that, if
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
and
(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
are co-
t
-structures, the condition
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\subseteq\Sigma\mathcal{A}
is equivalent to the condition
\Sigma\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{B}
. Also, we have both
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
if and only if the co-
t
-structures coincide.

Remark 5.4
.

A coheart of a co-
t
-structure on
\mathscr{D}
is a weakly idempotent complete additive category, which is not necessarily idempotent complete or abelian (see
[
BV20
]
for the details).
It is always a presilting subcategory of
\mathscr{D}
. It is silting in
\mathscr{D}
if and only if the co-
t
-structure is bounded; moreover, there is a bijection between silting subcategories and bounded co-
t
-structures, see
[
MSSSS13
, Corollary 5.9]
. Each co-
t
-structure restricts to a bounded co-
t
-structure on
\mathrm{thick}(\mathcal{S})
, and this is the largest thick subcategory of
\mathscr{D}
which admits a bounded co-
t
-structure restricted from a given one. A co-
t
-structure is bounded if and only if each its intermediate co-
t
-structure is bounded, since we have
\mathrm{thick}(\mathcal{S}^{\prime})=\mathrm{thick}(\mathcal{S})
for each intermediate co-
t
-structure with coheart
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
, or, equivalently, the coheart of each intermediate co-
t
-structure is silting in
\mathrm{thick}(\mathcal{S})
.

Theorem 5.5

(Pauksztello–Zvonareva
[
PZ20
]
)
.

Let
\mathscr{D}
be a triangulated category and let
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
be a co-
t
-structure on
\mathcal{D}
.
There are mutually inverse bijective correspondences

\displaystyle\varphi_{2}:\operatorname{Co-}t_{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})}(\mathscr{D})

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\operatorname{cCP}(\mathscr{C}):\psi_{2},

where

\varphi_{2}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime})=(\mathcal{B}\cap\Sigma\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\cap\Sigma^{2}\mathcal{A})\text{ and}

\psi_{2}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})=\left(\operatorname{add}(\Sigma^{-1}\mathcal{A}\ast\Sigma^{-1}\mathcal{X}),\operatorname{add}(\mathcal{Y}\ast\Sigma^{2}\mathcal{B})\right).

Remark 5.6
.

The bijective correspondence
\varphi_{2}
moreover sends cohearts to cores: with the notations of the theorem, let
(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime})\in\operatorname{Co-}t_{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})}(\mathscr{D})
and let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})=\varphi_{2}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime})
. Then
\mathcal{X}\cap\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\Sigma\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
.

Note that this result has the following nice corollary. It might be known to experts, but we were not able to find this statement in the literature.

Corollary 5.7
.

Let
\mathscr{D}
be a triangulated category and let
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
be a co-
t
-structure on
\mathcal{D}
with coheart
\mathcal{S}
. Then for each co-
t
-structure
(\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime})
on
\mathscr{T}:=\mathrm{thick}(\mathcal{S})
intermediate with respect to the restriction
(\mathcal{A}\cap\mathscr{T},\mathcal{B}\cap\mathscr{T})
of
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
, there exists a unique co-
t
-structure
(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime})\in\operatorname{Co-}t_{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})}(\mathscr{D})
extending
(\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime})
.

Proof.

Note that
\mathscr{C}\subset\mathscr{T}
.
Therefore, both collections
\operatorname{Co-}t_{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})}(\mathscr{D})
and
\operatorname{Co-}t_{(\mathcal{A}\cap\mathscr{T},\mathcal{B}\cap\mathscr{T})}(\mathscr{T})
are in bijective correspondences with
\operatorname{cCP}(\mathscr{C})
, hence they are themselves in a bijective correspondence. It remains to show that the bijection
\operatorname{Co-}t_{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})}(\mathscr{D})\to\operatorname{Co-}t_{(\mathcal{A}\cap\mathscr{T},\mathcal{B}\cap\mathscr{T})}(\mathscr{T})
is given by the restriction. Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y})
be the cotorsion pair corresponding to
(\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime})
. Then

(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime}):=\left(\operatorname{add}(\Sigma^{-1}\mathcal{A}\ast\Sigma^{-1}\mathcal{X}),\operatorname{add}(\mathcal{Y}\ast\Sigma^{2}\mathcal{B})\right)

is the corresponding co-
t
-structure on
\mathcal{D}
, while we have

(\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime})=\left(\operatorname{add}(\Sigma^{-1}(\mathcal{A}\cap\mathscr{T})\ast\Sigma^{-1}\mathcal{X}),\operatorname{add}(\mathcal{Y}\ast\Sigma^{2}(\mathcal{B}\cap\mathscr{T}))\right).

Observe that we have
\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime}\subset\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\cap\mathscr{T}
and
\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime}\subset\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\cap\mathscr{T}
. Thus,
(\mathcal{A}^{\prime\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime\prime})=(\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\cap\mathscr{T},\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\cap\mathscr{T})
.
∎

Remark 5.8
.

Note that
Corollary

5.7
ensures the existence and uniqueness of extensions of co-t-structures intermediate with respect to the restriction
(\mathcal{A}\cap\mathscr{T},\mathcal{B}\cap\mathscr{T})
, to co-t-structures intermediate with respect to
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
. A similar result is true for co-t-structures “intermediate” in a broader sense, i.e. such that
\Sigma^{a}\mathcal{A}\subseteq\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\subseteq\Sigma^{b}\mathcal{A}
for finite
a<b
. This follows from
[
AT22
, Corollary 3.9]
applied twice as in the proof of
Corollary

5.7
.
[
AT22
, Corollary 3.9]

generalizes
Theorem

5.5
to suitable

(b-a)
-Auslander extriangulated categories.

The “intermediate” assumptions, however, cannot be dropped completely. Here is an elementary explanation: each non-zero triangulated category
\mathscr{D}
admits at least two distinct co-t-structures
(0,\mathscr{D})
and
(\mathscr{D},0)
. For either of them, the coheart is the zero subcategory. They are both unbounded and the largest thick subcategory which admits a bounded co-t-structure restricted from either of them is again the zero subcategory
0=\mathrm{thick}(0)
. The restricted co-t-structures, of course, both equal
(0,0)
. In other words, the co-t-structure
(0,0)
on the zero subcategory admits two distinct extensions to
\mathscr{D}
. We do not get a contradiction with
Corollary

5.7
precisely because
(0,\mathscr{D})
is not intermediate with respect to
(\mathscr{D},0)
and vice versa.

Note also that there exists a similar result for t-structures with finite distance from a given one, see
[
CLZ22
]
and references to Keller
[
Kel05
]
and Marks-Zvonareva
[
MZ22
]
therein.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
\mathscr{D}
be an essentially small
R
-linear triangulated category and let
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
be a co-
t
-structure with associated coheart
\mathcal{S}
and extended coheart
\mathscr{C}
.
Then the extriangulated category
\mathscr{C}
is reduced 0-Auslander, with projective objects
\mathcal{S}
and injective objects
\Sigma\mathcal{S}
.

Proof.

Since
\mathscr{C}=\mathcal{S}\ast\Sigma\mathcal{S}
, it is enough to show that
\mathcal{S}
is projective in
\mathscr{C}
.
Let
S\in\mathcal{S}
and
C\in\mathscr{C}
.
Then
S
belongs to
\Sigma\mathcal{A}\cap\mathcal{B}
while
\Sigma C
lies in
\Sigma^{3}\mathcal{A}\cap\Sigma\mathcal{B}
.
Because
\Sigma\mathcal{A}
is left Hom-orthogonal to
\Sigma\mathcal{B}
, we have
\mathscr{D}(S,\Sigma C)=0
.
∎

Corollary 5.10
.

Let
\mathscr{D}
be an essentially small
R
-linear triangulated category and let
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
be a co-
t
-structure with associated extended coheart
\mathscr{C}
. There are mutually inverse bijective correspondences

\displaystyle\varphi_{3}:\operatorname{Co-}t_{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})}(\mathscr{D})

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\operatorname{Tilt}(\mathscr{C}):\psi_{3},

given by

\varphi_{3}(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime})=\Sigma\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\text{ and}

\psi_{3}(\mathcal{R})=(\left(\operatorname{add}(\Sigma^{-1}\mathcal{A}\ast\Sigma^{-1}(\operatorname{copr}\mathcal{R}),\operatorname{add}((\operatorname{pr}\mathcal{R})\ast\Sigma^{2}\mathcal{B})\right),

where
\operatorname{pr}\mathcal{R},\operatorname{copr}\mathcal{R}
are considered in
\mathscr{C}
.

Proof.

Combine
Lemma

5.9
with
Theorems

5.2
and
5.5
.
∎

Corollary 5.11
.

[Mutation of intermediate co-
t
-structures]

Let
\mathscr{D}
be an essentially small
R
-linear triangulated category and let
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
be a co-
t
-structure with extended coheart
\mathscr{C}
.
Let
(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{B}^{\prime})\in\operatorname{Co-}t_{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})}(\mathscr{D})
with coheart
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
.
Assume that the category
\mathscr{C}
satisfies the assumptions of
Theorem

4.26
and let
X\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
be indecomposable.
Then there is a unique intermediate co-
t
-structure
(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})\in\operatorname{Co-}t_{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})}(\mathscr{D})
whose coheart
\mathcal{T}
satisfies
\mathcal{T}\cap\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=\operatorname{add}\{X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime},X^{\prime}\text{ \emph{indecomposable and} }X^{\prime}\ncong X\}
.
Moreover, there is an indecomposable object
Y\in\mathcal{T}
such that

\operatorname{add}\{Y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T},Y^{\prime}\text{ \emph{indecomposable and} }Y^{\prime}\ncong Y\}=\operatorname{add}\{X^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime},X^{\prime}\text{ \emph{indecomposable and} }X^{\prime}\ncong X\}.

Proof.

Combine
Theorem

4.26
,
Lemma

5.9
and
Corollary

5.10
.
∎

Remark 5.12
.

We note that the assumptions of
Theorem

4.26
are satisfied for example if
\mathscr{C}
is Krull–Schmidt and any almost complete rigid subcategory is functorially finite in
\mathscr{C}
, as in Condition (F) in
[
AI12
, Secion 2.4]
. Note though that our assumptions concern the subcategory
\mathscr{C}
and not the entire category
\mathscr{D}
which might have no silting subcategories.

The theory of mutations of bounded co-
t
-structures is implicit in
[
AI12
]
and stated explicitly in certain generality in
[
KY14
]
, see also
[
Jør18
, Section 6]
. It goes through the bijection of bounded co-
t
-structures on
\mathscr{D}
and silting subcategories in
\mathscr{D}
mentioned in
Remark

5.4
and silting mutations from
[
AI12
]
.
Mutations of co-
t
-structures, intermediate with respect to a certain bounded co-
t
-structure (and so also bounded), are considered in
[
BY13
, Corollary 4.3]
. It follows from the results in this section that mutations of intermediate co-
t
-structures can be canonically defined via mutations of silting subcategories in the extended coheart of the original co-
t
-structure, which do not have to be silting in the entire
\mathscr{D}
.

Proposition 5.13
.

(i)

Mutations in
Corollary

5.11
recover known mutations of co-
t
-structures, intermediate with respect to a bounded one.

(ii)

Mutations in
Corollary

5.11
commute with the restriction to and extension from
\mathrm{thick}(\mathcal{S}).

Proof.

Part (i) follows from part (ii), which in turn is direct consequence of
Corollary

5.7
and its proof. ∎

Remark 5.14
.

The upshot of
Corollary

5.11
and
Proposition

5.13
is that
the theory of mutation for bounded co-t-structures implies that for non-bounded ones by restricting to and extending from thick subcategories. The fact that the extension of a mutated restriction exists and is unique follows from
Corollary

5.7
and seems to be novel. See also the discussion in
Remark

5.8
.

Remark 5.15
.

Another result of Pauksztello–Zvonareva
[
PZ20
, Theorem 3.6]
provides a bijection between complete cotorsion pairs in extended cohearts
\mathcal{S}\ast\Sigma\mathcal{S}
of co-
t
-structures and functorially finite torsion classes in
\operatorname{mod}\mathcal{S}
, under certain natural conditions. By adapting the proofs in
[
PZ20
, Section 3]
to the setting of
\mathrm{Hom}
-finite Krull-Schmidt 0-Auslander categories with noetherian
\operatorname{mod}\overline{\mathcal{P}}
and using our
Proposition

3.11
, we can obtain a bijection between complete cotorsion pairs in such 0-Auslander categories and functorially finite torsion classes in
\operatorname{mod}\overline{\mathcal{P}}
. A similar bijection was given in
[
LZ20
]
for a certain class of 0-Auslander subcategories (defined in terms of 2-rigid subcategories
\mathcal{W}
with
\mathcal{I}\subsetneq\mathcal{W}
) in extriangulated categories; there, support
\tau
-tilting subcategories were considered instead of functorially finite torsion classes. Via these bijections,
Theorem

4.26
recovers mutations of functorially finite torsion classes and support
\tau
-tilting subcategories in
\tau
-tilting theory
[
AIR14
,
IR14
,
Tre21
,
IJY14
]
.

Generalizing
[
PZ20
, Theorem 3.6]
, the functor inducing the equivalence in
Proposition

3.11
sends cotorsionfree classes to functorially finite torsion classes. A
maximal green sequence
for a finite-dimensional algebra
\Lambda
is a maximal chain of functorially finite torsion classes in the lattice of torsion classes in
\operatorname{mod}\Lambda
. In our setup, this motivates a (dual, in some sense) definition of
maximal green sequences for 0-Auslander categories
as a maximal chain in the poset of cotorsionfree classes in complete cotorsion pairs. We do not investigate such sequences further in this paper.

6.
0-Auslander
categories from gentle algebras

Inspired by
[
PPP21
, Figure 30]
, it was suggested in
[
INP18
]
that the
\tau
-tilting theory for gentle algebras might be viewed as a mutation theory for maximal rigid objects in a certain exact category constructed from the module category over the blossoming gentle algebra. In [loc. cit.], an explicit small example was considered. In this section, we carry out this strategy for all gentle algebras.
We define two exact subcategories of the module category over the blossoming algebra and show that they are equivalent.
The first subcategory, studied in
Section

6.2
, is algebraically more convenient, while the second (see
Section

6.3
) is more obviously related to the combinatorics of non-kissing walks
[
McC17
,
BDM+20
,
PPP21
]
.
We prove that those categories are 0-Auslander and their maximal rigid objects are in bijection with support
\tau
-tilting modules
[
AIR14
]
over the gentle algebra, respectively with non-kissing facets. Under this identification, straight walks bijectively correspond to indecomposable projective-injective objects. The ideal quotients of these categories by the projective-injective objects are no longer exact, but are reduced 0-Auslander extriangulated categories.
The mutation theory developed in
Section

4
thus recovers
\tau
-tilting mutation for gentle algebras, and flips of non-kissing facets.

6.1.
Reminder on gentle algebras and non-kissing

Fix an algebraically closed field
K
.
If
\alpha
and
\beta
are two arrows in a quiver with
t(\alpha)=s(\beta)
, we write
\alpha\beta
for their composition.

6.1.1.
Gentle bound quivers

Definition 6.1
.

[
BR87
]

A gentle algebra is a basic, finite-dimensional algebra
A=KQ/I
where:

•

Each vertex in
Q
is the source of at most two arrows and the target of at most two arrows.

•

The ideal
I
is generated by paths of length two.

•

For each arrow
\alpha
in
Q
with target vertex
i
, there is at most one arrow
\beta
and at most one arrow
\gamma
with source
i
, such that
\alpha\beta\notin I
and
\alpha\gamma\in I
.

•

For each arrow
\alpha
in
Q
with source vertex
j
, there is at most one arrow
\beta
and at most one arrow
\gamma
with target
j
, such that
\beta\alpha\notin I
and
\gamma\alpha\in I
.

6.1.2.
String and band modules

Indecomposable representations of a gentle bound quiver
(Q,I)
are parameterized by strings and bands.
A string for
(Q,I)
is a non-oriented path in
Q
, written as a word
\alpha_{1}^{\pm 1}\cdots\alpha_{r}^{\pm 1},
where:

•

for every subpath
\alpha_{i}\alpha_{i+1},
we have
t(\alpha_{i})=s(\alpha_{i+1})
and
\alpha_{i}\alpha_{i+1}\notin I
;

•

for every subpath
\alpha_{i}^{-1}\alpha_{i+1}^{-1},
we have
s(\alpha_{i})=t(\alpha_{i+1})
and
\alpha_{i+1}\alpha_{i}\notin I
;

•

for every subpath
\alpha_{i}\alpha_{i+1}^{-1},
or
\alpha_{i}^{-1}\alpha_{i+1}
we have
\alpha_{i}\neq\alpha_{i+1}
.

Note that strings of length 0 are allowed.
For each vertex
p\in Q_{0}
, we write
\varepsilon_{p}
for the string of length 0 at
p
, and also for the associated idempotent in
KQ/I
.
Strings are usually considered unoriented (e.g. the strings
\alpha\beta^{-1}
and
\beta\alpha^{-1}
are implicitly identified).

With each string
\sigma
, one can associate an indecomposable representation
M_{\sigma}
of
(Q,I)
: The vertices appearing in the string encode basis vectors, while the arrows show which basis vector is sent to which other basis vector under the corresponding linear map.
The representation associated with the string
\varepsilon_{p}
is the simple object concentrated at vertex
p
.

Example 6.2
.

The quiver below, with the ideal of relations generated by
\beta\epsilon
and
\delta\gamma
, is gentle.
On the right, the string
\epsilon^{-1}\delta^{-1}\alpha\beta\gamma
is drawn.:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 3}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 3}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 5}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\epsilon^{-1}\delta^{-1}\alpha\beta\gamma:}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 5}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 3}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 4}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 4}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}

The associated indecomposable representation is:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{K^{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[{}^{1}_{0}\right]}
[1 0]
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\left[{}^{0}_{1}\right]}
[0 1]

This is the indecomposable projective cover of the simple module concentrated at vertex 1.

A band is a string of length at least one, whose source and target coincide, and such that

•

its square is a string;

•

it is not the power of a smaller string.

Given a band, an element
\lambda\in K^{\times}
and a positive integer
d
, there is an associated indecomposable representation.
The vector space at each vertex appearing in the band is
K^{d}
(note that the source and target vertices of the band are identified), all linear maps are identities except for one which is given by a Jordan block of size
d
with eigenvalue
\lambda
.

All indecomposable representations of a gentle bound quiver are given by string and band modules. We refer to
[
BR87
, p.161]
for a more detailed statement.

6.1.3.
Blossoming

Let
(Q,I)
be a gentle bound quiver.
In order to simplify computations (of the Avella-Alaminos–Geiß invariant
[
AAG08
]
in
[
Asa12
]
or of the Auslander–Reiten translation in
[
BDM+20
,
PPP21
]
), it is convenient to expand
(Q,I)
by making all vertices in
Q_{0}
4-valent by adding as few arrows as possible.
The resulting gentle bound quiver is called the blossoming bound quiver of
(Q,I)
and is denoted
(Q^{\text{❀}},I^{\text{❀}})
.
It is characterised (up to isomorphism) by the fact that:

•

it is gentle;

•

its vertices are either leaves (i.e. sinks or sources) or 4-valent;

•

by deleting its leaves, one recovers
(Q,I)
.

The leaves of
Q^{\text{❀}}
are called its blossoming vertices, or simply its blossoms.

Example 6.3
.

The blossoming bound quiver of the gentle bound quiver appearing in
Example

6.2
is:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 3}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 5}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 4}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\epsilon}

We denote by
A^{\text{❀}}
the algebra
KQ^{\text{❀}}/I^{\text{❀}}.

A walk on
(Q,I)
is defined as a maximal string for
(Q^{\text{❀}},I^{\text{❀}})
.
A cohook is a string of the form
\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{r}\beta^{-1}
.
By adding left and a right cohooks to a string for
(Q,I)
, one obtains a walk on
(Q,I)
:
If
\sigma
is a string for
(Q,I)
and if
\omega
is the associated walk, then we have
M_{\omega}=\tau_{A^{\text{❀}}}M_{\sigma}
.
This yields a bijection between strings for
(Q,I)
and walks on
(Q,I)
that do not correspond to injective string modules over
(Q^{\text{❀}},I^{\text{❀}})
.
It is shown in
[
BDM+20
,
PPP21
]
that the
\tau
-rigidity of representations of
(Q,I)
can be described purely combinatorially by making use of this bijection.
The compatibility relation between walks corresponding to the
\tau
-rigidity is called non-kissing and was introduced (for grids) in
[
McC17
]
.
Maximal sets of pairwise non-kissing walks on
(Q,I)
, called non-kissing facets, are thus in bijection with isomorphism classes of basic
\tau
-tilting pairs (in the sense of
[
AIR14
]
) over
A
. By
[
McC17
,
BDM+20
,
PPP21
]
, there is a notion of flip for non-kissing facets, similar to the notion of flip for triangulations.
Under the bijection explained above, flips correspond to
\tau
-tilting mutations.

6.2.
Exact blossoming of the module category of a gentle algebra

Definition 6.4
.

Let
\mathscr{E}\subseteq\operatorname{mod}A^{\text{❀}}
be the full subcategory consisting of those
E\in\operatorname{mod}A^{\text{❀}}
that satisfy the following conditions.

(e1)

\mathrm{Hom}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(\operatorname{soc}P,E)=0
for any
P\in\operatorname{proj}A^{\text{❀}}
.

(e2)

\mathrm{pd}_{A^{\text{❀}}}E\leq 1
.

It is straightforward to check that
\mathscr{E}\subseteq\operatorname{mod}A^{\text{❀}}
is closed under isomorphisms, extensions, and direct summands.

Remark 6.5
.

For any
E\in\operatorname{mod}A^{\text{❀}}
, the following holds.

(1)

E
satisfies (e1) if and only if it satisfies
\mathrm{Hom}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(S,E)=0
for any simple projective
S\in\operatorname{mod}A^{\text{❀}}
, if and only if it does not contain any non-zero simple projective in its composition series.

(2)

If
E
satisfies (e2), then
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(E,\operatorname{Fac}J)=0
holds for any
J\in\operatorname{inj}A^{\text{❀}}
. This is true in any abelian category.

(3)

E
belongs to
\mathscr{E}
if and only if there exists a short exact sequence

(6.1)

0\to P_{1}\overset{\iota}{\longrightarrow}P_{0}\to E\to 0

in
\operatorname{mod}A^{\text{❀}}
satisfying
P_{0},P_{1}\in\operatorname{proj}A^{\text{❀}}
and
\iota(\operatorname{soc}P_{1})=\operatorname{soc}P_{0}
.

Proposition 6.6
.

The following holds.

(1)

For any
P\in\operatorname{proj}A^{\text{❀}}
, we have
P/\operatorname{soc}P\in\operatorname{proj}\mathscr{E}
.

(2)

\mathscr{E}
has enough projectives. Moreover, any
E\in\mathscr{E}
has a projective resolution of the form

0\to P_{1}/\operatorname{soc}P_{1}\to P_{0}/\operatorname{soc}P_{0}\to E\to 0.

In particular, we have
\mathrm{pd}_{\mathscr{E}}E\leq 1
.

(3)

Any object in
\operatorname{proj}\mathscr{E}
is a direct summand of an object of the form
P/\operatorname{soc}P
for some
P\in\operatorname{proj}A^{\text{❀}}
.

Proof.

(1) Remark that we have
\operatorname{soc}P\in\operatorname{proj}A^{\text{❀}}
. Thus,
P/\operatorname{soc}P\in\mathscr{E}
is a direct consequence of
Remark

6.5
(3).
From the short exact sequence
0\to\operatorname{soc}P\to P\to P/\operatorname{soc}P\to 0
and the assumption of
\mathrm{Hom}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(\operatorname{soc}P,E)=0
, we also have
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(P/\operatorname{soc}P,E)=0
for any
E\in\mathscr{E}
. This shows
P/\operatorname{soc}P\in\operatorname{proj}\mathscr{E}
.

(2) This follows from (1) and
Remark

6.5
(3).

(3) This follows immediately from (1) and (2).
∎

Lemma 6.7
.

The following holds.

(1)

If
E\in\mathscr{E}
belongs to
\operatorname{Fac}J
for some
J\in\operatorname{inj}A^{\text{❀}}
, then
E\in\operatorname{inj}\mathscr{E}
.

(2)

If a short exact sequence in
\operatorname{mod}A^{\text{❀}}

0\to P\overset{\iota}{\longrightarrow}H\to I\to 0

satisfies
P\in\operatorname{proj}A^{\text{❀}}
,
H\in(\operatorname{proj}A^{\text{❀}})\cap(\operatorname{inj}A^{\text{❀}})
and
\iota(\operatorname{soc}P)=\operatorname{soc}H
, then we have
I\in\operatorname{inj}\mathscr{E}
.

(3)

For any
P\in\operatorname{proj}A^{\text{❀}}
, there exists a short exact sequence
0\to P\overset{\iota}{\longrightarrow}H\to I\to 0
in
\operatorname{mod}A^{\text{❀}}
satisfying the conditions in
(2)
. Remark that this also induces the following short exact sequence in
\mathscr{E}
,

0\to P/\operatorname{soc}P\overset{\iota}{\longrightarrow}H/\operatorname{soc}H\to I\to 0

in which we have
P/\operatorname{soc}P\in\operatorname{proj}\mathscr{E}
,
H/\operatorname{soc}H\in(\operatorname{proj}\mathscr{E})\cap(\operatorname{inj}\mathscr{E})
, and
I\in\operatorname{inj}\mathscr{E}
.

Proof.

(1) This follows from
Remark

6.5
(2).

(2) This follows from (1) and
Remark

6.5
(3).

(3) Since
\operatorname{soc}P\in\operatorname{proj}A^{\text{❀}}
, we just have to take an injective hull
P\overset{\iota}{\longrightarrow}H
and put
I=\operatorname{Cok}\iota
.
∎

Corollary 6.8
.

The category
\mathscr{E}
is
0-
Auslander.

Proof.

This is a combination of
Proposition

6.6
.(2) and
Lemma

6.7
.(3): these state that
\mathrm{pd}(\mathscr{E})\leq 1
and that
\mathrm{dom.dim}(\mathscr{E})\geq 1,
respectively.
∎

By
Proposition

3.6
, we find that
\mathscr{E}
also has enough injectives and that
\mathrm{id}(\mathscr{E},\mathbb{E})\leq 1\leq\mathrm{codom.dim}(\mathscr{E})
. For the sake of completeness, we describe the injectives and write this more explicitly.

Proposition 6.9
.

The following holds.

(1)

For any
E\in\mathscr{E}
, there exists
H\in(\operatorname{proj}A^{\text{❀}})\cap(\operatorname{inj}A^{\text{❀}})
and a short exact sequence

(6.2)

0\to E\to I^{0}\to I^{1}\to 0

satisfying
I^{0},I^{1}\in\mathscr{E}\cap\operatorname{Fac}H
.

(2)

\mathscr{E}
has enough injectives. More precisely, for any
E\in\mathscr{E}
, the sequence
(\ref{InjResolE})
gives an injective resolution of
E
in
\mathscr{E}
. In particular, we have
\mathrm{id}_{\mathscr{E}}E\leq 1
.

(3)

Any object in
\operatorname{inj}\mathscr{E}
belongs to
\mathscr{E}\cap\operatorname{Fac}H
for some
H\in(\operatorname{proj}A^{\text{❀}})\cap(\operatorname{inj}A^{\text{❀}})
. Moreover for any
I\in\operatorname{inj}\mathscr{E}
, there is a short exact sequence in
\operatorname{mod}A^{\text{❀}}

0\to P\overset{\iota}{\longrightarrow}H\to I\to 0

satisfying conditions in
Lemma

6.7

(2)
. Remark that this also induces the following short exact sequence in
\mathscr{E}
,

0\to P/\operatorname{soc}P\overset{\iota}{\longrightarrow}H/\operatorname{soc}H\to I\to 0

in which we have
P/\operatorname{soc}P\in\operatorname{proj}\mathscr{E}
,
H/\operatorname{soc}H\in(\operatorname{proj}\mathscr{E})\cap(\operatorname{inj}\mathscr{E})
, and
I\in\operatorname{inj}\mathscr{E}
.

Proof.

(1) By
Remark

6.5
(3) and
Lemma

6.7
(3), we have short exact sequences in
\operatorname{mod}A^{\text{❀}}

0\to P_{1}\overset{\iota_{1}}{\longrightarrow}P_{0}\to E\to 0,\quad 0\to P_{0}\overset{\iota_{2}}{\longrightarrow}H\to I^{1}\to 0

satisfying
P_{0},P_{1}\in\operatorname{proj}A^{\text{❀}}
,
H\in(\operatorname{proj}A^{\text{❀}})\cap(\operatorname{inj}A^{\text{❀}})
, and

\iota_{1}(\operatorname{soc}P_{1})=\operatorname{soc}P_{0},\quad\iota_{2}(\operatorname{soc}P_{0})=\operatorname{soc}H.

Thus we obtain the following commutative diagram in
\operatorname{mod}A^{\text{❀}}
for some
I^{0}\in\operatorname{mod}A^{\text{❀}}
,

\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{P_{1}}
\textstyle{P_{1}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{P_{0}}
\textstyle{H}
\textstyle{I^{1}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{E}
\textstyle{I^{0}}
\textstyle{I^{1}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

in which rows and columns are exact.
Remark

6.5
(3) shows
I^{0},I^{1}\in\mathscr{E}
.

(2) This follows from (1), since
Lemma

6.7
(2) shows
I^{0},I^{1}\in\operatorname{inj}\mathscr{E}
.

(3) By (2), any
I\in\operatorname{inj}\mathscr{E}
is a direct summand of an object in
\mathscr{E}\cap\operatorname{Fac}H
for some
H\in(\operatorname{proj}A^{\text{❀}})\cap(\operatorname{inj}A^{\text{❀}})
. Since
\mathscr{E}\cap\operatorname{Fac}H\subseteq\operatorname{mod}A^{\text{❀}}
is closed under isomorphisms and direct summands, it follows
I\in\mathscr{E}\cap\operatorname{Fac}H
.

Take an epimorphism
H\to I
in
\operatorname{mod}A^{\text{❀}}
. Since
\mathrm{pd}_{A^{\text{❀}}}I\leq 1
, it gives a short exact sequence
0\to P\overset{\iota}{\longrightarrow}H\to I\to 0
with
P\in\operatorname{proj}A^{\text{❀}}
, which necessarily satisfies
\iota(\operatorname{soc}P)=\operatorname{soc}H
since
\mathrm{Hom}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(\operatorname{soc}H,I)=0
.
∎

Let
\varepsilon_{p}\in A^{\text{❀}}
denote the idempotent element corresponding to each
p\in Q_{0}^{\text{❀}}
. Define
\varepsilon\in A^{\text{❀}}
to be

\varepsilon=\sum_{p\in Q_{0}^{\text{❀}}\setminus Q_{0}}\varepsilon_{p},

namely, the sum of idempotents corresponding to blossom vertices. Denote the two-sided ideal
A^{\text{❀}}\varepsilon A^{\text{❀}}\subseteq A^{\text{❀}}
by
\mathfrak{a}
.

Definition 6.10
.

Denote the functor tensoring with
A=A^{\text{❀}}/\mathfrak{a}
by

G\colon\operatorname{mod}A^{\text{❀}}\to\operatorname{mod}A\ ;\ M\mapsto M/\mathfrak{a}M.

The following proposition can be seen as a consequence of
Proposition

3.11
and
Corollary

6.8
; we nonetheless give a representation-theoretic proof.

Proposition 6.11
.

The functor
G
induces an equivalence of categories
\overline{G}\colon\mathscr{E}/\operatorname{inj}\mathscr{E}\overset{\simeq}{\longrightarrow}\operatorname{mod}A
.

Proof.

Since
G
obviously satisfies
G(H)=0
for any
H\in(\operatorname{proj}A^{\text{❀}})\cap(\operatorname{inj}A^{\text{❀}})
,
Proposition

6.9
(3) and the right-exactness of
G
shows
G(\operatorname{inj}\mathscr{E})=0
. Thus
G
naturally induces an additive functor
\overline{G}\colon\mathscr{E}/\operatorname{inj}\mathscr{E}\to\operatorname{mod}A
. Let us show
\overline{G}
is an equivalence of categories.

Let
E,F\in\mathscr{E}
be any pair of objects. By
Proposition

6.9
, we have a short exact sequence

0\to E\to I^{0}\to I^{1}\to 0

in
\operatorname{mod}A^{\text{❀}}
, satisfying
I^{0},I^{1}\in\operatorname{inj}\mathscr{E}
. Since
F
does not contain any non-zero simple projective in its composition series, so does
\mathfrak{a}F
. This implies that
\mathrm{top}(\mathfrak{a}F)\in\operatorname{mod}A^{\text{❀}}
is a direct sum of simples corresponding to source blossom vertices, and thus
\mathfrak{a}F
has a projective cover
H\to\mathfrak{a}F
for some
H\in(\operatorname{proj}A^{\text{❀}})\cap(\operatorname{inj}A^{\text{❀}})
. Thus by
Remark

6.5
(2), we have
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(I^{1},\mathfrak{a}F)=\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(E,\mathfrak{a}F)=0
, and obtain the following exact sequences.

0\to\mathrm{Hom}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(E,\mathfrak{a}F)\to\mathrm{Hom}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(E,F)\to\mathrm{Hom}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(E,F/\mathfrak{a}F)\to 0,

\mathrm{Hom}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(I^{0},\mathfrak{a}F)\to\mathrm{Hom}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(E,\mathfrak{a}F)\to 0.

They naturally induce the isomorphism

(\mathscr{E}/\operatorname{inj}\mathscr{E})(E,F)\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(E,F/\mathfrak{a}F)\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(E/\mathfrak{a}E,F/\mathfrak{a}F),

which shows that
\overline{G}
is fully faithful.

It remains to show that
\overline{G}
is essentially surjective.
Remark that for any
v\in Q_{0}
, we have
G(\varepsilon_{v}A^{\text{❀}})=\varepsilon_{v}A^{\text{❀}}/\operatorname{soc}(\varepsilon_{v}A^{\text{❀}})\cong\varepsilon_{v}A
. Let
M\in\operatorname{mod}A
be any object, and take its projective presentation

Q_{1}\overset{q}{\longrightarrow}Q_{0}\to M\to 0\quad(Q_{0},Q_{1}\in\operatorname{proj}A)

in
\operatorname{mod}A
. By the remark above, we may find
P_{0},P_{1}\in\operatorname{proj}A^{\text{❀}}
and epimorphisms in
\operatorname{mod}A^{\text{❀}}

\pi_{i}\colon P_{i}\to Q_{i}\quad(i=1,2)

which give
G(P_{i})\cong Q_{i}
. Here,
Q_{0},Q_{1}\in\operatorname{mod}A
are regarded as objects in
\operatorname{mod}A^{\text{❀}}
through the fully faithful embedding
\operatorname{mod}A\hookrightarrow\operatorname{mod}A^{\text{❀}}
induced by the restriction of the action along the surjective homomorphism of
K
-algebras
A^{\text{❀}}\to A^{\text{❀}}/\mathfrak{a}\cong A
. Since
P_{1}\in\operatorname{proj}A^{\text{❀}}
, we may find
p\in\mathrm{Hom}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(P_{1},P_{0})
which makes

\textstyle{P_{1}}
\textstyle{P_{0}}
\textstyle{Q_{1}}
\textstyle{Q_{0}}
\scriptstyle{p}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{q}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

commutative in
\operatorname{mod}A^{\text{❀}}
. Since
G
is right exact, this gives
G(\operatorname{Cok}p)\cong M
in
\operatorname{mod}A
.

We will modify
p\colon P_{1}\to P_{0}
to make the cokernel belong to
\mathscr{E}
.
Take an injective hull
P_{1}\overset{i}{\longrightarrow}H
, with
H\in(\operatorname{proj}A^{\text{❀}})\cap(\operatorname{inj}A^{\text{❀}})
. Then

p^{\prime}=\begin{bmatrix}p\\
i\end{bmatrix}\colon P_{1}\to P_{0}\oplus H

is a monomorphism in
\operatorname{mod}A^{\text{❀}}
. By adding the semisimple projective
S=\operatorname{soc}(P_{0}\oplus H)/p^{\prime}(\operatorname{soc}P_{1})
to
P_{1}
, we obtain a monomorphism

\iota=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}p&\ast\\
i&\ast\end{array}\right]\colon P_{1}\oplus S\to P_{0}\oplus H

which satisfies
\iota(\operatorname{soc}(P_{1}\oplus S))=\operatorname{soc}(P_{0}\oplus H)
. Since we have
G(S)=G(H)=0
, we have
G(\operatorname{Cok}\iota)\cong G(\operatorname{Cok}p)\cong M
. By
Remark

6.5
(3), we have
\operatorname{Cok}\iota\in\mathscr{E}
.
∎

6.3.
The category of walks

The exact category
\mathscr{E}
of
Section

6.2
is convenient to define and study from a purely algebraic point of view, but is not well-adapted to the combinatorics of walks (i.e. maximal strings in the blossoming bound quiver).
In this section, we exchange this subcategory for an equivalent one, which will be more adapted to walks.

Definition 6.12
.

The exact category of walks is defined as the full subcategory
\mathscr{F}
of
\operatorname{mod}A^{\text{❀}}
whose objects
M
satisfy
\mathrm{Hom}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(\operatorname{soc}P,\tau M)=0
(equivalently
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(M,\operatorname{soc}P)=0
) for any
P\in\operatorname{proj}A^{\text{❀}}
,
M
has projective dimension at most one, and has no simple projective summands.

Remark 6.13
.

The subcategory
\mathscr{F}
is extension-closed in
\operatorname{mod}A^{\text{❀}}
, and is thus an exact category.

Remark 6.14
.

The subcategory
\mathscr{F}
contains all band modules, since they are all of projective dimension one, and not supported at sinks or sources.

Example 6.15
.

In
Figure

1
, taken from
[
INP18
]
, we highlight the indecomposable
A^{\text{❀}}
-modules that belong to the subcategories
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{E}
.
Those in
\mathscr{F}
precisely coincide with the modules whose support contains two different blossoming vertices.
Moreover, we see that
\mathscr{F}
is stable under the action of
\tau_{A^{\text{❀}}}^{2}
.
Those two facts are instances of
Proposition

6.21
and
Corollary

6.26
respectively.
One can also notice that the full subquivers of this Aulsander–Reiten quiver given by
\mathscr{E}
and
\mathscr{F}
are isomorphic. This is explained by the equivalence of exact categories proven in
Proposition

6.16
.
