Figure 1
.
The Auslander–Reiten quiver of
\operatorname{mod}A^{\text{❀}}
: The indecomposable objects in
\mathscr{E}
are circled in blue; the projective (resp. injective) modules are highlighted by a red vertical line to their left (resp. to their right) and similarly for the relative projectives (resp. injectives) in
\mathscr{E}
by using dashed blue vertical lines. The indecomposable objects in
\mathscr{F}
are squared in green. Projectives (resp. injectives) in
\mathscr{F}
are the projective (resp. injective)
A^{\text{❀}}
-modules that belong to
\mathscr{F}
.

Proposition 6.16
.

There is an equivalence of exact categories
F:\mathscr{F}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathscr{E}
, sending an
A^{\text{❀}}
-module
M\in\mathscr{F}
to the cokernel
FM
of
\varepsilon.\operatorname{soc}M\to M
, where
\varepsilon
is the sum of the idempotents
e_{t}
, over all sinks
t\in Q_{0}^{\text{❀}}
.
A quasi-inverse is constructed as follows: For an
A^{\text{❀}}
-module
N\in\mathscr{E}
, consider its minimal projective resolution
0\to S_{1}\oplus P_{1}\to P_{0}\to N
, where
S_{1}
is semisimple and
P_{0},P_{1}
have no simple summands.
Then the cokernel
G^{\prime}N
of
P_{1}\to P_{0}
belongs to
\mathscr{F}
.

Proof.

The assignment
M\mapsto FM
is functorial by construction.

\bullet
For any
M\in\mathscr{F}
,
FM
belongs to
\mathscr{E}
: The short exact sequence
\varepsilon.\operatorname{soc}M\rightarrowtail M\twoheadrightarrow FM
induces, for any simple projective
S
, an exact sequence

0\to(S,\varepsilon.\operatorname{soc}M)\xrightarrow{f}(S,M)\xrightarrow{g}(S,FM)\to\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(S,\varepsilon.\operatorname{soc}M)

where we have written
(L,N)
for
\mathrm{Hom}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(L,N)
.
By definition of
\varepsilon
, the map
f
is bijective, hence
g
is zero.
Moreover,
S
being projective, we have
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(S\varepsilon.\operatorname{soc}M)=0
.
Thus
\mathrm{Hom}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(S,M)=0
.

Because
M
has projective dimension at most one, there is a short exact sequence
P_{1}\rightarrowtail P_{0}\twoheadrightarrow M
, with
P_{0},P_{1}
projective
A^{\text{❀}}
-modules.
There is a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P_{1}\oplus K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FM}

(where
K=\varepsilon.\operatorname{soc}M
) showing that
FM
also has projective dimension at most one.

\bullet
For any
N\in\mathscr{E}
,
G^{\prime}N
belongs to
\mathscr{F}
: By definition,
G^{\prime}N
has projective dimension at most one and there is a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S_{1}\oplus P_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{P_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{\prime}N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}

Because
P_{0}
has no simple summands,
G^{\prime}N
has no simple projective summands.
Moreover, for any simple projective
A^{\text{❀}}
-module
S
, there is an induced exact sequence

(S_{1},S)\xrightarrow{h}\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(N,S)\to\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(G^{\prime}N,S)\to\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(S_{1},S)

where the last term vanishes since
S_{1}
is projective, and where the map
h
is an isomorphism (because
\mathrm{Hom}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(P_{1},S)=0=\mathrm{Hom}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(P_{0},S)
).
This shows that
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(G^{\prime}N,S)=0

\bullet
The funtor
F
is dense: Let
N\in\mathscr{E}
.
The short exact sequence
S_{1}\rightarrowtail G^{\prime}N\twoheadrightarrow N
induces an isomorphism
\operatorname{soc}G^{\prime}N\cong\operatorname{soc}N\oplus S_{1}
, where
\operatorname{soc}N
has no projective summands.
This implies
FG^{\prime}N\cong N
.

\bullet
The functor
F
is full: Let
M,N\in\mathscr{F}
and let
FM\xrightarrow{f}FN
be any morphism.
The composition
M\twoheadrightarrow FM\to FN
factors through
N\twoheadrightarrow FN
, as in the diagram below, because
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(M,\varepsilon.\operatorname{soc}N)=0
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varepsilon.\operatorname{soc}N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FN}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

We thus have
Fg=f
.

\bullet
The functor
F
is faithful:
Given a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\varepsilon.\operatorname{soc}N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FN}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{N}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}

there is a morphism
h
such that
i_{N}h=g
.
However,
M
has no simple projective summands.
This implies that
h
, hence
g
, vanishes.

\bullet
The functor
F
is exact: Let
L\rightarrowtail M\twoheadrightarrow N
be a short exact sequence with all three terms in
\mathscr{F}
.
There is an induced split exact sequence
\varepsilon.\operatorname{soc}L\to\varepsilon.\operatorname{soc}M\to\varepsilon.\operatorname{soc}N
.
By the snake lemma, the induced sequence between the cokernels
FL\to FM\to FN
is short exact.
∎

Corollary 6.17
.

An
A^{\text{❀}}
-module belongs to
\mathscr{F}
if and only if it is the cokernel of an injection between projective
A^{\text{❀}}
-modules having no simple summands.

Proof.

By
Proposition

6.16
, any module in
\mathscr{F}
has projective dimension one and has a projective presentation by projective modules having no simple summands.
Conversely, let
P_{1}\rightarrowtail P_{0}\twoheadrightarrow M
be short exact with
P_{0},P_{1}
projective without any simple summand.
Then
M
is not simple projective and has projective dimension at most one. Moreover, for any simple projective
A^{\text{❀}}
-module
S
,
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(M,S)
is given by the cokernel of the induced map
\mathrm{Hom}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(P_{1},S)\to\mathrm{Hom}{A^{\text{❀}}}(P_{0},S)
whose domain and codomain both vanish.
Hence
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(M,S)=0
and
M
belongs to
\mathscr{F}
.
∎

Lemma 6.18
.

For any
v\in Q_{0}
, there is a short exact sequence in
\operatorname{mod}A^{\text{❀}}

0\to P_{v}\to I_{a}\oplus I_{b}\to I_{v}\to 0,

where
\operatorname{soc}P_{v}=S_{a}\oplus S_{b}
.

Proof.

This is immediate from string combinatorics:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hskip 30.0pt\oplus}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{b\hskip 25.0pt}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}

∎

Corollary 6.19
.

The projective objects of
\mathscr{F}
are precisely the projective
A^{\text{❀}}
-modules having no simple summands, and the injective objects of
\mathscr{F}
are precisely the injective
A^{\text{❀}}
-modules having no simple summands.

Proof.

Any non-simple projective
A^{\text{❀}}
-module belongs to
\mathscr{F}
and is projective in
\mathscr{F}
.
Conversely, let
M\in\mathscr{F}
and let
P_{1}\rightarrowtail P_{0}\twoheadrightarrow M
be short exact with
P_{0},P_{1}
projective
A^{\text{❀}}
-modules having no simple summands.
If
M
is projective in
\mathscr{F}
, then this sequence splits and
M
is a summand of
P_{0}
, hence a projective in
\operatorname{mod}A^{\text{❀}}
having no simple summands.

Let
I
be indecomposable injective in
\operatorname{mod}A^{\text{❀}}
.
If
I
is also projective, then it belongs to
\mathscr{F}
and is thus injective in
\mathscr{F}
.
Otherwise,
I
has simple socle not supported at a sink, hence
I
belongs to
\mathscr{E}
and
\varepsilon.\operatorname{soc}I=0
, showing that
I=GI
belongs to
\mathscr{F}
.

Conversely, let
M
be injective in
\mathscr{F}
.
Then, by
Proposition

6.16
,
FM
is injective in
\mathscr{E}
, which implies that
FM
belongs to
\operatorname{Fac}H
by
Lemmas

6.7
and
6.9
.
From the short exact sequence
\varepsilon.\operatorname{soc}M\rightarrowtail M\twoheadrightarrow FM
, one easily deduce that
FM
belongs to
\operatorname{Fac}H
if and only if
M
belongs to
\operatorname{Fac}H
(using that
M
has no simple projective summands).
It follows that
M
admits a projective resolution of the form
P_{1}\rightarrowtail H_{0}\twoheadrightarrow M
, where
P_{1}
is projective having no simple summands and
H_{0}
is projective-injective.
By
Lemma

6.18
, there is a short exact sequence
P_{1}\rightarrowtail H_{1}\twoheadrightarrow I_{1}
with
H_{1}
projective-injective and
I_{1}
injective having no simple summands.
Therefore,
M
is a summand of
H_{0}\oplus I_{1}
, hence an injective with no simple summands.
∎

Lemma 6.20
.

Let
\sigma
be a string for
(Q^{\text{❀}},I^{\text{❀}})
.
Then
M_{\sigma}
is of projective dimension at most one if and only if one of the following three conditions is satisfied:

(1)

M_{\sigma}
is simple projective over
A^{\text{❀}}
.

(2)

M_{\sigma}
is simple, supported at a non-blossoming vertex, and is projective as an
A
-module.

(3)

\sigma
contains at least one arrow or inverse arrow and

(a)

starts at a blossom or is such that the target of the unique arrow
\alpha
, such that
\alpha^{-1}\sigma
is a string, is a sink and

(b)

ends in a blossom or is such that the target of the unique arrow
\beta
, such that
\sigma\beta
is a string, is a sink.

Proof.

Let
\sigma
be a string for
(Q^{\text{❀}},I^{\text{❀}})
.

\bullet
Assume first that
M_{\sigma}=S_{i}
is simple.
If
i
is a sink, then
S_{i}
is projective, hence of projective dimension at most one.
Otherwise, its projective presentation is of the form
P_{j}\oplus P_{k}\to P_{i}\twoheadrightarrow S_{i}
, where the quiver
Q^{\text{❀}}
contains the subquiver
j\leftarrow i\to k
.
Because each non-blossoming vertex has two successors, the morphism
P_{j}\oplus P_{k}\to P_{i}
is injective if and only if both
j
and
k
are sinks, if and only if
i
is a sink in
Q
.

\bullet
Assume that
M_{\sigma}
is not simple, and let
P_{1}\to P_{0}
be a projective presentation of
M_{\sigma}
.

If the starting vertex of
\sigma
is not a blossom, then
P_{1}
contains
P_{t(\alpha)}
as a summand, where
\alpha^{-1}\sigma
is a string.
The restriction
P_{t(\alpha)}\to P_{0}
of
P_{1}\to P_{0}
is injective if and only if
t(\alpha)
is not the source of two distinct arrows, if and only if
t(\alpha)
is a blossoming vertex.
A similar argument applies at the endpoint of
\sigma
.
By string combinatorics (see for example
[
PPP21
, Proposition 1.43]
), we have that
P_{1}\to P_{0}
is injective if and only if
P_{t(\alpha)}\oplus P_{t(\beta)}\to P_{0}
is injective (with the convention that
P_{t(\alpha)}
is zero if
\sigma
starts at a blossoming vertex, and similarly for
P_{t(\beta)}
).
∎

The following proposition justifies the name of the exact category
\mathscr{F}
.

Proposition 6.21
.

Let
\sigma
be a string for the gentle bound quiver
(Q^{\text{❀}},I^{\text{❀}})
. Then the string module
M_{\sigma}
belongs to
\mathscr{F}
if and only if
\sigma
is a maximal string for
(Q^{\text{❀}},I^{\text{❀}})
, hence a walk for
(Q,I)
.

Proof.

Let
\sigma
be a string for
(Q^{\text{❀}},I^{\text{❀}})
.

\bullet
Assume that
M_{\sigma}
belongs to
\mathscr{F}
, i.e. that
M_{\sigma}
is not simple projective over
A^{\text{❀}}
, has projective dimension at most one, and
\mathrm{Ext}_{A^{\text{❀}}}^{1}(M_{\sigma},S)=0
(equivalently
\mathrm{Hom}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(S,\tau M_{\sigma})=0
) for each simple projective
A^{\text{❀}}
-module
S
.
Notice first that
Lemma

6.20
implies that
M_{\sigma}
cannot be simple.
Indeed, case (1) of
Lemma

6.20
is excluded by definition of
\mathscr{F}
and case (2) is excluded because every vertex that is a sink in
Q
is the source, in
Q^{\text{❀}}
, of an arrow with target a blossoming vertex
p
, thus has a non-split extension with the simple projective
S_{p}
.
Hence,
\sigma
is of the form given in condition (3) of
Lemma

6.20
.
The requirement on vanishing of extensions says that there cannot exist an arrow
\alpha
as in (3)(a) nor an arrow
\beta
as in (3)(b).
Thus
\sigma
, which has length at least one, starts and ends at a blossom, meaning that it is a walk on
(Q,I)
.

\bullet
Assume that
\sigma
is a walk on
(Q,I)
. Then
\sigma
starts at and ends in a blossoming vertex, and
M_{\sigma}
is not a simple module.
Hence, by
Lemma

6.20
,
M_{\sigma}
has projective dimension at most one.
Moreover, because
\sigma
is maximal,
\tau M_{\sigma}
is the indecomposable module associated with the string
\sigma^{\prime}
obtained from
\sigma
by removing a left and a right cohook.
In particular,
\sigma^{\prime}
is not supported on any blossom, showing that
\mathrm{Hom}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(S,\tau M_{\sigma})=0
for each simple projective
A^{\text{❀}}
-module
S
.
∎

Recall from
[
PPP21
]
that:

•

A projective walk is a walk of the form
\alpha_{1}^{-1}\cdots\alpha_{r}^{-1}\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{s}
, hence the string of an indecomposable projective
A^{\text{❀}}
-module at a non-blossoming vertex.

•

A shifted projective walk is a walk of the form
\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{r}\beta_{1}^{-1}\cdots\beta_{s}^{-1}
, hence the string of an indecomposable injective
A^{\text{❀}}
-module at a non-blossoming vertex.

•

A straight walk is a walk that is a path in
Q^{\text{❀}}
, hence the string of a projective-injective
A^{\text{❀}}
-module.

Proposition 6.22
.

Let
\omega
be a maximal string for
(Q^{\text{❀}},I^{\text{❀}})
.
The following properties holds:

(1)

The string module
M_{\omega}
is projective and non-injective in
\mathscr{F}
if and only if
\omega
is a projective walk.

(2)

The string module
M_{\omega}
is injective and non-projective in
\mathscr{F}
if and only if
\omega
is a shifted projective walk.

(3)

The string module
M_{\omega}
is projective and injective in
\mathscr{F}
if and only if
\omega
is a straight walk.

Proof.

This is a reformulation of
Corollary

6.19
.
∎

Although the following corollary is also immediate from
Propositions

6.16
and
6.8
, we make it explicit for future references.

Corollary 6.23
.

The exact category
\mathscr{F}
is 0-Auslander.

Lemma 6.24
.

Let
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}
be two strings for
(Q,I)
and let
\omega,\omega^{\prime}
be the associated walks.
Then, we have

\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(M_{\sigma},M_{\sigma^{\prime}})\cong\overline{\mathscr{F}}(M_{\omega},M_{\omega^{\prime}}),

where
\overline{\mathscr{F}}
denotes the quotient by the ideal of morphisms factoring through an injective object.

Proof.

Noting that
M_{\sigma}=GFM_{\omega}
, the statement follows from combining
Propositions

6.16
and
6.11

∎

Lemma 6.25
.

Let
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}
be two strings for
(Q,I)
, and let
\omega,\omega^{\prime}
be the associated walks.
Assume that
M_{\sigma^{\prime}}=\tau_{A}M_{\sigma}
.
Then
M_{\omega^{\prime}}=\tau_{A^{\text{❀}}}^{2}M_{\omega}
.

Proof.

This is
[
PPP21
, Proposition 2.49]
.
∎

Corollary 6.26
.

The subcategory
\mathscr{F}
of
\operatorname{mod}A^{\text{❀}}
is
\tau^{2}
-stable.

Proof.

This follows from
Propositions

6.21
and
6.25
.
∎

Proposition 6.27
.

The subcategory
\mathscr{F}
of
\operatorname{mod}A^{\text{❀}}
is functorially finite.

Proof.

Recall that middle terms of almost-split sequences in
\operatorname{mod}A^{\text{❀}}
have precisely one or two indecomposable summands.
Meshes in the Auslander–Reiten quiver of
A^{\text{❀}}
are thus either squares or triangles.
Moreover, arrows in this Auslander–Reiten quiver are given by adding or deleting one hook or cohook.
All this will be used implicitly in the proof.

Let
M\in\operatorname{mod}A^{\text{❀}}
be indecomposable not in
\mathscr{F}
.
We construct a right
\mathscr{F}
-approximation of
M
, the construction of a left approximation being similar.
Note that
M
cannot be a band module.
Thus it is a string module, associated with some string
\sigma
, at most one of whose endpoints is a blossom.
Let us consider three cases.

\bullet
If
\sigma
is reduced to a sink.
Then
M
is simple projective and its right approximation is 0.

\bullet
If
\sigma
has length at most one, and has one endpoint a blossom.
Then at the other endpoint of
\sigma
(which we may assume is its starting point, and which might be the blossom itself if
\sigma
is reduced to a source) it is possible to add a cohook
c
.
Since every cohook in
(Q^{\text{❀}},I^{\text{❀}})
ends in a blossom,
c\sigma
is a walk, hence
M_{c\sigma}\in\mathscr{F}
by
Proposition

6.21
.
Moreover, in the ray of the Auslander–Reiten quiver of
A^{\text{❀}}
ending in
M_{\sigma}
and not containing
M_{c\sigma}
, all indecomposables are associated with strings starting at the same endpoint as
\sigma
, hence strings that are not walks.
The string module
M_{c\sigma}
is thus the right approximation of
M_{\sigma}
.

\bullet
If
\sigma
has length at most one, and does not contain any blossom.
Then if
N
is an indecomposable
A^{\text{❀}}
-module sitting in one of the two rays of the Auslander–Reiten quiver of
A^{\text{❀}}
ending in
M_{\sigma}
, then its string is obtained by a succession of addings of cohooks or removings of hooks, always on the same side of the string
\sigma
.
Hence one of its endpoints is an endpoint of
\sigma
, thus in
Q_{0}
.
Using
Proposition

6.21
, this implies that
N
does not belong to
\mathscr{F}
.
Therefore, any morphism from a module in
\mathscr{F}
to
M_{\sigma}
factors through
\tau_{A^{\text{❀}}}M_{\sigma}
.
The string
\rho
associated with
\tau_{A^{\text{❀}}}M_{\sigma}
is obtained from
\sigma
by adding a cohook on each side of
\sigma
.
It follows that
\tau_{A^{\text{❀}}}M_{\sigma}
belongs to
\mathscr{F}
(using
Proposition

6.21
again) and that it is the right approximation of
M_{\sigma}
.
∎

Corollary 6.28
.

The category
\mathscr{F}
has almost-split extensions and, for any
M\in\mathscr{F}
, we have
\tau_{\mathscr{F}}M=\tau_{A^{\text{❀}}}^{2}M
.

Proof.

First note that
\mathscr{F}
is
\tau_{A^{\text{❀}}}^{2}
-stable by
Corollary

6.26
, so that the statement makes sense.
Since
\mathscr{F}
is functorially finite (
Proposition

6.27
), it has almost-split extensions.
Moreover, for any indecomposable module
M\in\mathscr{F}
, its Auslander–Reiten translation
\tau_{\mathscr{F}}M
is a summand of the right
\mathscr{F}
-approximation of
\tau_{A^{\text{❀}}}M
.
If
M
is a band module, then
\tau_{A^{\text{❀}}}M=M
and we have
\tau_{\mathscr{F}}M=\tau_{A^{\text{❀}}}M=\tau_{A^{\text{❀}}}^{2}M
.
If
M
is projective in
\operatorname{mod}A^{\text{❀}}
, then it is projective in
\mathscr{F}
by
Corollary

6.19
and we have
\tau_{\mathscr{F}}M=0=\tau_{A^{\text{❀}}}^{2}M
.
Otherwise,
M
is a string module associated with a non-projective walk
\omega
, and the string associated with
\tau_{A^{\text{❀}}}M
is obtained from that walk by removing a hook at each side of
\omega
.
We are thus in the third case of the proof of
Proposition

6.27
, and the right
\mathscr{F}
-approximation of
\tau_{A^{\text{❀}}}M
is
\tau_{A^{\text{❀}}}^{2}M
, which is indecomposable.
Therefore
\tau_{\mathscr{F}}M=\tau_{A^{\text{❀}}}^{2}M
.
∎

Notation 6.29
.

If
M
belongs to
\operatorname{mod}A
, there is a unique (up to isomorphism) object
X_{M}
in
\mathscr{F}
such that

•

GFX_{M}=M
, where
F
and
G
are the functors from
Propositions

6.11
and
6.16
, and

•

X_{M}
has no non-zero injective summands.

In other words we have
X_{M_{\sigma}}=M_{\omega}\in\operatorname{mod}A^{\text{❀}}
, where
\omega
is the walk associated with
\sigma
if
\sigma
is a string and where
\omega=\sigma
if
\sigma
is a band.
If
v\in Q_{0}
and
P=P_{v}
is the associated indecomposable projective
A
-module, let
X_{(0,P)}
be the indecomposable injective
A^{\text{❀}}
-module
I_{v}^{\text{❀}}
associated with vertex
v
.
Let
X_{(M,P_{v_{1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus P_{v_{r}})}=X_{M}\oplus X_{(0,P_{v_{1}})}\oplus\cdots\oplus X_{(0,P_{v_{r}})}
.

Following
[
AIR14
]
, for two pairs
(M,P),(N,Q)\in\operatorname{mod}A\times\operatorname{proj}A
we write
(M,P)\perp_{\tau}(N,Q)
if and only if
\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(M,\tau N)=0
and
\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(Q,M)=0
.

Theorem 6.30
.

Assume that
(Q,I)
is gentle, and let
A=KQ/I
.

(1)

Let
M,N\in\operatorname{mod}A\times\operatorname{proj}A
.
Then
M\perp_{\tau}N
if and only if
\mathbb{E}(X_{N},X_{M})=0
in
\mathscr{F}
.

(2)

Let
\omega,\omega^{\prime}
be two walks for
(Q,I)
.
Then
\mathbb{E}(M_{\omega^{\prime}},M_{\omega})\neq 0
in
\mathscr{F}
if and only if the walk
\omega
kisses the walk
\omega^{\prime}
.

Proof.

The second part of the statement follows from (1) and
[
PPP21
, Theorem 2.46]
.
Let us prove (1):
Recall from
Proposition

6.27
that the category
\mathscr{F}
is functorially finite in
\operatorname{mod}A^{\text{❀}}
and hence has Auslander–Reiten–Serre duality. Moreover, for any walk or band
\omega
, we have
\tau_{\mathscr{F}}M_{\omega}=\tau_{A^{\text{❀}}}^{2}M_{\omega}
by
Corollary

6.28
.

\bullet
Assume first that
\omega
and
\omega^{\prime}
are the walks associated with some strings
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}
.
Let
\sigma^{\prime\prime}
be such that
M_{\sigma^{\prime\prime}}=\tau_{A}M_{\sigma^{\prime}}
, and let
\omega^{\prime\prime}
be the walk associated with
\sigma^{\prime\prime}
.
By
Lemmas

6.24
,
6.25
and
6.28
and Auslander–Reiten–Serre duality, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(M_{\sigma},\tau_{A}M_{\sigma^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(M_{\sigma},M_{\sigma^{\prime\prime}})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\overline{\mathscr{F}}(M_{\omega},\tau_{A^{\text{❀}}}^{2}M_{\omega^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\overline{\mathscr{F}}(M_{\omega},\tau_{\mathscr{F}}M_{\omega^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle D\mathbb{E}(M_{\omega^{\prime}},M_{\omega}).

\bullet
We note that the sequence of isomorphisms in the first point above is still valid if
\sigma
or
\sigma^{\prime}
are bands, with the following convention: the walk associated with a band for
(A,I)
is the same band, viewed as a band for
(Q^{\text{❀}},I^{\text{❀}})
. We also note that
\sigma^{\prime\prime}=\sigma^{\prime}
when
\sigma^{\prime}
is a band.

\bullet
Let
v\in Q_{0}
, let
\sigma
be a string or a band for
(Q,I)
and let
\omega
be the associated walk (with the convention that
\omega=\sigma
is
\sigma
is a band).
Let
c,d
be the blossoming vertices appearing in the top of the injective
A^{\text{❀}}
-module
I_{v}^{\text{❀}}
(with
d=\emptyset
if the top of
I_{v}^{\text{❀}}
is simple), and consider the short exact sequence

0\to P_{v}^{\text{❀}}\to P_{c}^{\text{❀}}\oplus P_{d}^{\text{❀}}\to I_{v}^{\text{❀}}\to 0,

of
Lemma

6.18
, with the convention that
P_{d}=0
if
d=\emptyset
.
There is an induced exact sequence

\mathrm{Hom}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(P_{c}^{\text{❀}}\oplus P_{d}^{\text{❀}},M_{\omega})\xrightarrow{\varphi}\mathrm{Hom}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(P_{v}^{\text{❀}},M_{\omega})\to\mathbb{E}(I_{v}^{\text{❀}},M_{\omega})\to 0.

If
\sigma
is a band, then
\sigma=\omega
does not contain any blossoming vertex and the first term of the exact sequence vanishes, showing that
\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(P_{v},M\sigma)\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(P_{v}^{\text{❀}},M_{\omega})\cong\mathbb{E}(I_{v}^{\text{❀}},M_{\omega})
.
If
\sigma
is a string, then a basis for
\mathrm{Hom}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(P_{v}^{\text{❀}},M_{\omega})
is parametrized by the occurrences of
v
in
\omega
and a basis for the image of
\varphi
is given by those morphisms that are parametrized by the occurrences of
v
in
\omega
that do not belong to
\sigma
(indeed, only the endpoints of
\omega
are blossoms).
Hence
\mathbb{E}(I_{v}^{\text{❀}},M_{\omega})\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{A^{\text{❀}}}(P_{v}^{\text{❀}},M_{\omega})/\operatorname{Im}\varphi
is non-zero if and only if
v
occurs in
\sigma
, if and only if
\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(P_{v},M_{\sigma})\neq 0
.

The remaining cases being trivially satisfied, this finishes the proof.
∎

By combining
Theorems

4.26
,
6.23
and
6.30
, we obtain the following:

Corollary 6.31
.

For any finite-dimensional gentle algebra, the flip of non-kissing facets is an instance of mutations of maximal rigid objects in 0-Auslander extriangulated categories.

7.
Negative extensions
in hereditary extriangulated categories

In
[
GNP21
]
, we defined covariant and contravariant left derived functors of the functor
\mathrm{Hom}
in essentially small extriangulated categories having enough projective, respectively enough injective morphsims. We proved that they agree under certain conditions; in this case, they form a bivariant
\delta-
functor satisfying natural universal properties, whose components we called
balanced negative extensions
. We conjectured that one can define balanced negative extensions in each extriangulated category. In this section, we give a partial confirmation in the hereditary case. Namely, we define balanced negative extensions
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{\bullet}
in hereditary categories satisfying certain restrictive conditions
(\rm{N+})
. Also, we construct another bivariant
\delta-
functor
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{\bullet}
for each
0-
Auslander category embedded into a triangulated category in a special way. These two functors do not agree in general, but we have nontrivial examples when they do agree. The latter
\delta-
functor a priori depends on the embedding, but for categories linear over fields and having finite-dimensional
\mathrm{Hom}-
and
\mathbb{E}-
spaces,
dimensions of its components are independent of this choice. Moreover, we expect that whenever a
0-
Auslander category admits universal balanced negative extensions, these are given by
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{\bullet}
, and so
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{\bullet}
is the correct replacement of
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}
when the condition
(\rm{N+})
is not satisfied. We will work in the following generality, which, in particular, allows for a relatively easy presentation of relevant results from
[
GNP21
]
.

Assumption 7.1
.

In this section, we always assume that
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
is an essentially small hereditary
R
-linear extriangulated category which has enough projective objects and enough injective objects.

By
7.1
, to each object
X\in\mathscr{C}
we can assign a pair of
\mathfrak{s}
-triangles

(7.1)

Q_{X}\overset{q_{X}}{\longrightarrow}P_{X}\overset{p_{X}}{\longrightarrow}X\overset{\omega_{X}}{\dashrightarrow},

(7.2)

X\overset{i_{X}}{\longrightarrow}I_{X}\overset{j_{X}}{\longrightarrow}J_{X}\overset{\iota_{X}}{\dashrightarrow}

in which
P_{X}\in\mathscr{C}
is projective and
I_{X}\in\mathscr{C}
is injective. These
\mathfrak{s}
-triangles are automatically
dominant
(resp.
codominant
) in the sense of
[
GNP21
]
, meaning that the morhpisms

\omega_{X}^{\sharp}:\mathscr{C}(Q_{X},-)\to\mathbb{E}(X,-)\quad\mbox{and}\quad{\iota_{X}}_{\sharp}:\mathscr{C}(-,J_{X})\to\mathbb{E}(-,X)

are epimorphic.

Remark that since
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
is hereditary,
Q_{X}
is projective and
J_{X}
is injective, so these
\mathfrak{s}
-triangles can be thought of as a projective resolution (resp. an injective coresolution) of
X
. For each
X
, we fix a pair of
\mathfrak{s}
-triangles (
7.1
) and (
7.2
) and use these in our constructions. Note though that the connected sequences of functors we define will not depend on such a choice.

7.1.
Reminder on negative extensions

Recall that we defined in
[
GNP21
]
a
covariant connected sequence of functors
as a pair
(F^{\bullet}=\{F^{n}\colon\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{C}\to\mathrm{Mod}R\}_{n\in[a,b]},\delta_{\sharp})
, where

•

a\in\left\{-\infty\right\}\cup\mathbb{Z}
and
b\in\mathbb{Z}\cup\left\{+\infty\right\}
such that
a\leq b
;

•

F^{\bullet}=\{F^{n}\colon\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{C}\to\mathrm{Mod}R\}_{n\in[a,b]}
a sequence of
R
-bilinear functors;

•

\delta_{\sharp}=\delta_{F,\sharp}^{n}\in\mathscr{C}(F^{n}(-,C),F^{n+1}(-,A))
is a collection of morphisms in
\mathrm{Mod}\mathscr{C}
, for any
\mathbb{E}
-extension
\delta\in\mathbb{E}(C,A)
and
n\in[a,b],
natural with respect to morphisms of
\mathbb{E}
-extensions.

Contravariant
connected sequences were defined dually. A
bivariant connected sequence of functors
is a triplet
(F^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp},\delta^{\sharp})
where
(F^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp})
and
(F^{\bullet},\delta^{\sharp})
form a covariant, respectively a contravariant connected sequence.
As in
[
GNP21
]
, we use the shorthand notation
f_{\ast}=F^{n}(-,f)
and
f^{\ast}=F^{n}(f,-)
for any morphism
f
in
\mathscr{C}
and any
F^{n}
in any connected sequence.

A
covariant partial
\delta
-functor
is a connected sequence
(F^{\bullet}=\{F^{n}\colon\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{C}\to\mathrm{Mod}R\}_{n\in[a,b]},\delta_{\sharp})
such that
for any
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
A\overset{x}{\longrightarrow}B\overset{y}{\longrightarrow}C\overset{\delta}{\dashrightarrow}
, the complex

(7.3)

F^{a}(-,A)\overset{F^{a}(-,x)}{\longrightarrow}F^{a}(-,B)\overset{F^{a}(-,y)}{\longrightarrow}F^{a}(-,C)\overset{\delta^{a}_{F,\sharp}}{\longrightarrow}F^{a+1}(-,A)\overset{F^{a+1}(-,x)}{\longrightarrow}\cdots\overset{F^{b}(-,y)}{\longrightarrow}F^{b}(-,C)

is exact except at the end-terms. It is a
\delta
-functor
if
a=-\infty,b=+\infty
. For a connected sequence
(F^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp})
, we say that an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle is
(F^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp})
-acyclic
if the associated unbounded complex (
7.3
) is exact. In other words, a sequence
(F^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp})
is a
\delta
-functor if and only if each
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle in
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
is
(F^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp})
-acyclic.

Contravariant
and
bivariant
(also called
balanced
)
(partial)
\delta
-functors
are defined similarly.

The bifunctors
\mathrm{Hom}
and
\mathbb{E}
are half-exact in either argument and, therefore, are bivariant partial
\delta
-functors. Moreover, the sequence
(\mathbb{E}^{\bullet}=\{\mathbb{E}^{n}\}_{n\geq 0},\delta_{\sharp},\delta^{\sharp})
is also a bivariant partial
\delta
-functor, where we denote
\mathbb{E}^{0}=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}
, see
[
GNP21
, Example 5.8]
(under
7.1
,
\mathbb{E}^{n}=0
for
n\geq 2
).

We are interested in connected sequences
(F^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp})
such that
F^{n}=\mathbb{E}^{n}
, and
\delta_{\sharp}\colon F^{n}(-,C)\to F^{n+1}(-,A)
agrees with
\delta_{\sharp}\colon\mathbb{E}^{n}(-,C)\to\mathbb{E}^{n+1}(-,A)
for each
n\geq 0,\,A,C\in\mathscr{C}
. We then say that
(F^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp})

has

F^{n}=\mathbb{E}^{n}

with the canonical connecting morphisms
for
n\geq 0
. Similarly for contravariant and bivariant sequences. To prove that the connected sequences we construct later are
\delta
-functors, we will apply the following useful result.

Lemma 7.2
.

Assume that
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
satisfies
7.1
. Then a covariant
(F^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp})
having
F^{n}=\mathbb{E}^{n}
with the canonical connecting morphisms for
n\geq 0
is a
\delta
-functor if and only if for each
X
, some (equivalently, each) dominant
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle (
7.1
) is
(F^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp})
-acyclic. Dually, a contravariant
(F^{\bullet},\delta^{\sharp})
having
F^{n}=\mathbb{E}^{n}
with the canonical connecting morphisms for
n\geq 0
is a
\delta
-functor if and only if for each
X
, some (equivalently, each) dominant
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle (
7.2
) is
(F^{\bullet},\delta^{\sharp})
-acyclic.

Proof.

This is a direct application of
[
GNP21
, Proposition 4.22]
and its dual version for contravariant functors to our setting and generality, since the dominance of
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle (
7.1
) implies that it can be taken as
\theta
in the condition
[
GNP21
, Proposition 4.22.(2)]
for any
\delta\in\mathbb{E}(X,Y)
, for any
Y\in\mathscr{C}
.
∎

Remark 7.3
.

In fact, the same result holds (with the same proof) for any category
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
of finite (positive) global dimension and having
enough projective morphisms
for
(F^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp})
(resp.
enough injective morphisms
for
(F^{\bullet},\delta^{\sharp})
), in the sense of
[
GNP21
]
.

A balanced partial
\delta
-functor
(F^{n})_{n\geq-1},\delta_{\sharp},\delta^{\sharp})
having
F^{n}=\mathbb{E}^{n}
with the canonical connecting morphisms for
n\geq 0
(more precisely, its part with
n\in[-1,0]
) is the same as a
negative first extension structure
on
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
in the sense of
[
AET21
]
. We are interested in
universal
negative extensions, where the natural universal property was introduced in
[
GNP21
]
:

A contravariant
\delta
-functor
(F^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}},\delta_{\sharp})
is
universal
among
\delta
-functors having
F^{n}=\mathbb{E}^{n}
with the canonical connecting morphisms for
n\geq 0
if for each
((G^{n}),n\in\mathbb{Z};\delta_{\sharp})
having
G^{n}=\mathbb{E}^{n}
with the canonical connecting morphisms for
n\geq 0
, there exists a sequence of natural transformations

\{\varphi^{n}\colon G^{n}\to F^{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
which satisfies the following conditions:

(i)

\varphi^{n}=\mathrm{id}
for any
n\geq 0
;

(ii)

For any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the following diagram

\textstyle{G^{n}(A,-)}
\textstyle{G^{n+1}(C,-)}
\textstyle{E^{n}(A,-)}
\textstyle{E^{n+1}(C,-)}
\scriptstyle{\delta^{\sharp,n}}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{n}_{A,-}}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{n+1}_{C,-}}
\scriptstyle{\delta^{\sharp,n}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

is commutative in
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{Mod}
for any
\delta\in\mathbb{E}(C,A)
.

If such universal
\delta
-functor
(F^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}},\delta_{\sharp})
exists,
F^{-n}
is called the
n
-th left contravariant derived functor
of the bifunctor
\mathrm{Hom}
, or the
universal contravariant negative
n
-th extension
.

Similar definitions apply for covariant and for bivariant (= balanced)
\delta
-functors. Note that
balanced negative extensions
are considered
universal
if they are universal among the balanced
\delta
-functors (having
F^{n}~=~\mathbb{E}^{n}
with the canonical connecting morphisms for
n\geq 0
), which is a weaker property than being a bivariant
\delta
-functor being universal both among covariant and contravariant
\delta
-functors.

In
[
GNP21
, Section 5]
, for categories having enough projective, respectively enough injective morphisms, we defined universal covariant
(\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp})
and contravariant
(\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{\bullet},\delta^{\sharp})

\delta
-functors having
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{n}~=~\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{n}~=~\mathbb{E}^{n}
with the canonical connecting morphisms for
n\geq 0
. Their definition involves the dualities

(-)^{\vee}\colon\mathscr{C}\mathrm{Mod}\overset{\leftarrow}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{Mod}\mathscr{C}\colon(-)^{\vee},

see
[
GNP21
, Section 5.1]
. In particular, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-n}(-,X)=(\mathbb{E}^{n}(X,-))^{\vee},\quad\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-n}(X,-)=(\mathbb{E}^{n}(-,X))^{\vee}.

In our special case - that is, under
7.1
, - their components
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-n},\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-n}
admit alternative easier recursive descriptions given in
[
GNP21
, Remark 5.23]
. Namely, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-n}(-,X)\cong\mathrm{Ker}\big(\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-(n-1)}(-,Q_{X})\overset{(q_{C})_{\ast}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-(n-1)}(-,P_{X})\big),

for any
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle of the form (
7.1
), for each
X\in\mathscr{C}
. This is well-defined up to unique natural isomorphisms, independently of the choice of
\mathfrak{s}
-triangles. Dually for
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{\bullet}
, where one can use any
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle (
7.2
) for the first argument. In what follows we will not use the precise definition of connecting morphisms, only their existence; the reader is referred to
[
GNP21
, Definition 5.3]
.

Remark 7.4
.

•

If
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
is exact, then
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-n}(X,Y)=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-n}(X,Y)=0
.

•

It is clear from the definition via dualities
that for a category of (positive) global dimension
n
, we have
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-m}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-m}=0,
for each
m>n
. In particular, under
7.1
, we have
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-m}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-m}=0,
for each
m\geq 2
(this can also be seen directly from the alternatve description).

The universality of
(\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp})
and
(\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{\bullet},\delta^{\sharp})
is proved in
[
GNP21
, Proposition 5.19, Corollary 5.21, Corollary 5.22]
. It means that
(\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-n}),n>0
and
(\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-n}),n>0
should be seen as negative extensions in the extriangulated category
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
. In general, thus defined negative extensions are not balanced: given an arbitrary extriangulated category, we often do not have
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-n}\cong\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-n}
for all
n>0
.

A special case
[
GNP21
, Corollary 5.39]
says that if
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
satisfies
7.1
and conditions (NI
+
) and (NII+) defined below, then
(\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{n}\cong\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
gives
a universal bivariant
\delta
-functor having
\mathbb{E}^{n}
with the canonical connecting morphisms for
n\geq 0
.

Condition 7.5
.

(NI
+
)

((\omega_{Y})_{\sharp})^{-1}((\mathcal{I})(X,Q_{Y}))=0

holds in
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(X,Y)
for any
X,Y\in\mathscr{C}
and
\omega_{Y}
in any
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle of the form (
7.1
) for
Y
.

(NII
+
)

((\iota_{X})^{\sharp})^{-1}((\mathcal{P})(J_{X},Y))=0
holds in
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(X,Y)
for any
X,Y\in\mathscr{C}
and
\iota_{X}
in any
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle of the form (
7.2
) for
X
.

Example 7.6
.

Conditions (NI
+
), (NII
+
) are satisfied if
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
is an exact category or the category
K^{[-1,0]}(\operatorname{proj}\Lambda)
, where
\Lambda
is a finite-dimensional algebra, see
[
GNP21
, Remark 5.32, Example 5.34]
.

Remark 7.7
.

Certain weaker conditions (NI) and (NII) for categories which are
\mathrm{Hom}
-finite over a field
k
were shown in
[
GNP21
, Proposition 5.30]
to be sufficient to ensure that

\mbox{dim}_{k}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-n}=\mbox{dim}_{k}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-n},n>0.

Under
7.1
, we can reformulate these conditions as follows by using
[
GNP21
, Corollary 5.9, Corollary 5.11]
:

(NI)

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(I,-)=0
holds for any injective object
I\in\mathscr{C}
.

(NII)

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(-,P)=0
holds for any projective object
P\in\mathscr{C}
.

Consider the following properties a bivariant
\delta
-functor
(E^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp},\delta^{\sharp})
might have:

(U)

(Universality)
(E^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp},\delta^{\sharp})
is universal among
\delta
-functors having
F^{n}=\mathbb{E}^{n}
with the canonical connecting morphisms for
n\geq 0
;

(C)

(Compatibility)
(E^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp},\delta^{\sharp})
has
F^{n}=\mathbb{E}^{n}
with the canonical connecting morphisms for
n\geq 0
and

E^{-n}(I,-)\cong\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-n}(I,-);\quad E^{-n}(-,P)\cong\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-n}(-,P),

for all
I
injective,
P
projective,
n>0
.

Note that under conditions (NI+) and (NII+) on
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
, the
\delta
-functor

(\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{n}\cong\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
satisfies
both properties (U) and (C). This motivates the following questions (cf.
[
GNP21
, Section 5.5]
).

Problem 7.8
.

Assume that
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
satisfies
7.1
.

(a)

Does
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
admits a bivariant
\delta
-functor
(\mathbb{E}^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp},\delta^{\sharp})
having property (C)?

(b)

Does
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
admit
(\mathbb{E}^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp},\delta^{\sharp})
satisfying property (U)?

In fact, we expect that if
a bivariant
\delta
-functor has property (U), it has property (C), so a positive answer
7.8
.(b) would also give the positive answer to
7.8
.(a). Moreover, we have the following easy observation.

Lemma 7.9
.

Under
7.1
, if
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
admits a pair of bivariant
\delta
-functors
(\mathbb{E}_{un}^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp},\delta^{\sharp})
having both properties
\rm{(U)}
and
\rm{(C)}
and
(E^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp},\delta^{\sharp})
having property
\rm{(C)}
.
Then we have
(\mathbb{E}_{un}^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp},\delta^{\sharp})\overset{\sim}{\to}(\mathbb{E}^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp},\delta^{\sharp})
.

Proof.

The universality of
(\mathbb{E}_{un}^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp},\delta^{\sharp})
implies the existence of a morphism of
\delta
-functors
(\mathbb{E}_{un}^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp},\delta^{\sharp})\to(\mathbb{E}^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp},\delta^{\sharp})
. Since both
\delta
-functors have property (C), they agree on pairs
(I,-)
and
(-,P)
. That the morphisms between components are in fact isomorphisms for each pair of objects then follows by the five lemma from the existence of (co)resolutions (
7.1
) and (
7.2
) for each object in
\mathscr{C}
.
∎

•

As explained above, for
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
satisfying conditions (NI+) and (NII+), it follows from
[
GNP21
]
that the answer to both
7.8
.(a) and (b) is positive and the
\delta
-functor in question is given by

(\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{n}\cong\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
with suitable connecting morphisms.

•

In
Section

7.3
, for
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
satisfying a certain condition (N+), we construct a bivariant
\delta
-functor
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{\bullet}
giving the positive answer both to
7.8
.(a) and (b);

•

In
Section

7.4
, for a class of
0
-Auslander categories embedded in triangulated categories, we construct bivariant
\delta
-functors
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{\bullet}
satisfying property (C).

In fact, under
7.1
, property (C) defines the bifunctor
\mathbb{E}^{-2}
uniquely up to natural isomorphism and implies that

E^{-m}=0,\quad\forall m\geq 3,

as will be explained in the next subsection. The nontrivial problems are then to find
\mathbb{E}^{-1}
,
\delta_{\sharp}
, and
\delta^{\sharp}
completing it to a
\delta
-functor, and to check if the result is universal.

7.2.
Definition of
\mathbb{E}^{-2}

In
[
GNP21
]
, for each
X,Y\in\mathscr{C}
and each choice of
\mathfrak{s}
-triangles (
7.1
) and (
7.2
), we studied the following two complexes:

C_{\mathrm{I}}^{\bullet}\colon\quad\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(J_{X},Y)\overset{j^{\ast}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(I_{X},Y)\overset{i^{\ast}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(X,Y)\overset{\iota^{\sharp}\circ\omega_{\sharp}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}(J_{X},Q_{Y})

and

C_{\mathrm{I}\!\mathrm{I}}^{\bullet}\colon\quad\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(X,Q_{Y})\overset{q_{\ast}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(X,P_{Y})\overset{p_{\ast}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(X,Y)\overset{\omega_{\sharp}\circ\iota^{\sharp}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}(J_{X},Q_{Y}).

We proved the following.

Fact 7.10
.

(i)

[
GNP21
, Lemma 5.26]
The rightmost morphisms in
C_{\mathrm{I}}^{\bullet}
and
C_{\mathrm{I}\!\mathrm{I}}^{\bullet}
, i.e.
\iota_{X}^{\sharp}\circ\omega_{Y\sharp}
and
\omega_{Y\sharp}\circ\iota_{X}^{\sharp}
, have the same image.

(ii)

[
GNP21
, Proposition 5.28]
For any
X,Y\in\mathscr{C}
, the cohomologies of the complexes
C_{\mathrm{I}}^{\bullet}
and
C_{\mathrm{I}\!\mathrm{I}}^{\bullet}
are isomorphic.

Note that
7.10
.(ii) implies, in particular, that the morphisms
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(J_{X},Y)\overset{j_{X}^{\ast}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(I_{X},Y)
and
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(X,Q_{Y})\overset{q_{Y\ast}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(X,P_{Y})
have isomorphic kernels. These kernels define the bifunctor
\mathbb{E}^{-2}
. More precisely, by taking kernels in the commutative square

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 67.02464pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern-67.02464pt\raise 17.07156pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathscr{C}(J_{X},Q_{Y})}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 12.6909pt\raise 17.07156pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathscr{C}(J_{X},P_{Y})}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-66.36145pt\raise-17.07156pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathscr{C}(I_{X},Q_{Y})}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 13.35411pt\raise-17.07156pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathscr{C}(I_{X},P_{Y})}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-9.8037pt\raise 22.25906pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{q_{Y\ast}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 12.6909pt\raise 17.07156pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-55.99521pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.99155pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{j_{X}^{\ast}}$}}}\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 39.83365pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.99155pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{j_{X}^{\ast}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 39.83365pt\raise-9.07156pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-9.8037pt\raise-22.25905pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{q_{Y\ast}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 13.35411pt\raise-17.07156pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.125pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.70752pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}}}},

we can define
\mathbb{E}^{-2}(X,Y)\in\mathrm{Mod}R
,
\eta^{\mathrm{I}}_{X,Y}\in(\mathrm{Mod}R)(\mathbb{E}^{-2}(X,Y),\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(J_{X},Y))
and

\eta^{\mathrm{I}\!\mathrm{I}}_{X,Y}\in(\mathrm{Mod}R)(\mathbb{E}^{-2}(X,Y),\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(X,Q_{Y}))
so that they fit in the following commutative diagram

(7.4)

\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}^{-2}(X,Y)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(X,Q_{Y})}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(X,P_{Y})}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(J_{X},Y)}
\textstyle{\mathscr{C}(J_{X},Q_{Y})}
\textstyle{\mathscr{C}(J_{X},P_{Y})}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(I_{X},Y)}
\textstyle{\mathscr{C}(I_{X},Q_{Y})}
\textstyle{\mathscr{C}(I_{X},P_{Y})}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{\mathrm{I}\!\mathrm{I}}_{X,Y}}
\scriptstyle{q_{Y\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{\mathrm{I}}_{X,Y}}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{X}^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{X}^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\omega_{Y\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{q_{Y\ast}}
\scriptstyle{j_{X}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{j_{X}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{j_{X}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\omega_{Y\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{q_{Y\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

whose rows and columns are exact.

Lemma 7.11
.

Take any
s\in\mathscr{C}(J_{X^{\prime}},J_{X})
and
t\in\mathscr{C}(Q_{Y},Q_{Y^{\prime}})
satisfying
x_{\ast}\iota_{X^{\prime}}=s^{\ast}\iota_{X}
and
y^{\ast}\omega_{Y^{\prime}}=t_{\ast}\omega_{Y}
, which exist since
I_{X}\in\mathscr{C}
is injective and
P_{Y}\in\mathscr{C}
is projective. Then there exists a unique morphism
\mathbb{E}^{-2}(x,y)\colon\mathbb{E}^{-2}(X,Y)\to\mathbb{E}^{-2}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
which makes

(7.5)

\textstyle{\mathbb{E}^{-2}(X,Y)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(J_{X},Y)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}^{-2}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(J_{X^{\prime}},Y^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{\mathrm{I}}_{X,Y}}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{E}^{-2}(x,y)}
\scriptstyle{s^{\ast}y_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{\mathrm{I}}_{X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

and

\textstyle{\mathbb{E}^{-2}(X,Y)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(X,Q_{Y})}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}^{-2}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(X^{\prime},Q_{Y^{\prime}})}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{\mathrm{I}\!\mathrm{I}}_{X,Y}}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{E}^{-2}(x,y)}
\scriptstyle{x^{\ast}t_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{\mathrm{I}\!\mathrm{I}}_{X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

commutative simultaneously.
Remark that either of these commutativity conditions determines
\mathbb{E}^{-2}(x,y)
uniquely. Since the left square does not involve
t
while the right one does not involve
s
, this in particular implies that
\mathbb{E}^{-2}(x,y)
does not depend on the choices of
s
and
t
.

Proof.

By the universality of kernel, there exists a unique morphism
\mathbb{E}^{-2}(x,y)
which makes the left square of (
7.5
) commutative. It is not difficult to show that this morphism makes the right diagram also commutative, by using the commutativity of (
7.4
) for
X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}
and the monomorphicity of morphisms appearing in the left-upper square in it.
∎

Proposition 7.12
.

\mathbb{E}^{-2}\colon\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{C}\to\mathrm{Mod}R
is an
R
-bilinear functor. Moreover,
\mathbb{E}^{-2}
is uniquely determined up to natural isomorphism, independently of the choices
(\ref{sTri_Chosen_proj})
and
(\ref{sTri_Chosen_inj})
.

Proof.

This follows from
Lemma

7.11
by the usual, straightforward argument.
∎

Remark 7.13
.

Note that the top left commutative square in the diagram (
7.4
) means that we have

(7.6)

\mathbb{E}^{-2}(X,Y)=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(J_{X},Y)\times_{\mathcal{C}(J_{X},Q_{Y})}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(X,Q_{Y}).

Proposition 7.14
.

Assume that
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
satisfies
7.1
. Let
(E^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp},\delta^{\sharp})
be a
\delta
-functor having
E^{n}=\mathbb{E}^{n}
with the canonical connecting morphisms for
n\geq 0
. If it satisfies property (C), then
E^{-2}
is naturally isomorphic to
\mathbb{E}^{-2}
and

E^{-m}=0,\quad\forall m\geq 3.

Proof.

This follows immediately from the long exact sequences for
E^{n}
applied to
\mathfrak{s}
-triangles of the form (
7.1
) for the first argument or, equivalently, to
\mathfrak{s}
-triangles of the form (
7.2
) for the second argument. Indeed, they show that
E^{-2}
agrees with the kernel of
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(J_{X},Y)\overset{j_{X}^{\ast}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(I_{X},Y)
and with the kernel
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(X,Q_{Y})\overset{q_{Y\ast}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(X,P_{Y})
, and that
E^{-n}=0
for
n\geq 3
.
∎

7.3.
Negative extensions via pullbacks

In this subsection, we complete
\mathbb{E}^{-2}
to a bivariant connected sequence of functors in an extriangulated category
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
satisfying
7.1
.
Under certain condition, this sequence is a
\delta
-functor having property (U), so its components give the (universal) balanced negative extensions in
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
(see
Theorem

7.24
below).

Definition 7.15
.

Let
X,Y\in\mathscr{C}
be any pair of objects. Using the complexes
C_{\mathrm{I}}^{\bullet},C_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{\bullet}
in
7.10
, we define
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(X,Y)\in\mathrm{Mod}R
and
\wp^{\mathbf{i}}_{X,Y}\in(\mathrm{Mod}R)(\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(X,Y),\mathbb{E}^{-1}_{\mathbf{i}}(X,Y))

(\mathbf{i}=\mathrm{I},\mathrm{I}\!\mathrm{I})
by a pullback diagram taken as below.

\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(X,Y)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(X,Y)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(X,Y)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}(J_{X},Q_{Y})}
\scriptstyle{\wp^{\mathrm{I}}_{X,Y}}
\scriptstyle{\wp^{\mathrm{I}\!\mathrm{I}}_{X,Y}}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{X}^{\sharp}\circ\omega_{Y\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\omega_{Y\sharp}\circ\iota_{X}^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

Remark that by
7.10
.(i),
\iota_{X}^{\sharp}\circ\omega_{Y\sharp}
and
\omega_{Y\sharp}\circ\iota_{X}^{\sharp}
appearing in the diagram have the same image, hence
\wp^{\mathbf{i}}_{X,Y}
is surjective for
\mathbf{i}=\mathrm{I},\mathrm{I}\!\mathrm{I}
.

Lemma 7.16
.

Let
x\in\mathscr{C}(X^{\prime},X)
and
y\in\mathscr{C}(Y,Y^{\prime})
be any pair of morphisms. Then there exists a unique morphism
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(x,y)\colon\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(X,Y)\to\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
which makes

\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(X,Y)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{i}}^{-1}(X,Y)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{i}}^{-1}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\wp^{\mathbf{i}}_{X,Y}}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(x,y)}
\scriptstyle{x^{\ast}y_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\wp^{\mathbf{i}}_{X^{\prime},Y^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

commutative for
\mathbf{i}=\mathrm{I},\mathrm{I}\!\mathrm{I}
.

Proof.

This follows from the commutativity of

\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(X,Y)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}(J_{X},Q_{Y})}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(X,Y)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}(J_{X^{\prime}},Q_{Y^{\prime}})}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{X}^{\sharp}\circ\omega_{Y\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\omega_{Y\sharp}\circ\iota_{X}^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{x^{\ast}y_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{s^{\ast}t_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{x^{\ast}y_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{X^{\prime}}^{\sharp}\circ\omega_{Y^{\prime}\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\omega_{Y^{\prime}\sharp}\circ\iota_{X^{\prime}}^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

and the universality of the pullback.

∎

We set
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}:=\mathbb{E}^{-2}
. In what follows, we abbreviate
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(x,y)
and
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}(x,y)
simply to
x^{\ast}y_{\ast}
, as before.

Proposition 7.17
.

With the above definition,
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}\colon\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{C}\to\mathrm{Mod}R
is an
R
-bilinear functor, and
\wp^{\mathbf{i}}\colon\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}\to\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{i}}^{-1}
is a natural transformation for
\mathbf{i}=\mathrm{I},\mathrm{I}\!\mathrm{I}
. Moreover,
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}
is uniquely determined up to natural isomorphism compatible with
\wp^{\mathbf{i}}
for
\mathbf{i}=\mathrm{I},\mathrm{I}\!\mathrm{I}
, independently of the choices
(\ref{sTri_Chosen_proj})
and
(\ref{sTri_Chosen_inj})
.

Proof.

This follows from
Lemma

7.16
by the usual, straightforward argument.
∎

Corollary 7.18
.

The following holds.

(1)

If
I
is injective, then
\wp^{\mathrm{I}}_{I,-}\colon\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(I,-)\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(I,-)
is an isomorphism,
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}(I,-)=0
.

(2)

If
P
is projective, then
\wp^{\mathrm{I}\!\mathrm{I}}_{-,P}\colon\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(-,P)\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(-,P)
is an isomorphism,
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}(-,P)=0
.

Proof.

(1) follows from the independency shown in
Proposition

7.17
, since we may choose an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle satisfying
J_{X}=0
if
X
is injective. Similarly for (2).
∎

Lemma 7.19
.

Let
\delta\in\mathbb{E}(W,X)
be any element. Remark that there exists
w\in\mathscr{C}(W,J_{X})
such that
w^{\ast}\iota_{X}=\delta
since
I_{X}\in\mathscr{C}
is injective. For the composition
\xi_{\delta}
of

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}(X,-)\overset{\eta^{\mathrm{I}}_{X,-}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(J_{X},-)\overset{w^{\ast}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(W,-),

the following holds.

(1)

\xi_{\delta}
is independent of the choice of
w
.

(2)

There exists a unique
\delta^{\sharp}\colon\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}(X,-)\to\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(W,-)
which makes

\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}(X,-)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(W,-)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(W,-)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(W,-)}
\scriptstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{\delta^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\xi_{\delta}}
\scriptstyle{\wp^{\mathrm{I}\!\mathrm{I}}_{W,-}}
\scriptstyle{\wp^{\mathrm{I}}_{W,-}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

commutative.

Proof.

(1) If
w^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}(W,J_{X})
also satisfies
w^{\prime\ast}\iota_{X}=\delta
, then there exists
l\in\mathscr{C}(W,I_{X})
such that
w-w^{\prime}=j_{X}\circ l
by the exactness of
\mathscr{C}(W,I_{X})\overset{j_{X}\circ-}{\longrightarrow}\mathscr{C}(W,J_{X})\overset{(\iota_{X})_{\sharp}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}(W,X)
.
Then it follows

w^{\ast}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{I}}_{X,Y}=w^{\prime\ast}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{I}}_{X,Y}+l^{\ast}\circ j_{X}^{\ast}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{I}}_{X,Y}=w^{\prime\ast}\circ\eta^{\mathrm{I}}_{X,Y}

since the composition of
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}(X,-)\overset{\eta^{\mathrm{I}}_{X,-}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(J_{X},-)\overset{j_{X}^{\ast}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(I_{X},-)
is zero by the definition of
\eta^{\mathrm{I}}_{X,-}
.

(2) Let
Y\in\mathscr{C}
be any object. It is enough to show that

\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}(X,Y)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(W,Y)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(W,Y)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}(J_{W},Q_{Y})}
\scriptstyle{(\xi_{\delta})_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{W}^{\sharp}\circ\omega_{Y\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\omega_{Y\sharp}\circ\iota_{W}^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

is commutative, namely that the equation

(7.7)

\iota_{W}^{\sharp}\circ\omega_{Y\sharp}\circ(\xi_{\delta})_{Y}=0

holds. Indeed if this is shown, we obtain a unique morphism
(\delta^{\sharp})_{Y}
which makes

\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}(X,Y)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(W,Y)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(W,Y)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(W,Y)}
\scriptstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{(\delta^{\sharp})_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{(\xi_{\delta})_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\wp^{\mathrm{I}\!\mathrm{I}}_{W,Y}}
\scriptstyle{\wp^{\mathrm{I}}_{W,Y}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

commutative, which is easily shown to be natural in
Y
, by using the universality of pullbacks.

It remains to show (
7.7
). Since
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
is hereditary,
J_{X}
is injective and we have
\mathbb{E}(J_{W},J_{X})=0
. In particular
w_{\ast}\iota_{W}=0
holds for
w\in\mathscr{C}(W,J_{X})
.
Thus (
7.7
) follows from the commutativity of the following diagram.

\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}(X,Y)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(J_{X},Y)}
\textstyle{\mathscr{C}(J_{X},Q_{Y})}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(W,Y)}
\textstyle{\mathscr{C}(W,Q_{Y})}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}(J_{W},Q_{Y})}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{\mathrm{I}}_{X,Y}}
\scriptstyle{(\xi_{\delta})_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{\omega_{Y\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{w^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{w^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\omega_{Y\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{(w_{\ast}\iota_{W})^{\sharp}=0}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{W}^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

∎

Remark 7.20
.

In
Lemma

7.19
, in particular if
\delta=\iota_{X}
, then we have
\xi_{\delta}=\eta^{\mathrm{I}}_{X,-}
since we can choose
w=\mathrm{id}
, and

\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}(X,-)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(J_{X},-)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(J_{X},-)}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{X}^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{\mathrm{I}}_{X,-}}
\scriptstyle{\wp^{\mathrm{I}}_{J_{X},-}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

becomes commutative.

Definition 7.21
.

For any extension
\delta\in\mathbb{E}(C,A)
, we define as follows.

(1)

Define
\delta^{\sharp}\colon\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(A,-)\to\mathscr{C}(C,-)
to be the composition of
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(A,-)\overset{\wp^{\mathrm{I}\!\mathrm{I}}_{A,-}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(A,-)\overset{\delta^{\sharp}}{\longrightarrow}\mathscr{C}(C,-)
.

(2)

Define
\delta_{\sharp}\colon\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(-,C)\to\mathscr{C}(-,A)
to be the composition of

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(-,C)\overset{\wp^{\mathrm{I}}_{-,C}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(-,C)\overset{\delta_{\sharp}}{\longrightarrow}\mathscr{C}(-,A)
.

(3)

Define
\delta^{\sharp}\colon\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}(A,-)\to\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(C,-)
to be the unique morphism which makes

\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}(A,-)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(C,-)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(C,-)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(J_{A},-)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(C,-)}
\scriptstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{\mathrm{I}}_{A,-}}
\scriptstyle{\delta^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{w^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\wp^{\mathrm{I}\!\mathrm{I}}_{C,-}}
\scriptstyle{\wp^{\mathrm{I}}_{C,-}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

commutative for any
w\in\mathscr{C}(C,J_{A})
such that
w^{\ast}\iota_{A}=\delta
, by using
Lemma

7.19
.

(4)

Define
\delta_{\sharp}\colon\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}(-,C)\to\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(-,A)
to be the unique morphism which makes

\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}(-,C)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(-,A)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(-,A)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(-,Q_{C})}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(-,A)}
\scriptstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{\mathrm{I}\!\mathrm{I}}_{-,C}}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{t_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\wp^{\mathrm{I}}_{-,A}}
\scriptstyle{\wp^{\mathrm{I}\!\mathrm{I}}_{-,A}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

commutative for any
t\in\mathscr{C}(Q_{C},A)
such that
t_{\ast}\omega_{C}=\delta
, by using the dual of
Lemma

7.19
.

Proposition 7.22
.

The following forms a bivariant connected sequence of functors
(\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp},\delta^{\sharp})
.

•

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{n}~=~\mathbb{E}^{n}
with the canonical connecting morphisms for
n\geq 0
.

•

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}
is the one obtained in
Proposition

7.17
.
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}=\mathbb{E}^{-2}
. Also, we put
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-n}=0
for
n>2
.

•

For any
\delta\in\mathbb{E}(C,A)
, those
\delta^{\sharp},\delta_{\sharp}
on
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}
and
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}
are those in
Definition

7.21
.

Proof.

It remains to show the naturality of each of

(i)

\delta^{\sharp}\colon\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(A,-)\to\mathscr{C}(C,-)
,
\delta^{\sharp}\colon\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}(A,-)\to\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(C,-)
, and

(ii)

\delta_{\sharp}\colon\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(-,C)\to\mathscr{C}(-,A)
,
\delta_{\sharp}\colon\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}(-,C)\to\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(-,A)

with respect to morphisms of
\mathbb{E}
-extensions.
By duality, it is enough to show (i).
Naturality of
\delta^{\sharp}\colon\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(A,-)\to\mathscr{C}(C,-)
follows from the fact that
(\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{\bullet},\delta^{\sharp})
is a contravariant
\delta
-functor and the naturality of
\wp^{\mathrm{I}\!\mathrm{I}}\colon\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}\to\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}
.

Let us show the naturality of
\delta^{\sharp}\colon\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}(A,-)\to\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(C,-)
. Let
\delta\in\mathbb{E}(C,A),\delta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{E}(C^{\prime},A^{\prime})
be any pair of extensions, and let
(a,c)\colon\delta\to\delta^{\prime}
be any morphism. Take
s\in\mathscr{C}(C,J_{A}),s^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}(C^{\prime},J_{A^{\prime}}),u\in\mathscr{C}(J_{A},J_{A^{\prime}})
such that

s^{\ast}\iota_{A}=\delta,\ \ s^{\prime\ast}\iota_{A^{\prime}}=\delta^{\prime},\ \ a_{\ast}\iota_{A}=u^{\ast}\iota_{A^{\prime}}

arbitrarily.
Then for
a_{\ast}\delta=c^{\ast}\delta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{E}(C,A^{\prime})
, we have
(s^{\prime}\circ c)^{\ast}\iota_{A^{\prime}}=(u\circ s)^{\ast}\iota_{A^{\prime}}=a_{\ast}\delta
. Thus by
Lemma

7.19
(1), compositions of

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}(A^{\prime},-)\overset{\eta^{\mathrm{I}}_{A^{\prime},-}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(J_{A^{\prime}},-)\overset{(s^{\prime}\circ c)^{\ast}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(C,-),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}(A^{\prime},-)\overset{\eta^{\mathrm{I}}_{A^{\prime},-}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(J_{A^{\prime}},-)\overset{(u\circ s)^{\ast}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(C,-)

are equal, which we denoted by
\xi_{a_{\ast}\delta}
. Then it follows that either of
\delta^{\sharp}\circ a^{\ast},c^{\ast}\circ\delta^{\prime\sharp}\colon\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}(A^{\prime},-)\to\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(C,-)
makes the following diagram commutative,

\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}(A^{\prime},-)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(C,-)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(C,-)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(C,-)}
\scriptstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{\xi_{a_{\ast}\delta}}
\scriptstyle{\wp^{\mathrm{I}\!\mathrm{I}}_{C,-}}
\scriptstyle{\wp^{\mathrm{I}}_{C,-}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

hence
\delta^{\sharp}\circ a^{\ast}=(a_{\ast}\delta)^{\sharp}=c^{\ast}\circ\delta^{\prime\sharp}
follows from (the uniqueness in)
Lemma

7.19
(2).
In particular the diagram

\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}(A^{\prime},-)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(C^{\prime},-)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}(A,-)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(C,-)}
\scriptstyle{\delta^{\prime\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{a^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{c^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\delta^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

is commutative, which shows the naturality as desired.
∎

Consider the following condition:

(N
+
)

For any
X,Y\in\mathscr{C}
, complex
C_{\mathrm{I}}^{\bullet}
in
7.10
associated with (
7.1
) and (
7.2
)

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(J_{X},Y)\overset{j_{X}^{\ast}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(I_{X},Y)\overset{i_{X}^{\ast}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(X,Y)\overset{\iota_{X}^{\sharp}\circ\omega_{Y\sharp}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}(J_{X},Q_{Y})

is exact at the both middle terms.

Remark 7.23
.

By
7.10
.(ii), condition (N
+
) is equivalent to asking the complex
C_{\mathrm{I}\!\mathrm{I}}^{\bullet}

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(X,Q_{Y})\overset{q_{Y\ast}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(X,P_{Y})\overset{p_{Y\ast}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(X,Y)\overset{\omega_{Y\sharp}\circ\iota_{X}^{\sharp}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}(J_{X},Q_{Y})

to be exact at the both middle terms
for any
X,Y\in\mathscr{C}
. In this sense, condition (N
+
) is self-dual.

Theorem 7.24
.

The following are equivalent.

(1)

(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
satisfies
(N
+
)
.

(2)

\omega_{X}
is
(\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp})
-acyclic for any
X\in\mathscr{C}
, where
\omega_{X}
is the extension chosen in (
7.1
).

(3)

\iota_{X}
is
(\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{\bullet},\delta^{\sharp})
-acyclic for any
X\in\mathscr{C}
, where
\iota_{X}
is the extension chosen in (
7.2
).

(4)

(\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp})
is a covariant
\delta
-functor.

(5)

(\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{\bullet},\delta^{\sharp})
is a contravariant
\delta
-functors.

(6)

(\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp},\delta^{\sharp})
is a bivariant
\delta
-functor.

Moreover, if one of these equivalent conditions is satisfied, then
(\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp},\delta^{\sharp})
satisfies properties (U) and (C).

Proof.

By definition, (6) means that both of (4) and (5) hold.
Equivalence (2)
\Leftrightarrow
(4) follows from
[
GNP21
, Proposition 4.22]
. Dually for (3)
\Leftrightarrow
(5).

Since (1)
\Leftrightarrow
(3) can be shown dually, it suffices to show (1)
\Leftrightarrow
(2).
Let
X,Y\in\mathscr{C}
be any pair of objects. By the definitions so far,

(7.8)

\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}(X,Y)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(J_{X},Y)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(I_{X},Y)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(X,Y)}
\textstyle{\mathscr{C}(J_{X},Y)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(X,Y)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}(X,Y)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(J_{X},Y)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(I_{X},Y)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(X,Y)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}(J_{X},Q_{Y})}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{X}^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{j_{X}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{i_{X}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{X}^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\wp^{\mathrm{I}\!\mathrm{I}}_{X,Y}}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{X}^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\wp^{\mathrm{I}}_{J_{X},Y}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\wp^{\mathrm{I}}_{I_{X},Y}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\wp^{\mathrm{I}}_{X,Y}}
\scriptstyle{\omega_{Y\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{\mathrm{I}}_{X,Y}}
\scriptstyle{j_{X}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{i_{X}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{X}^{\sharp}\circ\omega_{Y\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

is commutative. Remark that the sequence

0\to\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}(X,Y)\overset{\eta^{\mathrm{I}}_{X,Y}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(J_{X},Y)\overset{j_{X}^{\ast}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(I_{X},Y)

is always exact by the definition of
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}(X,Y)
.

In the rightmost square, since
\iota^{\sharp}_{X}\colon\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(X,Y)\to\mathscr{C}(J_{X},Y)
is monomorphic and since

\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(X,Y)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(X,Y)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(X,Y)}
\textstyle{\mathscr{C}(J_{X},Q_{Y})}
\scriptstyle{\wp^{\mathrm{I}\!\mathrm{I}}_{X,Y}}
\scriptstyle{\wp^{\mathrm{I}}_{X,Y}}
\scriptstyle{\omega_{Y\sharp}\circ\iota_{X}^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{X}^{\sharp}\circ\omega_{Y\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

is a pullback by the definition of
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(X,Y)
, we obtain an isomorphism
\kappa
for kernels which makes

(7.9)

\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Ker}(\iota_{X}^{\sharp})}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(X,Y)}
\textstyle{\mathscr{C}(J_{X},Y)}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Ker}(\iota_{X}^{\sharp}\circ\omega_{Y\sharp})}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(X,Y)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}(J_{X},Q_{Y})}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{X}^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\wp^{\mathrm{I}}_{X,Y}}
\scriptstyle{\omega_{Y\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{X}^{\sharp}\circ\omega_{Y\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

commutative. Thus
(\ref{DiagUnivBivProl1})
and
(\ref{DiagUnivBivProl2})
give an isomorphism of sequences

\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}(X,Y)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(J_{X},Y)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(I_{X},Y)}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Ker}(\iota_{X}^{\sharp})}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}(X,Y)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(J_{X},Y)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(I_{X},Y)}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Ker}(\iota_{X}^{\sharp}\circ\omega_{Y\sharp})}
\textstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{X}^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{j_{X}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{i_{X}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\wp^{\mathrm{I}}_{J_{X},Y}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\wp^{\mathrm{I}}_{I_{X},Y}}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\kappa}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{\mathrm{I}}_{X,Y}}
\scriptstyle{j_{X}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{i_{X}^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

in which we denoted the morphisms induced by
i_{X}^{\ast}
to kernels by the same symbols.
Consequently, its lower row is exact if and only if the upper row is exact. This is nothing but (1)
\Leftrightarrow
(2).

Property (C) holds by
Corollary

7.18
.

It remains to show the universality. Let
(F^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp},\delta^{\sharp})
be any bivariant
\delta
-functor having
F^{n}~=~\mathbb{E}^{n}
with the canonical connecting morphisms for
n\geq 0
. By
[
GNP21
, Corollary 5.21, Corollary 5.22]
, there exists a unique morphism

\varphi^{\bullet}\colon(F^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp})\to(\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp})
of covariant
\delta
-functors and a unique morphism
\psi^{\bullet}\colon(F^{\bullet},\delta^{\sharp})\to(\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{\bullet},\delta^{\sharp})
of contravariant
\delta
-functors.

Construction of
\lambda^{(-1)}\colon F^{-1}\to\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}

X,Y\in\mathscr{C}

\scriptstyle{\omega_{Y\sharp}}

\scriptstyle{\iota_{X}^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\omega_{Y\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

implies that of

, again by using the universality of pullbacks.

\scriptstyle{\omega_{Y\sharp}}

\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

implies that of

forms a natural transformation
\lambda^{(-2)}\colon F^{-2}\to\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}
, in a straightforward way.

So far we have obtained
\lambda^{\bullet}\colon F^{\bullet}\to\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{\bullet}
. It remains to show its compatibility with
\delta_{\sharp}
and
\delta^{\sharp}
. By duality, it is enough to show the compatibility with
\delta_{\sharp}
.

Let
\delta\in\mathbb{E}(C,A)
be any element. It is enough to show the commutativity of

(7.10)

\textstyle{F^{-2}(X,C)}
\textstyle{F^{-1}(X,A)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}(X,C)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(X,A)}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lambda^{(-2)}_{X,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lambda^{(-1)}_{X,A}}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

and

\textstyle{F^{-1}(X,C)}
\textstyle{\mathscr{C}(X,A)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(X,C)}
\textstyle{\mathscr{C}(X,A)}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lambda^{(-1)}_{X,C}}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

for any
X\in\mathscr{C}
. By definition, commutativity of the right square of
(\ref{CommD})
follows immediately from that of

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 125.19147pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern-110.15712pt\raise 22.76208pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.82002pt\hbox{$\textstyle{F^{-1}(X,C)}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 64.62866pt\raise 22.76208pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathscr{C}(X,A)}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-111.79254pt\raise-22.76208pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.82002pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(X,C)}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 64.62866pt\raise-22.76208pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathscr{C}(X,A)}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.56044pt\raise 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.56044pt\raise-48.40936pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise
