65.1854pt\raise-30.7648pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-60.03021pt\raise-8.53578pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.70752pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 22.48235pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.70752pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.1249pt\raise-34.14302pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.70752pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}}}}.

Commutativity of the left square of
(\ref{CommD})
follows from the commutativity of

\textstyle{F^{-2}(X,C)}
\textstyle{F^{-1}(X,A)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}(X,C)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(X,A)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(X,A)}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lambda^{(-2)}_{X,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lambda^{(-1)}_{X,A}}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{0}
\scriptstyle{\varphi^{(-1)}_{X,A}}
\scriptstyle{\wp^{\mathrm{I}}_{X,A}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

in which
\varphi^{(-1)}_{X,A}\circ\delta_{\sharp}=0
holds since
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-2}(X,C)=0
, and of

\textstyle{F^{-2}(X,C)}
\textstyle{F^{-1}(X,A)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}(X,C)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(X,A)}
\textstyle{F^{-1}(X,Q_{C})}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(X,Q_{C})}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(X,A)}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\lambda^{(-2)}_{X,C}}
\scriptstyle{\lambda^{(-1)}_{X,A}}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\omega_{C\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\psi^{(-1)}_{X,Q_{C}}}
\scriptstyle{t_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{t_{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\psi^{(-1)}_{X,A}}
\scriptstyle{\wp^{\mathrm{I}\!\mathrm{I}}_{X,A}}
\scriptstyle{\eta^{\mathrm{I}\!\mathrm{I}}_{X,C}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

for any
t\in\mathscr{C}(Q_{C},A)
such that
t_{\ast}\omega_{C}=\delta
, and the universality of pullback.

The above argument shows that
\lambda^{\bullet}\colon(F^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp},\delta^{\sharp})\to(\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp},\delta^{\sharp})
is a morphism of bivariant
\delta
-functors.
Its uniqueness is deduced from the uniqueness of
\varphi^{\bullet}
and
\psi^{\bullet}
, in the usual way.
∎

Remark 7.25
.

Under
7.1
, we have the following (see
[
GNP21
, Condition 5.12]
for the undefined notation, and also
Remark

7.7
).

(1)

(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
satisfies (NI
+
) if and only if it satisfies (NI) and (N
+
).

(2)

Dually,
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
satisfies (NII
+
) if and only if it satisfies (NII) and (N
+
).

In particular, if
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
satisfies both conditions (NI
+
) and (NII
+
), it satisfies condition (N
+
).
Indeed, in this case, we have
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{\bullet}\cong\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\bullet}\cong\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{\bullet}
and
Theorem

7.24
.(6) together with the universality of
(\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp},\delta^{\sharp})
follows from
[
GNP21
, Corollary 5.39]
recalled in Section 7.1.

The converse is not true. A basic example of a category satisfying condition (N
+
), but not having
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{\bullet}\cong\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{\bullet}
is given by the cluster category of type
A_{2}
with the largest relative structure making a given cluster-tilting object projective. See
Example

7.44
,
Example

7.45
for details.

7.4.
Balanced
\delta
-functor in embedded reduced
0
-Auslander categories

Let
\mathscr{D}
be a triangulated category with a rigid, full subcategory
\mathscr{T}
.
Define
\mathscr{C}
to be the full subcategory
\mathscr{T}\ast\Sigma\mathscr{T}
of
\mathscr{D}
.
Endow
\mathscr{D}
with the largest relative extriangulated structure making objects in
\mathscr{T}
projective.
Then
\mathscr{C}
is extension closed in
\mathscr{D}
for this extriangulated structure, and thus inherits an extriangulated structure
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
. The latter is
0
-Auslander, as discussed briefly in
Section

3.3.7
and in detail in
Section

3.3.11
, and has
\mathscr{T}
and
\Sigma\mathscr{T}
as the full subcategories of projectives and of injectives, respectively.

In what follows, the term “triangle” will always refer to triangles in the triangulated category
\mathscr{D}
, while “conflations” will refer to
\mathfrak{s}
-triangles in
\mathscr{C}
.

Remark 7.26
.

By definition, for any
A,C\in\mathscr{C}
, we have
\mathbb{E}(C,A)=[\Sigma\mathscr{T}](C,\Sigma A)\subseteq\mathscr{D}(C,\Sigma A)
.

For any
X\in\mathscr{C}
, there is a triangle
T_{1}^{X}\to T_{0}^{X}\to X\to\Sigma T_{1}^{X}
, with
T_{0}^{X},T_{1}^{X}\in\mathscr{T}
. We fix such a triangle for each
X
.
It defines a projective resolution of the form (
7.1
) of
X
in
\mathscr{T}
. By shifting this triangle in
\mathscr{D}
, we also have a triangle
X\to\Sigma T_{1}^{X}\to\Sigma T_{0}^{X}\to\Sigma X
, defining an injective coresolution of the form (
7.2
) of
X
in
\mathscr{T}
. In other words, in the notation of (
7.1
), (
7.2
), we have

P_{X}=T_{0}^{X},Q_{X}=T_{1}^{X},I_{X}=\Sigma T_{1}^{X},J_{X}=\Sigma T_{0}^{X}.

Lemma 7.27
.

For any
A,C\in\mathscr{C}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(C,A)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}/_{[\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{T}]}(C,\Sigma^{-1}A)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(C,A)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}/_{[\Sigma\mathscr{T}]}(C,\Sigma^{-1}A).

Proof.

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(C,A)
identifies with the kernel of
\mathscr{C}(C,T_{1}^{A})\to\mathscr{C}(C,T_{0}^{A})
.
Applying
\mathscr{D}(C,-)
to the triangle
\Sigma^{-1}T_{0}^{A}\to\Sigma^{-1}A\to T_{1}^{A}\to T_{0}^{A}
gives the exact sequence

\mathscr{D}(C,\Sigma^{-1}T_{0}^{A})\to\mathscr{D}(C,\Sigma^{-1}A)\to\mathscr{C}(C,T_{1}^{A})\to\mathscr{C}(C,T_{0}^{A})

from which we deduce that
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(C,A)
identifies with
the cokernel of the first map.
Since
\mathscr{T}
is rigid, this cokernel is precisely
\mathscr{D}/_{[\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{T}]}(C,\Sigma^{-1}A)
.
Similar computations based on a triangle of the form
\Sigma T_{1}^{A}\to\Sigma T_{0}^{A}\to\Sigma C\to\Sigma^{2}T_{1}^{A}
show that
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(C,A)
identifies with
\mathscr{D}/_{[\Sigma^{2}\mathscr{T}]}(\Sigma C,A)\cong\mathscr{D}/_{[\Sigma\mathscr{T}]}(C,\Sigma^{-1}A)
.
∎

Remark 7.28
.

From
Lemma

7.27
and its proof, it is straightforward to check that for any
A,C\in\mathscr{C}
, the morphisms
\iota_{C}^{\sharp}\circ\omega_{A\sharp}:\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(C,A)\to\mathbb{E}(J_{C},Q_{A})
and
\omega_{A\sharp}\circ\iota_{C}^{\sharp}:\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(C,A)\to\mathbb{E}(J_{C},Q_{A})
factor through the ideal quotients
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{i}}^{-1}(C,\Sigma^{-1}A)\to\mathscr{D}/_{\left\langle[\Sigma\mathscr{T}],[\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{T}]\right\rangle}(C,\Sigma^{-1}A)
for
\mathbf{i}=\mathrm{I},\mathrm{I}\!\mathrm{I}
.
Indeed,

\omega_{A\sharp}:\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-1}(C,A)\to\mathscr{C}(C,Q_{A})
is isomorphic to
\mathscr{D}/_{[\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{T}]}(C,\Sigma^{-1}A)\hookrightarrow\mathscr{D}/_{[\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{T}]}(C,T_{1}^{A})
(recall that
\mathscr{T}
is rigid, hence so is
\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{T}
), and the image of
\iota_{C}^{\sharp}:\mathscr{D}(C,T_{1}^{A})\to\mathscr{D}(T_{0}^{C},T_{1}^{A})
is the cokernel of
\mathscr{D}(\Sigma T_{1}^{C},T_{1}^{A})\overset{i_{C}^{\ast}}{\longrightarrow}\mathscr{D}(C,T_{1}^{A}),
hence
is isomorphic to

\mathscr{D}/_{[\Sigma\mathscr{T}]}(C,T_{1}^{A})=\mathscr{D}/_{\left\langle[\Sigma\mathscr{T}],[\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{T}]\right\rangle}(C,T_{1}^{A}).

Then
\iota_{X}^{\sharp}\circ\omega_{Y\sharp}
factors as

\mathscr{D}/_{[\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{T}]}(C,\Sigma^{-1}A)\twoheadrightarrow\mathscr{D}/_{\left\langle[\Sigma\mathscr{T}],[\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{T}]\right\rangle}(C,\Sigma^{-1}A)\to\mathscr{D}/_{\left\langle[\Sigma\mathscr{T}],[\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{T}]\right\rangle}(C,T_{1}^{A})\hookrightarrow\mathscr{D}(T_{0}^{C},T_{1}^{A}),

where the first morphism is the cokernel of

\mathscr{D}/_{[\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{T}]}(\Sigma T_{1}^{C},\Sigma^{-1}A)\overset{i^{\ast}}{\longrightarrow}\mathscr{D}/_{[\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{T}]}(C,\Sigma^{-1}A),

and the second morphism is the induced morphism of cokernels. A similar factorisation of
\omega_{A\sharp}\circ\iota_{C}^{\sharp}
can be written via dual arguments.
The pullback diagram in
Definition

7.15
then gives

(7.11)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(C,A)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}/_{([\Sigma\mathscr{T}]\cap[\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{T}])}(C,\Sigma^{-1}A).

Note that the ideal
([\Sigma\mathscr{T}]\cap[\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{T}])(-,-)
we quotient out is formed by the morphisms that admit a factorisation through an object in
\Sigma\mathscr{T}
and a possibly different factorisation through an object in
\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{T}
. This ideal can be strictly larger than the ideal generated by the morphisms which admit a factorisation through an object in
\Sigma\mathscr{T}
and an object in
\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{T}
, in some order. It can also be strictly larger than the ideal of the morphisms factoring through an object in the intersection
\Sigma\mathscr{T}\cap\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{T}
of subcategories in
\mathscr{D}
.

Lemma 7.29
.

\mathbb{E}^{-2}(C,A)=\mathscr{D}/_{[\Sigma^{-2}\mathscr{T}]}(T_{0}^{C},\Sigma^{-2}A)\times_{\mathscr{D}(T_{0}^{C},\Sigma^{-1}T_{1}^{A})}\mathscr{D}/_{[\Sigma\mathscr{T}]}(C,\Sigma^{-1}T_{1}^{A}).

Proof.

This follows directly from
Remark

7.13
. Indeed, in the notation of that remark, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(J_{C},A)=\mathscr{D}/_{[\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{T}]}(\Sigma T_{0}^{C},\Sigma^{-1}A)=\mathscr{D}/_{[\Sigma^{-2}\mathscr{T}]}(T_{0}^{C},\Sigma^{-2}A);

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}(J_{C},Q_{A})=\mathscr{C}(\Sigma T_{0}^{C},T_{1}^{A})=\mathscr{D}(T_{0}^{C},\Sigma^{-1}T_{1}^{A});

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(C,Q_{A})=\mathscr{D}/_{[\Sigma\mathscr{T}]}(C,\Sigma^{-1}T_{1}^{A}).

∎

Remark 7.30
.

An element in
\mathscr{D}(T_{0}^{C},\Sigma^{-2}A)\times_{\mathscr{D}(T_{0}^{C},\Sigma^{-1}T_{1}^{A})}\mathscr{D}(C,\Sigma^{-1}T_{1}^{A})
is a commutative square

\textstyle{(\ast)}
\textstyle{T_{0}^{C}}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{\Sigma T_{1}^{C}}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-2}T_{0}^{A}}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-2}A}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-1}T_{1}^{A}.}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

Because
\mathscr{T}
is rigid, the morphism
f
belongs to the ideal
[\Sigma^{-2}\mathscr{T}]
if and only if it factors through the morphism
\Sigma^{-2}T_{0}^{A}\to\Sigma^{-2}A
.
Similarly,
g
belongs to
[\Sigma\mathscr{T}]
if and only if it factors through
C\to\Sigma T_{1}^{C}
.
This shows that commutativity of the square only depends on the classes of
f
modulo
[\Sigma^{-2}\mathscr{T}]
and of
g
modulo
[\Sigma\mathscr{T}]
.
We also have:

\displaystyle g\in[\Sigma\mathscr{T}]

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle T_{0}^{C}\to C\xrightarrow{g}\Sigma^{-1}T_{1}^{A}\text{ vanishes}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle T_{A}\xrightarrow{f}\Sigma^{-2}A\to\Sigma^{-1}T_{1}^{A}\text{ vanishes}

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle f\in[\Sigma^{-2}\mathscr{T}].

Hence
\mathbb{E}^{-2}
is the kernel of the canonical map

\mathscr{D}(T_{0}^{C},\Sigma^{-2}A)\times_{\mathscr{D}(T_{0}^{C},\Sigma^{-2}\Sigma T_{1}^{A})}\mathscr{D}(C,\Sigma^{-2}\Sigma T_{1}^{A})\longrightarrow\mathscr{D}(T_{0}^{C},\Sigma^{-2}\Sigma T_{1}^{A}).

Definition 7.31
.

We define

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-1}(C,A)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}/_{\left[\Sigma\mathscr{T}\!\to\!\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{T}\right]}(C,\Sigma^{-1}A).

Lemma 7.32
.

(a)

For any
A,C\in\mathscr{C}
and any
T\in\mathscr{T}
, we have
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-1}(C,T)=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(C,T)
and
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-1}(\Sigma T,A)=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(\Sigma T,A)
.

(b)

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-1}(C,A)
is bifunctorial.

Proof.

(a) For any
T\in\mathscr{T}
and any
C\in\mathscr{C}
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-1}(C,T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}/_{\left[\Sigma\mathscr{T}\to\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{T}\right]}(C,\Sigma^{-1}T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}/_{[\Sigma\mathscr{T}]}(C,\Sigma^{-1}T)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(C,T).

The second equality is obtained similarly.

(b) The bifunctoriality of
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-1}
is inherited from that of
\mathscr{D}(-,-)
.

∎

Definition 7.33
.

Set

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{n}=\mathbb{E}^{n},n\geq 0;\qquad\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-2}:=\mathbb{E}^{-2};\qquad\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-m}:=0,m\geq 3.

We complete
(\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{\bullet})
to a bivariant connected sequence of functors as follows.

Consider a conflation
X\overset{i}{\to}Y\overset{p}{\to}Z\overset{\delta}{\dashrightarrow}
in
\mathscr{C}
. By definition of
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
, there is a triangle in
\mathscr{D}
of the form:
X\xrightarrow{i}Y\xrightarrow{p}Z\xrightarrow{\delta}\Sigma X
, where
\delta\in[\Sigma\mathscr{T}]
(see also
Remark

7.26
).
Moreover, the projective resolutions of
X
and
Z
of the form (
7.1
) induce a projective resolution

T_{1}^{X}\oplus T_{1}^{Z}\rightarrowtail T_{0}^{X}\oplus T_{0}^{Z}\twoheadrightarrow Y\dashrightarrow
of
Y
.

We define the connecting morphisms:

•

The map
\delta^{\sharp}:\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-1}(X,U)=\mathscr{D}/_{\left[\Sigma^{2}\mathscr{T}\to\mathscr{T}\right]}(\Sigma X,U)\to\mathscr{C}(Z,U)
is induced by precomposing with
\delta
. It is well-defined because
\delta\in[\Sigma\mathscr{T}]
and
\mathscr{T}
is rigid.

•

The map
\delta^{\sharp}:\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-2}(X,U)\to\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-1}(Z,U)
is defined as follows.
Recall that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-2}(X,U)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}/_{[\mathscr{T}]}(\Sigma^{2}T_{0}^{X},U)\times_{\mathscr{D}(\Sigma^{2}T_{0}^{X},\Sigma T_{1}^{U})}\mathscr{D}/_{[\Sigma^{3}\mathscr{T}]}(\Sigma^{2}X,\Sigma T_{1}^{U})\text{ and }

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-1}(Z,U)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}/_{\left[\Sigma^{2}\mathscr{T}\to\mathscr{T}\right]}(\Sigma Z,U).

Any element
(f,g)
in
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-2}(X,U)
can be completed to a morphism of triangles
(h,f,g)
below.
Since the morphism
\Sigma\delta
belongs to the ideal
[\Sigma^{2}\mathscr{T}]
, it factors through the morphism
\Sigma^{2}T_{0}^{X}\to\Sigma^{2}X
as in the diagram below.
Define
\delta^{\sharp}((f,g))
to be the composition
f\circ u
.

\textstyle{\Sigma Z}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{2}T_{1}^{X}}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{2}T_{0}^{X}}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{2}X}
\textstyle{T_{0}^{U}}
\textstyle{U}
\textstyle{\Sigma T_{1}^{U}}
\scriptstyle{h}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma\delta}
\scriptstyle{u}
\scriptstyle{\text{lifts }u-v}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

To see that the map is well-defined, note that if
v
is any other choice of a lift of
\Sigma\delta
to
\Sigma^{2}T_{0}^{X}
, then the difference
u-v
factors through

\Sigma^{2}T_{1}^{X}\to\Sigma^{2}T_{0}^{X}
.
This implies that the difference between
f\circ u
and
f\circ v
factors through
h
, and hence belongs to the ideal
[\Sigma^{2}\mathscr{T}\to\mathscr{T}]
.

•

Connecting morphisms
\delta_{\sharp}
in the second argument are defined in the dual way.

Proposition 7.34
.

The connecting morphisms
(\delta_{\sharp},\delta^{\sharp})
defined above are covariant, resp. contravariant natural transformations in each degree, natural with respect to morphisms of
\mathbb{E}
-extensions, and so
(\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp},\delta^{\sharp})
is a bivariant connected sequence of functors.

Proof.

Each morphism of
\mathbb{E}
-extensions is a morphism of triangles in
\mathscr{D}
. We keep the notation from
Definition

7.33
.

•

The map
\delta^{\sharp}:\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-1}(X,U)\to\mathscr{C}(Z,U)
is natural in
U
because it is induced by composition with
\delta
(considered as a morphism in
\mathscr{D}(Z,\Sigma Z)
).

•

To see that the map
\delta^{\sharp}:\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-2}(X,U)\to\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-1}(Z,U)
is also natural in
U
, simply note that if
U\xrightarrow{w}U^{\prime}
is any morphism, then both compositions
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-2}(X,U)\to\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-1}(Z,U)\to\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-1}(Z,U^{\prime})
and
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-2}(X,U)\to\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-2}(Z,U^{\prime})\to\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-1}(Z,U^{\prime})
send the class of
(f,g)
to the class of the composition
wfu
.

•

A morphism from the triangle in
Definition

7.33
realizing
\delta\in\mathbb{E}(Z,X)
to another triangle

X^{\prime}\xrightarrow{i^{\prime}}Y^{\prime}\xrightarrow{j^{\prime}}Z^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\delta^{\prime}}\Sigma X^{\prime}
realizing
\delta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{E}(Z^{\prime},X^{\prime})
is defined by a pair of morphisms
(x,z)\in\mathscr{D}(X,X^{\prime})\times\mathscr{D}(Z,Z^{\prime})
such that
\delta\circ z=\Sigma x\circ\delta^{\prime}
. This commutativity directly induces the commutativity of the diagram

\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-1}(X^{\prime},-)}
\textstyle{\mathscr{C}(Z^{\prime},-)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-1}(X,-)}
\textstyle{\mathscr{C}(Z,-)}
\scriptstyle{\delta^{\prime\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{x^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{z^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\delta^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

This shows the naturality of
\delta^{\sharp}:\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-1}(X,U)\to\mathscr{C}(Z,U)
with respect to morphisms of
\mathbb{E}
-extensions.

•

The naturality of
\delta^{\sharp}:\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-2}(X,U)\to\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-1}(Z,U)
with respect to morphisms of
\mathbb{E}
-extensions amounts to the commutativity of the diagram

(7.12)

\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-2}(X^{\prime},-)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-1}(Z^{\prime},-)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-2}(X,-)}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-1}(Z,-)}
\scriptstyle{\delta^{\prime\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{x^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{z^{\ast}}
\scriptstyle{\delta^{\sharp}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

The functoriality of
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-2}(-,U)=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-2}(-,U)
amounts to the fact that
x^{*}
sends
(f^{\prime},g^{\prime})\in\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-2}(X^{\prime},U)
to
(f^{\prime}\circ\mu,g^{\prime}\circ\Sigma^{2}x)
, for any completion of
\Sigma^{2}x\in\mathscr{D}(\Sigma^{2}X,\Sigma^{2}X^{\prime})
to the diagram of the form

\textstyle{\Sigma^{2}T_{1}^{X}}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{2}T_{0}^{X}}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{2}X}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{2}T_{1}^{X^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{2}T_{0}^{X^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{2}X^{\prime},}
\scriptstyle{p}
\scriptstyle{t}
\scriptstyle{\Sigma^{2}x}
\scriptstyle{p^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

and result gives an element of
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-2}(X,U)
which does not depend on the choice of such a diagram.

In the diagram (
7.12
) we then have:

–

(f^{\prime},g^{\prime})\in\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-2}(X^{\prime},U)
is mapped by
\delta^{\prime\sharp}
to
f^{\prime}\circ u^{\prime}
, for some
u^{\prime}\in\mathscr{D}(\Sigma Z^{\prime},\Sigma^{2}T_{0}^{X^{\prime}})
such that
\Sigma\delta^{\prime}=p^{\prime}\circ u^{\prime}
.

–

By functoriality of
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-1}(-,U)
,
z^{*}
maps
f^{\prime}\circ u^{\prime}
to
f^{\prime}\circ u^{\prime}\circ\Sigma z
.

–

By functoriality of
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-2}(-,U)
,
x^{*}
maps
(f^{\prime},g^{\prime})
to
(f^{\prime}\circ t,g^{\prime}\circ\Sigma^{2}x)
.

–

Finally,
\delta^{\sharp}
maps
(f^{\prime}\circ t,g^{\prime}\circ\Sigma^{2}x)
to
f^{\prime}\circ t\circ u
, for some
u\in\mathscr{D}(\Sigma Z^{\prime},\Sigma^{2}T_{0}^{X^{\prime}})
such that
\Sigma\delta=p\circ u
.

The diagram (
7.12
) is then commutative if and only if for each
(f^{\prime},g^{\prime})\in\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-2}(X^{\prime},U)
, we have

f^{\prime}\circ(t\circ u-u^{\prime}\circ\Sigma z)\in[\Sigma^{2}\mathscr{T}\to\mathscr{T}](\Sigma Z,U)\subseteq\mathscr{D}(\Sigma Z,U).

Note that we have

\displaystyle p^{\prime}\circ(t\circ u-u^{\prime}\circ\Sigma z)=\Sigma^{2}x\circ p\circ u-p^{\prime}\circ u^{\prime}\circ\Sigma z=\Sigma^{x}\circ\delta-\Sigma\delta^{\prime}\circ\Sigma z=0

As in the argument in
Definition

7.33
, this implies that
(\mu\circ u-u^{\prime}\circ\Sigma z)
factors through
\Sigma^{2}T_{1}^{X^{\prime}}\to\Sigma^{2}T_{0}^{X^{\prime}}
. Therefore, the difference
f^{\prime}\circ(t\circ u-u^{\prime}\circ\Sigma z)
factors through
h^{\prime}\in\mathscr{D}(\Sigma^{2}T_{1}^{X^{\prime}},T_{0}^{U})
completing
(f^{\prime},g^{\prime})
to a morphism of triangles, and hence belongs to the ideal
[\Sigma^{2}\mathscr{T}\to\mathscr{T}]
.

∎

Proposition 7.35
.

(\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}},\delta_{\sharp},\delta^{\sharp})
is a bivariant
\delta
-functor satisfying property
\rm{(C)}
.

Proof.

Let
C,A\in\mathscr{C}
. By
Lemma

7.2
, to show that it is a bivariant
\delta
-functor it is enough to prove that for each
X
some
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle of the form (
7.1
) is
(\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}},\delta_{\sharp})
-acyclic and some
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle of the form (
7.2
) is
(\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}},\delta^{\sharp})
-acyclic.

Let
C,A\in\mathscr{C}
.
As usual, fix two triangles
T_{1}^{A}\to T_{0}^{A}\to A\to\Sigma T_{1}^{A}
, and
T_{1}^{C}\to T_{0}^{C}\to C\to\Sigma T_{1}^{C}
with
T_{0}^{A},T_{1}^{A},T_{0}^{C},T_{1}^{C}\in\mathscr{T}
giving
\mathfrak{s}
-triangles of the form (
7.1
) and, up to shift, of the form (
7.2
) for
A
and
C
.
The
(\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}},\delta_{\sharp})
-acyclicity of the dominant
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
T_{1}^{A}\to T_{0}^{A}\to A\dashrightarrow
is equivalent to the exactness of the following sequence:

0

\to

\mathbb{E}^{-2}(C,A)

\to

\mathscr{D}/_{[\Sigma\mathscr{T}]}(C,\Sigma^{-1}T_{1}^{A})

\to

\mathscr{D}/_{[\Sigma\mathscr{T}]}(C,\Sigma^{-1}T_{0}^{A})

\to

\mathscr{D}/_{\left[\Sigma\mathscr{T}\to\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{T}\right]}(C,\Sigma^{-1}A)

\to

\mathscr{C}(C,T_{1}^{A})

\to

\mathscr{C}(C,T_{0}^{A})
.

Exactness at
\mathscr{C}(C,T_{1}^{A})
and
\mathscr{D}/_{\left[\Sigma\mathscr{T}\!\to\!\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{T}\right]}(C,\Sigma^{-1}A)
are immediate from the triangle
T_{1}^{A}\to T_{0}^{A}\to A\to\Sigma T_{1}^{A}
.

Exactness at
\mathscr{D}/_{[\Sigma\mathscr{T}]}(C,\Sigma^{-1}T_{0}^{A})
:
Let
f\in\mathscr{D}(C,\Sigma^{-1}T_{0}^{A})
be such that the composition
C\xrightarrow{f}\Sigma^{-1}T_{0}^{A}\to\Sigma^{-1}A
vanishes in
\mathscr{D}/_{\left[\Sigma\mathscr{T}\!\to\!\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{T}\right]}(C,\Sigma^{-1}A)
.
By definition, there is a commutative diagram, in plain below:

\textstyle{T_{0}^{C}}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{\Sigma T_{1}^{C}}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-1}T}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-1}T_{1}^{A}}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-1}T_{0}^{A}}
\textstyle{\Sigma^{-1}A}
\textstyle{T_{1}^{A}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

Since
\mathscr{T}
is rigid, the morphism
\Sigma^{-1}T\to\Sigma^{-1}A
factors through
\Sigma^{-1}T_{0}^{A}
(dashed, on the right).
The difference between
f
and the composition
C\to\Sigma T_{1}^{C}\to\Sigma^{-1}T\to\Sigma^{-1}T_{0}^{A}
vanishes once post-composed with the morphism
\Sigma^{-1}T_{0}^{A}\to\Sigma^{-1}A
.
Thus, there is a morphism
C\to\Sigma^{-1}T_{1}^{A}
(dashed, on the left) such that
f
is the sum of the two compositions involving the dashed morphisms.
This means precisely that the class of
f
in
\mathscr{D}/_{[\Sigma\mathscr{T}]}(C,\Sigma^{-1}T_{0}^{A})
is in the image of

\mathscr{D}/_{[\Sigma\mathscr{T}]}(C,\Sigma^{-1}T_{1}^{A})\to\mathscr{D}/_{[\Sigma\mathscr{T}]}(C,\Sigma^{-1}T_{0}^{A}).

Exactness at
\mathscr{D}/_{[\Sigma\mathscr{T}]}(C,\Sigma^{-1}T_{1}^{A})
and at
\mathbb{E}^{-2}(C,A)
are immediate consequences of the definition of
\mathbb{E}^{-2}(C,A)
as the kernel of the morphism
\mathscr{D}/_{[\Sigma\mathscr{T}]}(C,\Sigma^{-1}T_{1}^{A})\to\mathscr{D}/_{[\Sigma\mathscr{T}]}(C,\Sigma^{-1}T_{0}^{A})
.

The
(\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}},\delta^{\sharp})
-acyclicity of the codominant
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
C\to\Sigma T_{1}^{C}\to\Sigma T_{0}^{C}\dashrightarrow
is proved dually.

Property (C) is nothing but
Lemma

7.32
combined with the definition of
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-n}
for
n\geq 2
.

∎

7.5.
Comparison and universality

Let
(\mathscr{C}^{\prime},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
be an extriangulated category. Assume that there exists a triangulated category
\mathscr{D}
with a rigid full subcategory
\mathscr{T}
and an equivalence of extriangulated categories
F:\mathscr{C}^{\prime}\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{T}\ast\Sigma\mathscr{T}
, where the extriangulated structure on the category on the right hand side is, as before, induced by the largest relative extriangulated structure on
\mathscr{D}
making objects in
\mathscr{T}
projective. In this situation, we can apply constructions from both of the previous two subsections and define connected sequences of functors
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{\bullet}
and
(F^{\mathrm{op}}\times F)\circ\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{\bullet}
on
\mathscr{C}^{\prime}
, where
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{\bullet}
is defined on
\mathscr{T}\ast\Sigma\mathscr{T}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{D}
as in
Section

7.4
. The second sequence a priori depends on the embedding
\mathscr{T}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{D}
and the equivalence
F
. It is natural to compare these sequences and see if they form (universal balanced)
\delta
-functors. Results in
Section

7.4
imply that the second sequence is always a
\delta
-functor with property (C), while the first sequence is a
\delta
-functor with properties (C) and (U) under condition (N+) on the category
\mathscr{C}.

Proposition 7.36
.

Assume that
R=K
is a field,
\mathscr{C}
is
R
-linear and has finite-dimensional
\mathrm{Hom}
- and
\mathbb{E}
-spaces. Then
\dim\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-i}(-,-)
are independent of the choices of the categories
\mathscr{T}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{D}
and of the equivalence
\mathscr{C}\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathscr{T}\ast\Sigma\mathscr{T}.

Proof.

First note that for any projective
P\in\mathscr{C}^{\prime},
we have
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-i}(-,P)\cong\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-i}(-,P)
for any projective and so does not depend on the embedding or the equivalence. The result then follows by using the long exact sequence in the second argument and the fact that
\mathscr{C}
has projective dimension 1.
∎

Lemma 7.37
.

Assume that there exists a balanced
\delta-
functor
\mathbb{E}_{un}^{\bullet}
having
\mathbb{E}_{un}^{n}~=~\mathbb{E}^{n}
with the canonical connecting morphisms for
n\geq 0
and satisfying conditions
\rm{(U)}
and
\rm{(C)}
.
Then we have
\mathbb{E}_{un}^{\bullet}\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{\bullet}.

Proof.

This follows from
Proposition

7.35
by
Lemma

7.9
.
∎

We also give a more explicit reformulation of the condition (N+) in terms of restrictions of certain sub-bifunctors of
\mathscr{D}
on
\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{C}
and compare the constructions from
Section

7.3
and
Section

7.4
.

Corollary 7.38
.

The following are equivalent:

(i)

Condition
\rm{(N+)}
is satisfied;

(ii)

We have

([\Sigma\mathscr{T}]\cap[\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{T}])(\mathscr{C},\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{C})=[\Sigma\mathscr{T}\to\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{T}](\mathscr{C},\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{C});

(iii)

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{\bullet}
is a
\delta-
functor;

(iv)

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{\bullet}\overset{\sim}{\to}\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{\bullet}
.

Proof.

Conditions (i) and (iii) are equivalent by
Theorem

7.24
. Moreover, by the same theorem, (i) implies that
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{\bullet}=\mathbb{E}_{un}^{\bullet}
is universal and satisfies the conditions of
Lemma

7.37
. Thus, (i) implies (iv). If (iv) holds, then (iii) holds authomatically, since
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{\bullet}
is a
\delta-
functor by
Proposition

7.35
.

Finally, the equivalence (ii)
\Leftrightarrow
(iv) follows from the comparison of the formula (
7.11
) for
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}
with that for
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-1}
in
Definition

7.31
.
Alternatively, using the above paragraph it amounts to the equivalence (i)
\Leftrightarrow
(ii). To show the latter, note that in our settting, the complex
C_{\mathrm{I}}^{\bullet}
is always exact in the second term, so the condition (N+) holds if and only if it is exact in the third term (for each
C,A\in\mathscr{C}
). The latter happens if and only if

[\Sigma\mathscr{T}]/_{[\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{T}]}(\Sigma\mathscr{C},\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{C})=[\Sigma\mathscr{T}]/_{[\Sigma\mathscr{T}\to\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{T}]}(\Sigma\mathscr{C},\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{C}),

which is clearly equivalent to the reformulation in (ii). Dually, the exactness of
C_{\mathrm{I}\!\mathrm{I}}^{\bullet}
in the third term for each
C,A\in\mathscr{C}
amounts to asking that

[\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{T}]/_{[\Sigma\mathscr{T}]}(\Sigma\mathscr{C},\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{C})=[\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{T}]/_{[\Sigma\mathscr{T}\to\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{T}]}(\Sigma\mathscr{C},\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{C}),

which is also equivalent to the reformulation in (ii).
∎

7.6.
Examples

Example 7.39
.

\mathscr{C}
is exact if and only if
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}=0.
This can be immediately deduced from
[
AET21
, Proposition 2.6]
by using the universal properties of
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}
and
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}
. Also, the “only if” part is
[
GNP21
, Proposition 5.4]
and its dual, and the “if” part follows directly from
[
NP19
, Corollary 3.18]
.

Example 7.40
.

In
K^{[-1,0]}(\operatorname{proj}\Lambda),
we have
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-1}
and
\mathbb{E}^{-2}=0.
More precisely,
\mathbb{F}^{\bullet}
with
\mathbb{F}^{-1}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}
and
\mathbb{F}^{-i}=0,
for
i\geq 2,
is the universal bivariant
\delta-
functor among those having
\mathbb{F}^{i}=\mathbb{E}^{i},
for
i\geq 0.
This happens because conditions (NI+) and (NII+) are satisfied in this case (see
[
GNP21
, Example 5.34, Corollary 5.39, Example 5.42]
).

Example 7.41
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a triangulated category with a cluster-tilting subcategory
{\mathcal{T}}
. By
[
KZ08
, Theorem 3.3]
, the quotient category
{\mathcal{A}}=\mathscr{C}/{\mathcal{T}}
is abelian.
3
3

3

Note that in
[
KZ08
]
the authors call
tilting
those subcategories of triangulated categories which are nowadays known as
cluster-tilting
subcategories.
Assume that
{\mathcal{A}}
is Frobenius. By
[
KZ08
, Proposition 4.6]
, we then have
\Sigma{\mathcal{T}}=\Sigma^{-1}{\mathcal{T}}.
Thus, in this case for the relative structure on
\mathscr{C}
making
\mathcal{T}
the full subcategory of projectives, we have
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}
. Then conditions (NI+) and (NII+) are satisfied, and, in particular, (N+) is satisfied. Thus, we have
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-1}
and
\mathbb{E}^{-2}=0.

Example 7.42
.

We have the following important special case of Example
7.41
. Let
(Q,W)
be a Jacobi-finite quiver with potential. Let
\mathcal{T}=\operatorname{add}T
be the standard initial cluster-tilting subcategory in the associated cluster category
\mathscr{C}
. Assume that the Jacobian algebra
J(Q,W)=(\mathrm{End}_{\mathscr{C}}T)^{op}
is self-injective. By
[
KZ08
]
as in the previous example, and also by
[
IO13
, Proposition 3.6]
, we have
\Sigma\mathcal{T}=\Sigma^{-1}\mathcal{T}.
Thus, in this case, for the relative structure on
\mathscr{C}
making
\mathcal{T}
the full subcategory of projectives, we have
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-1}
and
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-2}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-2}=0.

Example 7.43
.

Assume that in the notation of the previous subsection, we have
C=\Sigma^{-1}T_{a}=\Sigma^{-2}A,
for some
T_{a}\in\mathcal{T}.

Then
\mathscr{D}(C,\Sigma^{-1}A)=\mathscr{D}(\Sigma^{-1}T_{a},T_{a})=0.
Therefore,
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-1}(C,A)=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(C,A)=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(C,A)=0.

We also have
T_{0}^{A}=0
and
T_{1}^{A}=T_{a}
. By
Lemma

7.29
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{-2}(C,A)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}/_{(\Sigma^{-2}\mathscr{T})}(T_{0}^{C},\Sigma^{-2}A)\times_{\mathscr{D}(T_{0}^{C},\Sigma^{-1}T_{1}^{A})}\mathscr{D}/_{(\Sigma\mathscr{T})}(C,\Sigma^{-1}T_{1}^{A})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}/_{(\Sigma^{-2}\mathscr{T})}(T_{0}^{C},\Sigma^{-1}T_{a})\times_{\mathscr{D}(T_{0}^{C},\Sigma^{-1}T_{a})}\mathscr{D}/_{(\Sigma\mathscr{T})}(C,C)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}(T_{0}^{C},\Sigma^{-1}T_{a})\times_{\mathscr{D}(T_{0}^{C},\Sigma^{-1}T_{a})}\mathscr{D}/_{(\Sigma\mathscr{T})}(C,C)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathscr{D}/_{(\Sigma\mathscr{T})}(C,C).

If
C\notin\Sigma\mathcal{T},
this contains the identity automorphism of
C
and so is non-zero.

To summarize, in this scenario we have

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(C,A)=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(C,A)=0,
but
\mathbb{E}^{-2}(C,A)\neq 0
. The latter happens because
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(C,\mathcal{T})\neq 0.

For an explicit example of a pair
C,A
with
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(C,A)=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(C,A)=0,
but
\mathbb{E}^{-2}(C,A)\neq 0,
we can consider the cluster category of the quiver
A_{2}.
By the above argument, it is sufficient to find a cluster–tilting subcategory
\mathcal{T}
with an object
T_{a}
such that
\Sigma^{-1}T_{a}\notin\Sigma\mathcal{T}
.

Example 7.44
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be the cluster category of type
A_{2}
, whose Auslander–Reiten quiver is shown below.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{b}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bullet}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{b}}}

The object
T=T_{a}\oplus T_{b}
is cluster–tilting, and we have

A=\Sigma T_{a}
,
C=\Sigma^{-1}T_{b}
.
Endow
\mathscr{C}
with the relative extriangulated structure given by this choice of a cluster tilting object: the
\mathfrak{s}
-triangles are those triangles for which the third morphism factors through
\Sigma\mathscr{T}
, where
\mathscr{T}=\operatorname{add}T
.
Because
\mathscr{C}(C,\Sigma^{-1}A)=0
, we have
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(C,A)=0=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(C,A)
, which shows that
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(C,A)=0
.
We also have
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-1}(C,A)=0
.
However,
\mathbb{E}^{-2}(C,A)\neq 0
, as shown by the following computation.
There are
\mathfrak{s}
-triangles

T_{a}\rightarrowtail T_{b}\twoheadrightarrow C\text{ and }T_{a}\rightarrowtail 0\twoheadrightarrow A.

Thus,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{-2}(C,A)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}/_{(\Sigma^{-2}\mathscr{T})}(T_{b},\Sigma^{-2}A)\times_{\mathscr{C}(T_{,}\Sigma^{-1}T_{a})}\mathscr{C}/_{(\Sigma\mathscr{T})}(C,\Sigma^{-1}T_{a})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}(T_{b},C)\times_{\mathscr{C}(T_{b},C)}\mathscr{C}(C,C)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}(C,C)\neq 0.

Example 7.45
.

For acyclic cluster-tilting objects in (acyclic) cluster categories, if a map factors both through
\Sigma^{-1}\mathcal{T}
and through
\Sigma\mathcal{T},
then it has to factor through
\Sigma\mathcal{T}\to\Sigma^{-1}{\mathcal{T}},
since the Auslander-Reiten quiver is directed. Thus, in this case,
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{emb}}^{-1}
, and the condition (N+) holds. Usually (and as
Example

7.44
shows), we have
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}\neq\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}
, and so
\mathbb{E}^{-2}\neq 0.

We see that the condition (N+) holds both for acyclic and self-injective seeds. These are, in a sense, two extreme cases, since a cluster-tilted algebra is always 1-Gorenstein
[
KR07
]
. It is thus natural to ask if the condition holds for any cluster seed. The answer turns out to be negative, and the counterexample is given by the smallest case which is neither acyclic, nor self-injective:

Example 7.46

(Non-acyclic seed in type
A_{4}
)
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be the cluster category of type
A_{4}
, whose Auslander–Reiten quiver is shown below.
Consider the cluster tilting object
T=T_{a}\oplus T_{b}\oplus T_{c}\oplus T_{d}
whose endomorphism algebra has a Gabriel quiver with a 3-cycle, but is not self-injective.
Endow
\mathscr{C}
with the relative extriangulated structure
\mathbb{E}_{\mathscr{T}}
associated with
\mathscr{T}=\operatorname{add}T
. The irreducible morphism
f:\Sigma^{-1}T_{b}\to\Sigma T_{c}
factors both through
[\Sigma\mathscr{T}]
and
[\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{T}]
, but does not factor through
[\Sigma\mathscr{T}\to\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{T}]
. By
Corollary

7.38
, this means that the condition (N+) is not satisfied for this extriangulated category, and so
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{\bullet}
is not a
\delta
-functor. Let us give a more explicit proof of this by providing an example of an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle which is not
(\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{\bullet},\delta_{\sharp},\delta^{\sharp})
-acyclic.

For the sake of contradiction, assume that condition (N+) holds.
Then from the
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
\Sigma^{-1}T_{b}\rightarrowtail\Sigma T_{c}\oplus\Sigma T_{a}\twoheadrightarrow\Sigma T_{b}\dashrightarrow
, we obtain a long exact sequence

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(\Sigma T_{b},\Sigma^{-1}T_{a})\to\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(\Sigma T_{c}\oplus\Sigma T_{a},\Sigma^{-1}T_{a})\to\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(\Sigma^{-1}T_{b},\Sigma^{-1}T_{a})\to

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}(\Sigma T_{b},\Sigma^{-1}T_{a})\to\mathscr{C}(\Sigma T_{c}\oplus\Sigma T_{a},\Sigma^{-1}T_{a}).

We have

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(\Sigma T_{b},\Sigma^{-1}T_{a})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(\Sigma T_{b},\Sigma^{-1}T_{a})=\mathscr{C}/_{[\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{T}]}(\Sigma T_{b},\Sigma^{-2}T_{a})=\mathscr{C}/_{[\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{T}]}(\Sigma T_{b},\Sigma T_{c})=0,

and

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(\Sigma T_{c}\oplus\Sigma T_{a},\Sigma^{-1}T_{a})=\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(\Sigma T_{c}\oplus\Sigma T_{a},\Sigma^{-1}T_{a})=\mathscr{C}/_{[\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{T}]}(\Sigma T_{c}\oplus\Sigma T_{a},\Sigma T_{c})\cong\mathscr{C}(\Sigma T_{c},\Sigma T_{c})\neq 0.

Therefore, we must have
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(\Sigma^{-1}T_{b},\Sigma^{-1}T_{a})\neq 0
.
But on the other hand, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I}}^{-1}(\Sigma^{-1}T_{b},\Sigma^{-1}T_{a})=\mathscr{C}/_{[\Sigma\mathscr{T}]}(\Sigma^{-1}T_{b},\Sigma T_{c})=0

and

\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{I\!I}}^{-1}(\Sigma^{-1}T_{b},\Sigma^{-1}T_{a})=\mathscr{C}/_{[\Sigma^{-1}\mathscr{T}]}(\Sigma^{-1}T_{b},\Sigma T_{c})=0,

so that the pullback diagram in
Definition

7.15
gives
\mathbb{E}_{\mathrm{PB}}^{-1}(\Sigma^{-1}T_{b},\Sigma^{-1}T_{a})=0
, a contradiction.

T_{a}
T_{b}
\bullet
T_{d}
\Sigma^{-1}T_{b}
\Sigma T_{c}
\Sigma^{-1}T_{a}
\bullet
\bullet
\Sigma T_{a}
T_{c}
\Sigma T_{b}
\bullet
T_{a}
\Sigma T_{d}
T_{b}
\bullet
T_{d}
f

We see that in this example condition (N+) is not satisfied and the pullback construction of
Section

7.3
fails to produce a
\delta
-functor.
This motivates the explicit computations in
Section

7.4
, that do give negative extensions in this example.

Appendix A
Reminder on extriangulated categories

Extriangulated categories, introduced in
[
NP19
]
, axiomatize extension-closed subcategories of triangulated categories and generalize both exact categories and triangulated categories.
An extriangulated category is given by:

•

an additive category
\mathscr{C}
,

•

an additive bifunctor
\mathbb{E}:\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{C}\to\mathrm{Ab}
taking values in the category of abelian groups (and to be thought of as an
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}
-bifunctor),

•

an additive realization
\mathfrak{s}
sending each element
\delta\in\mathbb{E}(Z,X)
to some equivalence class of diagrams
X\to Y\to Z
playing the role of short exact sequences or of triangles.

A few axioms, recalled below, have to be satisfied.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be an additive category, and let
\mathbb{E}:\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{C}\to\mathit{Ab}
be an additive bifunctor.

Remark A.1
.

Let
\delta\in\mathbb{E}(Z,X)
,
f\in\mathscr{C}(X,X^{\prime})
and
h\in\mathscr{C}(Z^{\prime},Z)
. By functoriality there are elements
\mathbb{E}(Z,f)(\delta)\in\mathbb{E}(Z,X^{\prime})
and
\mathbb{E}(h,X)(\delta)\in\mathbb{E}(Z^{\prime},X)
, which are written
f_{\ast}\delta
and
h^{\ast}\delta
for short.
Using those notations, we have, in
\mathbb{E}(Z^{\prime},X^{\prime})

\mathbb{E}(h,f)(\delta)=h^{\ast}f_{\ast}\delta=f_{\ast}h^{\ast}\delta.

Definition A.2
.

A
morphism

(f,h):\delta\to\delta^{\prime}
, where
\delta\in\mathbb{E}(Z,X)
and
\delta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{E}(Z^{\prime},X^{\prime})
, is a pair of morphisms
f\in\mathscr{C}(X,X^{\prime})
and
h\in\mathscr{C}(Z,Z^{\prime})
in
\mathscr{C}
, satisfying
f_{\ast}\delta=h^{\ast}\delta^{\prime}
.

Definition A.3
.

Let
X,Z\in\mathscr{C}
. Two diagrams

X\overset{x}{\longrightarrow}Y\overset{y}{\longrightarrow}Z\ \text{ and }\ X\overset{x^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}Y^{\prime}\overset{y^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}Z

in
\mathscr{C}
are
equivalent
if there exists an isomorphism
g\in\mathscr{C}(Y,Y^{\prime})
such that

\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{Y}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime}}
\textstyle{Z}
\scriptstyle{x}
\scriptstyle{y}
\scriptstyle{x^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{y^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{\cong}

commutes.
The equivalence class of
X\xrightarrow{x}Y\xrightarrow{y}Z
is denoted by
[X\xrightarrow{x}Y\xrightarrow{y}Z]
.

Notation A.4
.

For any
X,Y,Z,A,B,C\in\mathscr{C}
, and any
[X\xrightarrow{x}Y\xrightarrow{y}Z]
,
[A\xrightarrow{a}B\xrightarrow{b}C]
, we let

0=[X\overset{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}1\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right]}{\longrightarrow}X\oplus Y\overset{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}0\;1\end{smallmatrix}\right]}{\longrightarrow}Y]

and

[X\xrightarrow{x}Y\xrightarrow{y}Z]\oplus[A\xrightarrow{a}B\xrightarrow{b}C]=[X\oplus A\overset{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}x\;0\\
0\;a\end{smallmatrix}\right]}{\longrightarrow}Y\oplus B\overset{\left[\begin{smallmatrix}y\;0\\
0\;b\end{smallmatrix}\right]}{\longrightarrow}Z\oplus C].

Definition A.5
.

An
additive realization

\mathfrak{s}
is a correspondence associating, with any
X,Z,
and
\delta\in\mathbb{E}(Z,X)
, an equivalence class
\mathfrak{s}(\delta)=[X\xrightarrow{x}Y\xrightarrow{y}Z]
and satisfying the following three conditions:

(\ast)

Let
\delta\in\mathbb{E}(Z,X)
and
\delta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{E}(Z^{\prime},X^{\prime})
be such that

\mathfrak{s}(\delta)=[X\xrightarrow{x}Y\xrightarrow{y}Z]\text{ and }\mathfrak{s}(\delta^{\prime})=[X^{\prime}\xrightarrow{x^{\prime}}Y^{\prime}\xrightarrow{y^{\prime}}Z^{\prime}].

Then, for any morphism
(f,h):\delta\to\delta^{\prime}
, there exists
g\in\mathscr{C}(Y,Y^{\prime})
making

\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{Y}
\textstyle{Z}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime}}
\textstyle{Z^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{x}
\scriptstyle{y}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{h}
\scriptstyle{x^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{y^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

commute.

The sequence
X\xrightarrow{x}Y\xrightarrow{y}Z

realizes

\delta
if
\mathfrak{s}(\delta)=[X\xrightarrow{x}Y\xrightarrow{y}Z]
.

(1)

For any
X,Z\in\mathscr{C}
, the realization of
0\in\mathbb{E}(Z,X)
is given by
\mathfrak{s}(0)=0
.

(2)

For any
\delta\in\mathbb{E}(Z,X)
and
\delta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{E}(Z^{\prime},X^{\prime})
, the realization of
\delta\oplus\delta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{E}(Z\oplus Z^{\prime},X\oplus X^{\prime})
is given by
\mathfrak{s}(\delta\oplus\delta^{\prime})=\mathfrak{s}(\delta)\oplus\mathfrak{s}(\delta^{\prime})
.

Definition A.6
.

(
[
NP19
, Definition 2.12]
)
A triple
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
is called an
extriangulated category
if the following holds:

(ET1)

\mathbb{E}:\mathscr{C}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathscr{C}\to\mathit{Ab}
is an additive bifunctor;

(ET2)

\mathfrak{s}
is an additive realization of
\mathbb{E}
;

(ET3)

Let
\delta\in\mathbb{E}(Z,X)
and
\delta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{E}(Z^{\prime},X^{\prime})
be respectively realized by
X\xrightarrow{x}Y\xrightarrow{y}Z
and
X^{\prime}\xrightarrow{x^{\prime}}Y^{\prime}\xrightarrow{y^{\prime}}Z^{\prime}
. Then, for any commutative square

\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{Y}
\textstyle{Z}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime}}
\textstyle{Z^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{x}
\scriptstyle{y}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{x^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{y^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

in
\mathscr{C}
, there exists a morphism
(f,h):\delta\to\delta^{\prime}
satisfying
h\circ y=y^{\prime}\circ g
.

(ET3)

op
Dual of (ET3).

(ET4)

Let
\delta\in\mathbb{E}(Z^{\prime},X)
and
\delta^{\prime}\in\mathbb{E}(X^{\prime},Y)
be realized respectively by

X\overset{f}{\longrightarrow}Y\overset{f^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}Z^{\prime}\ \text{ and }\ Y\overset{g}{\longrightarrow}Z\overset{g^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}X^{\prime}.

Then there exist an object
Y^{\prime}\in\mathscr{C}
, a commutative diagram in
\mathscr{C}

\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{Y}
\textstyle{Z^{\prime}}
\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{Z}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime}}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{d}
\scriptstyle{h}
\scriptstyle{h^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{e}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\scriptstyle{\delta^{\prime\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\delta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}_{\ast}\delta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}
\scriptstyle{\circlearrowright}

and
\delta^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{E}(Y^{\prime},X)
realized by
X\overset{h}{\longrightarrow}Z\overset{h^{\prime}}{\longrightarrow}Y^{\prime}
, which satisfy the following compatibilities.

(i)

Z^{\prime}\overset{d}{\longrightarrow}Y^{\prime}\overset{e}{\longrightarrow}X^{\prime}
realizes
f^{\prime}_{\ast}\delta^{\prime}
,

(ii)

d^{\ast}\delta^{\prime\prime}=\delta
,

(iii)

f_{\ast}\delta^{\prime\prime}=e^{\ast}\delta^{\prime}
.

(ET4)

op
Dual of (ET4).

We use the following terminology.

Notation A.7
.

Let
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
be an extriangulated category.

(1)

A sequence
X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z
is called a
conflation
if it realizes some
\delta
in
\mathbb{E}(Z,X)
. In that case the morphism
X\xrightarrow{f}Y
is called an
inflation
, written
X\rightarrowtail Y
, and the morphism
Y\xrightarrow{g}Z
is called a
deflation
, written
Y\twoheadrightarrow Z
.

(2)

For an inflation
X\overset{f}{\rightarrowtail}Y,
the
cone

\operatorname{Cone}(f)
is an object
Z
appearing as the last term in some conflation
X\overset{f}{\rightarrowtail}Y\twoheadrightarrow Z.
It is unique up to isomorphism. Dually, for a deflation
Y\overset{g}{\twoheadrightarrow}Y,
the
cocone
, or the
fiber

\mbox{CoCone}(f)=\operatorname{Fib}(f)
is an object
X
appearing as the first term in some conflation
X\rightarrowtail Y\overset{g}{\twoheadrightarrow}Z.
It is unique up to isomorphism.

(3)

An
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle
is a diagram
X\overset{f}{\rightarrowtail}Y\overset{g}{\twoheadrightarrow}Z\overset{\delta}{\dashrightarrow}
where
X\overset{f}{\rightarrowtail}Y\overset{g}{\twoheadrightarrow}Z
is a conflation realizing
\delta\in\mathbb{E}(Z,X)
.

(4)

A
morphism of
\mathfrak{s}
-triangles
is a commutative diagram

\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{Y}
\textstyle{Z}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}}
\textstyle{Y^{\prime}}
\textstyle{Z^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\scriptstyle{x}
\scriptstyle{y}
\scriptstyle{z}
\scriptstyle{f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\delta^{\prime}}

where
x_{\ast}\delta=z^{\ast}\delta^{\prime}
.

The axioms above imply the following:

Proposition A.8
.

Assume that
(\mathscr{C},\mathbb{E},\mathfrak{s})
is an extriangulated category, and let
X\xrightarrow{f}Y\xrightarrow{g}Z\overset{\delta}{\dashrightarrow}
be an
\mathfrak{s}
-triangle.
Then the following sequences of natural transformations are exact:

\mathscr{C}(Z,-)\overset{-\circ g}{\longrightarrow}\mathscr{C}(Y,-)\overset{-\circ f}{\longrightarrow}\mathscr{C}(X,-)\overset{\delta^{\sharp}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}(Z,-)\overset{g^{\ast}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}(Y,-)\overset{f^{\ast}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}(X,-),

\mathscr{C}(-,X)\overset{f\circ-}{\longrightarrow}\mathscr{C}(-,Y)\overset{g\circ-}{\longrightarrow}\mathscr{C}(-,Z)\overset{\delta_{\sharp}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}(-,X)\overset{f_{\ast}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}(-,Y)\overset{g_{\ast}}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{E}(-,Z),

where
\delta^{\sharp}(x)=x_{\ast}\delta
and
\delta_{\sharp}(y)=y^{\ast}\delta
.

It follows that, in a conflation, the inflation is a weak kernel of the deflation, and the deflation is a weak cokernel of the inflation.

Proposition A.9
.

The following are examples of extriangulated categories (for more details, see
[
NP19
]
):

•

Triangulated categories;

•

Exact categories;

•

Extension-closed full subcategories of triangulated (or extriangulated) categories;

•

Quotients of exact (or extriangulated) categories by ideals generated by projective-injective objects (this was used in
Remark

3.10
);

•

Homotopy categories of exact infinity-categories (see
[
NP20
,
Kle22
]
).

The following shifted version of the octahedron axiom (and its dual) is often used in our proofs.

Lemma A.10
.

Let
X\rightarrowtail Y\twoheadrightarrow Z\dashrightarrow
and
X\rightarrowtail Y^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow Z^{\prime}\dashrightarrow
be two
\mathfrak{s}
-triangles whose inflations have the same domain.
Then there exists a diagram made of morphisms of
\mathfrak{s}
-triangles of the form

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Z^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Z^{\prime}}}

We also make use of
relative extriangulated structures
, as defined in
[
HLN21
, Section 3.2]
.
For any full subcategory
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathscr{C}
there are two associated subfunctors
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{X}}
and
\mathbb{E}^{\mathcal{X}}
of
\mathbb{E}
, defined as follows:

Definition A.11
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{X}}(C,A)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\delta\in\mathbb{E}(C,A)\;|\;g^{\ast}\delta=0\text{ for any $g$ with domain in }\mathcal{X}\}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{X}}(C,A)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{\delta\in\mathbb{E}(C,A)\;|\;f_{\ast}\delta=0\text{ for any $f$ with codomain in }\mathcal{X}\}

Those subfunctors are shown to induce extriangulated substructures in
[
HLN21
, Proposition 3.19]
, where the first (resp. second) is the maximal extriangulated substructure making all objects in
\mathcal{X}
projective (resp. injective).
